PRONUNCIATION
(Page 1)

These notes are not intended to cover the whole of this vast subject, nor must they be made a substitute for listening. You can only learn to pronounce a language by listening to the way the people of the country speak it. But these notes may help you to avoid some of the more obvious pitfalls.

Vowels: Give these "continental" rather than English values. Avoid diphthongs - e.g. O is not pronounced as English O, which is really sounded as O-U. And please remember that the short sound of A as in English "hat" does not occur anywhere in Luyia. Never use it.
There is a front-rounded vowel Ü, like the U in French "rue". It is made with the tongue in position for I and lips in position for U. It occurs in the following (and other) words, but is not very common:
    bira         pass
    sita         move along
    ifiko        kidney
    olusiba      deep water

Note that doubling is used to indicate a lengthening of the vowel sound, not a change in quality; e.g. the EE in AMABEELE is pronounced as the EA in "bear", and not as the EE in "beer".

KH: Velar fricative. This is the sound heard in Scottish "loch" and German "Dach". It is made with the tongue in position as for K, but not quite touching the soft palate, so that breath passes between with friction.
    e.g. KHUKHAABE, OKHUKHOLA.

B: Bilabial fricative. The lips do not meet and explode as in the English B, but breath passes through all the time, so that the sound approximates to V, but made with the two lips instead of the lip and teeth. The nasal compound MB is pronounced explosively, as in English "number".

F: This is probably bilabial also, but it is not as definitely so as the B, and in some cases seems to be definitely labio-dental, i.e. made with the lip and teeth.

H: This is voiced, and not breathed as is more usual in English. In English we would teach a child to pronounce H by expelling the breath forcibly before it begins to make the following vowel sound; but in Luyia the H can hardly be separated from the vowel. Doke says "Voiced H really indicates that the succeeding vowel is pronounced with a vibrant roughening caused by throat friction". The effect on the English ear is often simply that of a very lightly aspirated H.

L and R: There are really three main sounds to be represented by these two symbols:
    (1) Lateral L, like an English L, as in LOLA, LEKHA, OLULIMI.
    (2) Rolled R, which should definitely be rolled, i.e. formed by a succession of taps of the tongue against the teeth-ridge; note that this is not the same as the ordinary English R, which is a fricative - i.e. the tongue does not touch the teeth-ridge, but allows air to pass between with friction; e.g. RUMA, TSITARU. Note also that this Luyia rolled R often corresponds to a Swahili T, and occurs as T in some dialects of Luyia, e.g. in Samia.
    (3) Flapped L, made by curling up the tip of the tongue and flapping it down quickly, the under side of the tongue touching the teeth ridge once; (Page 2) it resembles a D very quickly made. It is represented by L; note that it always follows I or E; e.g. AMABELE, ESHILENJE.

NG': Pronounced as NG in "singer", "ringing"; e.g. NG'ANG'AALA.

NY: Pronounced as NI in "onion"; e.g. NYALA (cf. NIALA).

PALATALIZATION: Very important. Note that a front vowel (I or E) following certain consonants usually changes their pronunciation:
    K  becomes CH; thus RUKA   gives OMURUCHI, ARUCHILE, ARUCHE
    KH becomes SH;  "   TEEKHA   "   OMUTEESHI, ATEESHILE, ATEESHE
    NG becomes NJ;  "   LONGA    "   OMULONJI, ALONJILE, ALONJE
But note that if E results from the coalescence of A-I or A-E, it usually causes no change; thus KA-IFWE gives KEEFWE; KA-(Y)E, KE.
In a similar manner I (but in this case not E) usually changes H to Y; e.g. ABOHA in the past tense is ABOYILE; but the near future is NABOHE.

NASALIZATION: Also very important. N coming before certain consonants causes them to change as follows:
    P, B, and sometimes H, become MB
    T, L, and sometimes R, become ND
    K becomes NG
    TS becomes NZ
    CH becomes NJ
Note that when a syllable beginning with L is followed by one beginning with N or M, L may become N and not ND; e.g. EN-LIMA = ENIMA, not ENDIMA.
The following letters do not nasalize, so that an N coming immediately before one of them is dropped out:
    M   F   W
    N   S   Y
    KH  SH

SYLLABLES: There are no closed syllables in Luyia; that is, every syllable must end in a vowel. Beware of drawing the following consonant back into the vowel, thus altering its quality; e.g. the evening greeting, "Bwilile", should be divided thus: BWI-LI-LE; be careful that the first syllable is not pronounced as "bwir", rhyming with English "beer". When it is necessary to divide a word, as at the end of a line of writing, be careful to put the hyphen after a vowel and before a consonant.

-----oo0oo-----

ORTHOGRAPHY
(Page 2)

The following are the rules at present governing the spelling of Standard Oluluyia. It will be realised that they cannot represent the pronunciation in every dialect area, but are an attempt to provide a written form that will be acceptable to the greatest possible number, for literary purposes.

1. Vowels: Five only - A, E, I, O, U; but they can also be long - AA, EE, II, OO, UU.
(Page 3)
Note the following:
(a) i. Words in which the front-rounded vowel Ü occurs, or where there is an optional permutation of I and U, should be spelt with I; e.g. TIYA = bump; BIRA = pass; ISIMILISIA = be patient.
    ii. Where I and U (or W) come together, there is a tendency for the first of these to be assimilated to the second in pronunciation; this should not be indicated in orthography: e.g. AFWILE is often heard as AFIILE; OKHUFWIMBA, as OKHUFII-MBA; ELIUBA, as ELUUBA; EMIUYA, as EMUUYA. Occasionally in the I-U sequence, the assimilation is retrogressive, giving pronunciations ELIIBA and EMIIYA.

(b) Vowel length:
    i. Where there is an apparent lengthening, as in the underlined vowels in the examples given, caused by a preceding semi-vowel compound (e.g. OMWAMI, ESHILEYO), or a following nasal compound (e.g. LONDA, AMABANGA), or the vowel of a vowel stem (OKHWITSA, OKHWAMA), a single vowel should be written.
    Note that a preceding H, and a following Y, (as in OKHUHUTSA and OKHUBAYA respectively), and possibly some other letters, also frequently have the same effect, though not regularly; e.g. there are separate words HELA and HEELA, KHAYWA and KHAAYWA; but an apparently long vowel in these positions needs careful consideration, and when there is doubt I would suggest writing it singly.
    ii. Where there is a definite long vowel sound, either semantic or grammatical, it should be indicated by doubling. This will include the following:
        a. A long vowel in the stem of a word; e.g. AMABEELE (cf. AMABELE); OKHUKAANA (cf. OKHUKANA).
        b. Where the vowel of a noun class prefix is the same as the initial vowel of the stem following, both are written; e.g. ESHI-ILIMA = ESHIILIMA; OLU-UMBE = OLUUMBE.
        c. Where the vowel of a subjectival or objectival concord or of a tense infix is the same as the initial vowel of the stem immediately following, both are written; e.g. BA-AHULA = BAAHULA; KHU-BA-AHULA = KHUBAAHULA; A-SHI-ITSANGA = ASHIITSANGA.
        d. An A in any prefix or infix coalesces with the initial vowel of the stem to form long vowels as follows:
            A - E = EE     ABA-EKA = ABEEKA;  BA-KHA-EYA = BAKHEEYA
            A - I = EE     KHU-A-INJILA = KHWEENJILA;  BA-BA-IRA = BABEERA
            A - O = OO     BA-ORA = BOORA;  A-LA-OSIA = ALOOSIA
            A - U = OO     YA-ULA = YOOLA;  A-LA-USIA = ALOOSIA
(Page 4)
        e. In a polysyllabic verb, the vowel of the penultimate syllable is lengthened, and therefore doubled, in forming the near and intermediate past tenses - unless it is already long; e.g. ABUKUULE; ASINYIISHE; but AHULUUSHILE.
        f. In combined forms with IFWE and INYWE the resulting vowel is doubled; e.g. WA-IFWE = WEEFWE; NINA-INYWE = NINEENYWE; SE-IFWE = SEEFWE; KHULWA-INYWE = KHULWEENYWE.
        g. When a possessive particle is joined to a demonstrative beginning with a vowel, the A of the poss.part. does not seem to be dropped as in iii a. following; e.g. OMWANA WAABO = the child of those (people); cf. OMWANA WABO = their child, or his/her brother. Note that this does not often occur.

    iii. Note that in the following cases the letter A is dropped, and the following vowel is written singly:
        a. Possessive particle (except as in ii g. above); e.g. OMWANA WOMUKHASI; OBUKUSI BWAMABEELE; ESHISINA SHIELIKHUWA.
        b. NA conjunction; e.g. OMUKHASI NOMWANAWE; TSING'OMBE NETSIMBUSI.
        c. NE conjunction; e.g. BALIA EBILIIBWA, NOLUNYUMAKHWO... But note that this is sometimes separated from the following word, usually when the sense is "but" rather than "and"; e.g. YATSIA, NE ABEETSABE SHIBALI NIBALIYO TA.
        d. NA prefix of the Near Future before a vowel concord; e.g. NA-EKHOLE = NEKHOLE; NA-OTSIE = NOTSIE.
        e. The I in the NI copula is similarly dropped; e.g. NI-ISIE = NISIE; NI-OMULAYI = NOMULAYI.

    NOTE: In INDEXING a double vowel should immediately follow its single form; e.g. BOOLA would follow BOLA and precede BOMBA.

2. Semi-vowels: W and Y.
(a) W. There is often a tendency to insert U before W in a final syllable. This need not be represented in writing. Note, however, that U does actually precede the W in LIKHUWA, which in some dialects is pronounced LIKHUBA. See also note (a) ii. on p.3.
(b) Y. This symbol should be used only in the following positions:
    i. After N to indicate the palatal nasal NY; e.g. MANYA = know.
    ii. Initially in word stem; e.g. YUMBA = be overgrown; and in the pron.conc. YI-, e.g. INZU YIKWA = the house falls.
    iii. Intervocalically; e.g. OBWOYA = fur, hair; IYAKA = scratch oneself; OKHWEYA = to sweep.
    (Page 5) In all other cases, where Y would follow a consonant, the vowel I should be used; this includes noun prefixes and causative forms; e.g. ESHIALO = country; HONIA = save.
    The permutation of Y and H will be dealt with in section 9.

3. Bilabial Consonants: P, B, MB, M.
(a) Plosive P and B: P
    e.g. AMAPWONI = potatoes; PAPA = father
(b) Fricative B: B
    e.g. ABANDU = people; EBINDU = things; BISA = hide.
(c) Nasal Compound: MB
    e.g. OMULEMBE = peace; IMBWA = dog
(d) Nasal M: M
    e.g. MAMA = mother; AMATUMA = maize

4. Labio-dental (or Bilabial) Fricative F: F
    e.g. FWANA = resemble
Notes:
    i. The prefix to plural nouns in the ESHI- class, which is pronounced in some dialects as EFI-, should be spelt EBI-.
    ii. F may occur without following W, but this is not frequent, except in the case given in note i.

5. Dental and Alveolar: T, TS, N, L, R, S, ND, NZ
(a) Plosive T and D: T
    e.g. TAHA = draw; AMATUMA = maize
(b) Affricative TS, DZ, Z: TS
    e.g. AMATSI = water; AMATSUKHU = lungs
(c) Nasal N: N
    e.g. NOHO = or; NA = and; NUUNA = suck
(d) Lateral L: L
    e.g. LOLA = see; LITAALA = village
(e) Rolled R: R
    e.g. RUMA = send; TSITARU = three
    (Rolled R is heard as T in some dialects)
(f) Flapped L: L
    This is always derived from an L, the pronunciation being modified by a preceding I or E; so it should in all cases be represented by L. The following are places (amongst many) where it will be found: -ILE ending of Near and Intermed. Past Tense; -ELE of corresponding tenses in verb "to be"; Applied form, -IL- and -EL-; Persistive, -ILILA and -ELELA; following a prefix with I - LAMA gives ESHILAMA; and in stems of words, as TSIBILI, OKHUCHELA.
(g) Fricative S: S
    e.g. LISISSI = wall; SAMWANA = his father
(h) Nasal compounds: ND, NZ
    ND: INDUBI = basket; ENDI = I am
    NZ: ENZATSAHALA = I am happy; INZOFU = elephant
    (NZ is heard as NJ in some dialects)

6. Palato-Alveolar: CH, SH, NJ
(a) Affricative CH, J: CH
    e.g. CHAMA = be pleased; CHONYA = be tired
(b) Fricative SH: SH
    e.g. SHIAMA = go crooked; LISHELE = frog
(Page 6)
(c) Nasal Compound: NJ
    e.g. ENJAMA = I am pleased; WANJE = my
Note: These sounds occur as the result of palatalization of K, KH, and NG respectively by a following front vowel, though CH also occurs without a palatalising front vowel in words derived from an original palatal K. See section 8.

7. Palatal NY: NY
    e.g. INYAMA = meat; MANYA = know

8. Velar: K, NG', KH, NG
(a) Plosive K or G: K
    e.g. KUKA = grandfather; KONA = sleep
(b) Nasal NG': NG'
    e.g. ING'OMBE = cow; NG'OONA = beautify
Note: There seems to be a recent tendency in some cases to substitute NY for NG', particularly before a front vowel; e.g. INYININYINI (=star) is now more frequently heard than ING'INING'INI; and the Near Past of TONG'A is often heard as -TONYILE or TONG'YILE.
(c) Fricative KH: KH
    e.g. OKHUKHOLA = to do
(d) Nasal Compound NG: NG
    e.g. ENGONA = I sleep; INGUBO = cloth
Notes:
    i. In most dialects a following front vowel, I or E, palatalizes K to CH, KH to SH, and NG to NJ. This Palatalization should be shown; e.g. EHUCHILE = I was surprised; ATEESHE = she may cook; SINJILA = stand.
    ii. When E is the result of the coalescence of the A of a prefix or infix and E or I, palatalization does not usually take place; e.g. KA-IFWE = KEEFWE; KA-ITSA = KEETSA; YA-KHA-INJILA = YAKHEENJILA. This may be changing; one sometimes hears e.g., AKEKE pronounced as "akeche".

9. Laryngeal Fricative H: H
    e.g. HAMBA = seize; BOHA = bind
Notes:
    i. The Luyia H is always voiced.
    ii. An I following an H palatalises it to Y; this palatalization should be shown; e.g. ABOYILE = he bound (from BOHA).
    iii. An E following an H does not exert any palatalizing influence, and in some cases the syllables YE and HE distinguish words; e.g. HENGA = look; YENGA = brew beer.

10. Word Division:
Note: Noun Class Prefixes. These should be written in the forms OMU-, EMI-, ESHI-, etc.; i.e. with the vowel of the preprefix.
(a) The verb to be fully conjunctive; i.e. all pronominal, tense, formative and other suffixes to be joined to the verb stem.
    KHULAMUNYOOLAYO         we shall find him there
(Page 7)
Note that the second negative particle, TA, should be written separately, as its place in the sentence is variable:
    shikhulitsiakhwoyo ta       we will not go there at all
    shiyeechesinjia abaana      she doesn't teach little children
      abatiti ta
(b) Auxiliary verbs having their own subjectival concord, including the verb "to be" when used with the infinitive in the sense of "to be about to", to be written separately:
    ali okhwitsa                he is about to come.
(c) Locatives MU, KHU, HA, ENA, to be joined to following noun:
    mumuliango                  in the doorway
    eneesoomelo*                somewhere about the school
    * see note on p.54
(d) Possessive particle to be joined to the following word:
    tsingubo tsiabaana          the children's clothes
    olukuku lweinyanza          the shore of the lake
(e) Locative particle EWA (the locative form of the poss.part.) to be joined to the following noun or pronoun:
    yila ewomusoomesia          take this to the teacher's (place)
    enzia eweefwe               I am going to our (place)
But note that where EWA means "where", "the place at which", it is not joined:
    yila ewa omusoomesia ali    take this where the teacher is
(f) Indirect relative concord, i.e. obj.rel. and poss.rel. (which are also formed by using the poss.part.) are not to be joined to the following word:
    tsingubo etsia bakula       the clothes which they bought
    tsingubo etsia abandu       the clothes which the people bought
      bakula
    omukhasi owa omwanawe       the woman whose child has died
      afwile
(g) Monosyllabic possessives, i.e. 2nd and 3rd pers.sing. in personal class, to be joined to the preceding noun:
    omukhaanawo                 your daughter
    ing'ombeye                  his cow
(h) OLWA and KHULWA to be written separately from the following word, except in contracted forms:
    olwa eshialo bwashia        when it was getting light
    khulwa okhwitsakhwe         for his coming
    but khulwako                because of that
(i) NI copula to be joined to the following word; it may be written separately for emphasis:
    niwina? nomusoomesia        who is it? it is the teacher
    ni omusoomesia toto         it really is the teacher
(j) NA conjunction to be joined to the following word:
    omukhasi nomwanawe          the woman and her child
(Page 8)
(k) NE = BUT to be written separately; it is pronounced separately:
    ne omanye mbu.....          but you should know that.....
(l) When Poss.Part., Locatives, etc., come immediately before Proper nouns, they are joined as before; but the first (remaining) letter of the Proper noun is written as a capital:
    eshitabo shiOluswahili      a Swahili book
    Omuruchi wAbayahudi         the King of the Jews
    yashiyila khuMwami          he took it to the Chief
    yatsia khwIsabo             he went to the Feast
    oyo nOmuyindi               that is an Indian
    omundu weButere             a Butere person
Note that WE in the last example represents coalescence of WA with locative E, = AT.

-----oo0oo-----

NOUNS
(Page 8)

Every Luyia noun consists of two parts, a stem (the really important part of the word) with a prefix before it. One of the main characteristics of Bantu languages is the division of nouns into classes according to their prefixes. There are twelve classes of nouns in Luyia, distinguished by their prefixes - although you won't be introduced to them all immediately; and eight of the twelve have singular and plural forms, again distinguished by prefixes. Further, these prefixes (and others) are used to bring the different words of a sentence into agreement; when they are used in this way they are called concords. A full table will be found at the end of this book, showing the noun classes with their distinctive prefixes and concords, and some of the words formed with the help of these concords. There is no need to learn them all at once; they will be introduced gradually in the following pages.

Class 1: OMU - ABA

Nouns in this class almost always refer to human beings; probably the only exception is when, e.g., a lamb is called a "sheep's child" - OMWANA WELIKONDI; and very few nouns in other classes are used for human beings. In the singular the prefix is OMU-, and in the plural it is ABA-; e.g. OMUNDU = person; ABANDU = people. Before we go any further, write the plurals of the following words, and learn them:

Exercise 1
    omusoliili      a youth                 omukhasi        a married woman
    omukhaana       girl, unmarried woman   omusaatsa       a man
    omusiani        a boy                   omulosi         a witch
    omucheni        a visitor, stranger     omushiele       an old woman

In all of these the stem (NDU, KHAANA, KHASI, etc.) began with a consonant. (Page 9) When the stem begins with a vowel certain changes take place. A "U" coming before a vowel (as in the prefix OMU-) always changes to W, except before another U, when it remains; thus with the stem ANA we get a noun OMWANA and not OMUANA. In the plural the changes are a little more complicated, but you will very quickly get used to them so that they are automatic. When the letter A in a noun prefix immediately precedes an initial A in the noun stem, they both remain; thus the plural of OMWANA (which means a child) is ABA-ANA, i.e. ABAANA; the two A's are not pronounced separately, but the two together form an A sound that is longer than that in ABAKHASI or ABASAATSA. But when A comes immediately before any other vowel the two grow together, or coalesce, to form a new sound.
Thus:
    a - e = ee      aba-eka = abeeka        pupils, learners    (omweka)
    a - i = ee      aba-itsa = abeetsa      friends             (omwitsa)
    a - o = oo      aba-osi = aboosi        washers             (omwosi)
    a - u = oo      aba-upi = aboopi        players, beaters    (omuupi)

Exercise 2
Write the singular and plural forms of the following nouns, of which only the stems and meanings are given:
    -ami            chief                   -ikho           relation
    -ibi            thief                   -eha            bride
    -umbashi        builder                 -ibuli          parent

This system of prefixes is extremely useful. As we shall see, it means that by changing the prefix a new noun can be formed, associated in meaning with the original. Also, that verbs can be formed from nouns, and nouns from verbs, and both from adjectives. There are two main regular methods of forming Class 1 nouns from verbs. In both cases the OMU- prefix is put before the stem of the verb, and the final vowel of the verb is changed -
(a) to I, when the person indicated is the agent, or doer of the action:
    sooma           read            gives   omusoomi        a student
    chenda          travel            "     omuchedi        a traveller
(b) to E, when the person indicated is the one acted upon:
    boha            bind            gives   omubohe         a captive
    ruma            send              "     omurume         a messenger

Exercise 3
Give singular and plural forms of nouns formed as follows:
    from stem aya = to herd                 meaning a herdboy, shepherd
      "    "  khwa = to pay dowry              "    bridegroom, one who pays dowry
      "    "   "           "                   "    one who receives dowry
      "    "  khola = work, do                 "    worker
      "    "  baatsa = work in wood            "    carpenter
      "    "  ahula = separate, elect          "    one elected, set aside
      "    "  laama = bequeath                 "    heir
      "    "  lima = cultivate                 "    farmer
      "    "  ibula = give birth               "    native, one born in the country

-----oo0oo-----

Class 2: OMU - EMI
(Page 10)

This class includes the names of most trees, the fruit being put into the LI- class; it also includes a number of parts of the body, and many other words outside these classifications; e.g. OMUSAALA = a tree; EMISAALA = trees. Before a vowel stem the U behaves exactly as it does in Class 1, but in the plural the I does not change; so the plural of OMWALO = a river, is EMIALO. In Class 1 we had OMWIKHO = a relation, with plural ABEEKHO; in Class 2 a word similarly spelt (but with a difference of tone) is OMWIKHO = paddle, cooking spoon; and the plural is EMIIKHO.

Exercise 4
Write the plurals of the following words:
    omuhembe        a mango tree            omubili         body
    omuchungwa      an orange tree          omurwe          a head
    omwesi          month, moon             omwiro          feather, plume

And the singulars of the following:
    emikongo        backs                   emikoye         cords, ropes, strings
    emikanda        crowds                  emioyo          hearts, voices
    emiikhoyo       rejoicings, celebrations  emilambo      corpses

In this class too nouns can be formed from verbs - usually by changing the final vowel of the verb to O; the resulting noun indicates that which results from the performance of the action; thus BAYA = play gives OMUBAYO = a game; and LAAMA = pray gives OMULAAMO = a church service.

     "    sila = renounce, tabu                "    a tabu, thing strictly forbidden

     "    kaba = divide, share                 "    a share, portion allotted
     "    langa = call                         "    a calling

-----oo0oo-----

Class 3: (E)LI - AMA
(Page 10)

You will have noticed that each of the prefixes we have considered so far consists of two syllables, the first one being a vowel and the second consonant-vowel. That initial vowel is known as the pre-prefix; there used, very many generations ago, to be another consonant in front of it, but this has been dropped by most of the Abaluyia; but you will still hear the full pre-prefix in Olubukusu, the Luyia dialect spoken near Mount Elgon. They say BABANDU for people, and KUMURONGORO for a tree. (Page 11) In many Bantu languages the whole of the pre-prefix has been dropped, leaving MU, BA, and MI for these prefixes; and among the Abaluyia you will find a tendency to drop it at times. In the class we are just going to consider it is almost always dropped in the singular, except before a vowel stem; e.g. a stone is LICHINA (plur. AMACHINA), but a boat is ELIARO (plur. AMAARO).
Most fruits are in this class; we have had OMUHEMBE = a mango tree; a mango is LIHEMBE, and mangoes are AMAHEMBE.
There are several very interesting things about this class. One is that sometimes words are put into it to indicate a mild annoyance or contempt; e.g. LIRWE instead of OMURWE for head, when you are annoyed by a persistent headache; LISAATSA instead of OMUSAATSA for a man, when you don't think much of him. It is not advisable to do this yourself until you are very sure of the particular word and form you want to use; you might find yourself saying something that is definitely offensive. It has been suggested that all the words in this class originally had a ceremonial significance; that is probably an exaggeration, but a glance down the list of words in the vocabulary will show that it is true of a large number of words, and probably investigation along these lines would give interesting results.
Parts of the body which appear in pairs sometimes take an alternative plural in AMA-, usually when they are regarded as parts with a specific work; e.g. ESHIRWI = ear, and EBIRWI or AMARWI, ears; OMUKHONO = hand, arm; EMIKHONO or AMAKHONO, hands.
Then there are two special uses of the AMA- plural prefix that you will meet:
(i) Prefix of Quantitative Plural, to indicate great numbers, masses, or sets; e.g. OMWANA = child; ABAANA = children; AMAANA = larvae, masses of children.
(ii) Prefix of Pluralia Tantum - words found in plural form only; these usually refer to liquids; e.g. AMATSI = water; AMABEELE = milk; AMAFURA = oil; AMABANGA = blood.

Nouns in this class may be formed from verbs in two ways:
(a) From the simple stem with the final vowel changed to O; this gives a word usually meaning "that which is.....".
    boha            tie up          gives   liboho          something tied up, bundle
    sooma           read              "     lisoomo         portion to be read.
(b) From the applied form of the verb; but as we won't be dealing with it for the present we will merely note the fact in passing. They usually indicate a place where an action is performed habitually; e.g. SOOMA = read; SOOMELA = read at, in, to; LISOOMELO = school (place where they read); and again TEEKHA = cook; TEESHELA = cook at, in, for; LITEESHELO = kitchen.

Before a vowel stem the A and I behave as they did in the other classes.

-----oo0oo-----

ADJECTIVES
(Page 12)

With, I think, only one exception (BULI = every) the Adjective in Oluluyia must be brought into agreement with the noun by means of the Adjectival Concord; but as this is the same as the Class Prefix of the noun this is quite easy. (There is sometimes a slight difference in Class 5, which will be dealt with when we come to that class). Thus the adjective stem -LAYI = good, with OMUNDU is OMULAYI; with ABANDU, ABALAYI; with LICHINA, LILAYI; etc. Note too that the adjective follows the noun, again BULI = every being the only regular exception; so we get OMUNDU OMULAYI = a good person; ABANDU ABALAYI = good people; LICHINA LILAYI = a good stone.
There are not many adjective stems in Luyia, but there are often other ways of conveying an idea that in English we would convey by the use of an adjective. Here are some of the most common stems:
    -layi           good, pleasing, clean, beautiful, etc.
    -bii            bad, displeasing, dirty, ugly, etc.
    -lafu           light in colour, clean, white
    -mali           dark in colour, dirty, black
    -titi (-tutu)   little, small, few
    -khongo         big, large, (but not many), great
    -rambi          long, tall, and sometimes deep
    -imbichiti      short (remember what happens to the vowel of the prefix before a vowel stem)
    -tinyu          hard, difficult
    -angu           quick, easy, light

There is no Article, either definite or indefinite, in Luyia - i.e. nothing directly corresponding to the English "a", "an", and "the". Where it is necessary to convey the idea that these convey in English there are of course ways of doing so; but usually it is not at all necessary; and for the present we will not try to indicate them in Luyia.

-----oo0oo-----

NI COPULA
(Page 13)

We will be dealing later with the verb corresponding to the English verb TO BE. But quite often where English would use that verb as a copula, Luyia uses the Copulative Formative NI. Note that the I is elided, or dropped, before an initial vowel, and the N- joined to the following word, except occasionally for emphasis. It will be more fully dealt with later; for the present it may be used in the following cases:
(a) To equate two nouns:
    Yohana nomusoomi.           John is a student.
(b) To join an attribute to its subject:
    Lichina nelikhongo.         The stone is big.

-----oo0oo-----

PRONOMINAL CONCORD
(Page 13)

You have already learnt to use the adjectival concord, and found it easy because it was just the same as the class prefix of the noun. The next to learn is the Pronominal Concord, which is used in building up the Demonstratives (this, that, these, etc.), the Possessives (my, our, your, etc.), and many other important words. It is based not on the prefix, but on the pre-prefix (see p. 10); and although it is only in Bukusu that the full pre-prefix is used, yet there is sufficient connection between it and the prefix to make it simple for us to deduce what the Pronominal Concord will be in each case.
Before we go on to give the method of finding the Pron.Conc., turn back to page 2 and read carefully the note on Palatalization, because we will need to use it. Remember that an I coming after K changes it to CH, so that we get CHI instead of KI.
The Pron.Conc. will consist of a consonant (or a pair of consonants, that is a digraph, e.g. CH) and a vowel. (Page 14) To work them out for yourself, look at the Class Prefix of the class you are considering; let us take two examples, (i) ABA-, and (ii) AMA-. First you discard the initial vowel, i.e. the pre-prefix, in both our examples the letter A. Then if the consonant of the prefix is not a nasal (N or M), write it down; if it is a nasal, write K instead; this gives with our examples (i) B, and (ii) K. Finally write the remaining vowel of the prefix, in both examples A, giving (i) BA-, and (ii) KA-. There is just one more point to notice: the note on Palatalization said that an I coming after K changes it to CH; so with the prefix EMI-, after discarding the E you find you have a nasal, M, and write instead of it a K; but the remaining vowel is I, which will change your K to CH, so that you get a final form CHI-.
Here is a table showing the Pronominal Concords for the classes we have already dealt with. You can, if you think it will help you, work them out for yourself first, or analyze them as you find them here; or if you find it easier, you can simply memorize them.

One more thing - the Singular of the First Class is an exception to the above (you will find very few exceptions in Luyia; the grammar keeps to the rules). Probably because it is the class most used, the consonant of the Pron.Conc. has been dropped, and the vowel is sometimes O and sometimes U.

    Class number        1                   2                   3
    Noun                omundu  abandu      omusaala  emisaala  lichina  amachina
    Class Prefix        omu-    aba-        omu-      emi-      li-      ama-
    Pron.Conc.          o, u    ba-         ku-       chi-      li-      ka-

-----oo0oo-----

POSSESSIVE CONCORD
(Page 14)

The first use to which we will put the Pron.Conc. is in forming the Possessive Concord; this brings two words into an agreement that implies a close association between the objects referred to, but not necessarily one of possession. It can often be represented by the English word "of", as in "the child of John" (or John's child); "a man of the country"; "a member of the council"; "a word of truth" (i.e. a true word). It is formed from the Pron.Conc. agreeing with the preceding noun plus the letter A. As before, a preceding U- changes to W-, and I- remains unchanged; but a preceding A- drops out, leaving just one short A sound. So the simplest forms of the Poss.Part. are as follows:
    omundu wa-              abandu ba-
    omusaala kwa-           emisaala chia-
    lichina lia-            amachina ka-
These particles are joined on to the following word, as is indicated by the hyphen; when they are directly followed by a proper noun (a name, written with a capital letter) the capital is retained:
    omusaatsa waRoda        Roda's husband
When they are joined to a common noun, which of course would have the ordinary Class prefix, we would have two vowels together; but as you have seen this is usually avoided, where possible; in this case the vowel of the Poss.Part. is elided, or dropped. (Page 15) In Class 3 sing., the LI- class, the pre-prefix E- which had been dropped is brought back in this case. As before it is the preceding noun with which the Poss. Part. is brought into concordial agreement. (There is a slight difference in Class 5, which will be dealt with in due course.)
    e.g.    omwana womukhasi            the woman's child
            omundu welitaala            a person of the village
            emioyo chiabandu            people's hearts
            omukanda kwabandu           a crowd of people

-----oo0oo-----

The Poss.Part. can also be combined with the following forms of the Absolute Pronoun to form Possessives (Adjectives or Pronouns), i.e. my, mine, your, yours, etc.:
                    Sing.                           Plur.
    1st Person      my      -nje            our     -ifwe (or -ifu)
    2nd Person      thy     -u              your    -inywe (or -inyu)
    3rd Person      his     -e              their   -bwe (or -bo)

Remember that this part agrees with the possessor; so far we have only shown them for the 1st Class, the one referring to people. The word is completed by prefixing the Poss.Conc. agreeing with the thing possessed; as the possessive immediately follows the name of the thing possessed this is not difficult. The construction of course is exactly the same as with nouns; instead of saying OMWANA WA-RODA you say OMWANA WA-NJE. Note that where the -A comes before -U (2nd sing.) the two coalesce to form O; and before -I- (1st and 2nd plur.) they coalesce to form E; so we get the following forms:

Noun        omwana      abaana      omusaala    emisaala    lichina     amachina
my          wanje       banje       kwanje      chianje     lianje      kanje
thy         -wo         -bo         -kwo        -chio       -lio        -ko
his, her    -we         -be         -kwe        -chie       -lie        -ke
our         weefwe      beefwe      kweefwe     chieefwe    lieefwe     keefwe
your        weenywe     beenywe     kweenywe    chieenywe   lieenywe    keenywe
their       wabwe       babwe       kwabwe      chiabwe     liabwe      kabwe

(Page 16) Notice in the last column, under AMACHINA, that where the E or EE = A-I, it does not change the K to CH.
The 2nd and 3rd pers.sing., -E and -O (thy and his) are enclitic, i.e. they are joined on to the preceding noun, and cannot stand alone; e.g. OMWANAWO, AMAKONDIKE. For emphasis a reduplicated form is used and this can stand alone. In these three classes it takes the following forms:
    wuwo    bobo    kukwo   chichio     lilio   koko
    wuwe    bebe    kukwe   chichie     lilie   keke

When a possessive is used as a pronoun (as English MINE, YOURS, etc., in such sentences as "This is mine; where is yours?") it takes the initial vowel (pre-prefix) of the noun class, giving such forms as the following:
    owanje      abanje      okwanje     echianje    elianje     akanje
    owuwo       abobo       okukwo      echichio    elilio      akoko
    etc.

The possessive when used adjectivally (my book, her child) follows immediately after the noun, other adjectives coming after it; the fact that THY and HER are actually joined on to the noun will help you to remember this.
    omwana wanje                        my child
    omusaalakwo                         your (sing.) tree
    omusaala kukwo                      your tree (not mine)
    okukwo                              yours
    omusaala nokukwo                    the tree is yours
    litaala lieefwe                     our village
    litaala nelieefwe                   the village is ours
    emikoye chianje emirambi            my long ropes

THE VERB
(Page 17)

The simple stem of the verb, with apparently only two exceptions (the -LI stem of the verb TO BE, and the -LUTSI "perfect" stem of the verb TO SEE) ends in -A. The sign of the infinitive is the prefix OKHU-; and the U as before changes to W before a vowel.
    khola       do              okhukhola       to do (or doing)
    sunga       speak           okhusunga       to speak (or speaking)
    itsa        come            okhwitsa        to come (or coming)
    lola        see             okhulola        to see (or seeing)

The verb must always be brought into agreement with its subject by the use of the Subjectival Concord; whether the noun that is the real subject is mentioned or not, the Subjectival Concord agreeing with it must be attached to the beginning of the verb. As we shall see later, the different tenses and various derived forms of the verb are formed by means of affixes attached to the beginning or end or put in the middle of the word. But the Present Tense is simply formed by prefixing the Subj.Conc. to the stem of the verb, and that is the one we will take first.

Subjectival Concords
                Singular                                    Plural
Class 1:
    1st (I)     en- (enz-, eny-, end-)          (we)        khu- (khw-)
    2nd (thou)  o- (w-)                         (you)       mu- (mw-)
    3rd (he)    a- (ya-)                        (they)      ba- (ba-)
Class 2:
    (it)        ku- (kw-)                       (they)      chi- (chi-)
Class 3:
    (it)        li- (li-)                       (they)      ka- (ka-)

You will notice that except in Class 1 these are simply the Pron. Conc. The forms enclosed in brackets are those used before vowel stems; in the 1st sing. the usual concord is EN- before a consonant; but re-read the note on Nasalization on p. 2, and notice what happens when the N comes before certain consonants. When the stem begins with a vowel the prefix is usually ENZ-, unless the first consonant in the stem is a nasal, and then the prefix is ENY-; thus we get from ITSA (come), ENZITSA (I am coming); but from IMBA (sing), we get ENYIMBA (I am singing). This differentiation is breaking down, and you will quite often hear the ordinary ENZ- before a vowel plus nasal; (Page 18) in fact with some verbs, and particularly ENYA (want), that is the more usual form - ENZENYA; but with most verbs of this type the ENY- form is more strictly correct, and is still more usual, though in some cases it may sound a little pedantic.

Present Tense
This is the tense usually best translated with the use of the English AM, ARE, etc.; e.g. I am writing, he is walking. It is formed from Subj.Conc. plus Verb Stem.

    sooma = read
    esooma          I am reading                khusooma        we are reading
    osooma          you are reading             musooma         you are reading
    asooma          he/she is reading           basooma         they are reading
    kusooma         it is reading               chisooma        they are reading (Cl. 2)
    lisoomo         it is reading               kasooma         they are reading (Cl. 3)
    Note that the N was dropped in 1st sing., because S does not nasalize.

    teema = try
    endeema         I am trying                 khuteema        we are trying
    oteema          you are trying              muteema         you are trying
    ateema          he is trying                bateema         they are trying
    kuteema         it is trying                chiteema        they are trying
    liteema         it is trying                kateema         they are trying
    In the 1st sing., N-T = ND.

Those both had stems beginning with a consonant; now let us look at a verb with a vowel stem:
    itsa = come
    enzitsa         I am coming                 khwitsa         we are coming
    witsa           you are coming              mwitsa          you are coming
    yeetsa          he is coming                beetsa          they are coming
    kwitsa          it is coming                chiitsa         they are coming
    liitsa          it is coming                keetsa          they are coming
    ya-itsa gives yeetsa;  ba-itsa gives beetsa;  ka-itsa gives keetsa (not sheetsa)

A Few Verbs
    mala            finish                      chinga          lift, carry
    lima            dig, cultivate              chenda          walk, travel
    bakha           paint, anoint               linda           wait, mind, look after
    teekha          cook                        hulila          hear, understand, obey
    kwa             fall                        para            think
    tsia            go                          kora            get lost
    homa            smear (as floor)            leera           bring
    osia            wash                        imba            sing
    eya             sweep                       eka             learn

-----oo0oo-----

NUMBERS
(Page 19)

Of the ordinary numbers used in counting (cardinal numbers), 1-6 have to agree with their noun, but 7-10 are invariable. 1 is formed by prefixing the Adj.Conc. (without the initial vowel) to the stem -LALA; and 2-6 are formed from the stems given below with the Pron. Conc. (not the Adjectival, as with 1); but note that in Class 3, 1 is ELALA. In abstract counting the forms used are those used with Class 5 nouns, so these forms are given below.
The word for 10 is EKHUMI; but multiples of 10 (20, 30, etc.) are formed by making the stem into a noun in Class 3, LIKHUMI, with plural AMAKHUMI. Numbers between the tens are shown by the number of tens plus NA (= and) plus the number representing the units; it is only this number representing the units that is made to agree with the noun.
In a sentence numbers may either follow or precede other adjectives; of course they follow possessives.

Cardinal numbers (Stems and Class 5 / Abstract counting forms):
    1   -lala       ndala
    2   -bili       tsibili
    3   -taru       tsitaru
    4   -ne         tsine
    5   -raano      tsiraano (tsirano)
    6   -sasaba     tsisasaba
    7   musaafu     musaafu (invariable)
    8   munaane     munaane (invariable)
    9   shienda     shienda (invariable)
    10  ekhumi      ekhumi (invariable)
    Multiples of 10:
    20  amakhumi kabili
    30  amakhumi kataru
    40  amakhumi kane
    50  amakhumi karaano
    60  amakhumi kasasaba
    70  amakhumi musaafu
    80  amakhumi munaane
    90  amakhumi shienda
    100 ekhumi yakhabili (or amakhumi ekhumi / elikhumi)

Ordinal Numbers
The ordinal numbers (first, second, third, etc.) are formed from the appropriate Poss.Part. plus KHA (only with numbers 2-6) plus the number stem; except that "first" is Poss.Part. plus MBELI (IMBELI = in front). The initial vowel of the Poss.Part. is sometimes retained in forming the ordinals, specially when they are used as pronouns (e.g. "This is first, and that is second.")

    The first person            Omundu wambeli
    The first (lot of) people   Abandu bambeli
    The first trees             Emisaala chiambeli
    The first village           Litaala liambeli
    The second man              Omusaatsa wakhabili
    The third (lot of) ropes    Emikoye chiakhataru
    Which man? The fourth       Omundu shiina? Owakhane
    The seventh name            Eliira liamusaafu (Often heard with -OMU: liomusaafu, kwomunaane)
    The eighth month            Omwesi kwamunaane
    The ninth child             Omwana washienda
    The tenth orange            Lichungwa liekhumi (or lielikhumi)
    The eleventh person         Omundu welikhumi nalulala*
    The twelfth stone           Lichina lielikhumi nakhabili
    The twentieth messenger     Omurume wamakhumi kabili
    The forty-first sheep       Likondi liamakhumi kane nalulala*
    The fifty-fourth potato     Lipwoni liamakhumi karaano nakhane
    The ninety-eighth tree      Omusaala kwamakhumi shienda namunaane

* As -MBELI literally means the leading one, it cannot be used after 10 or a multiple of 10; so as shown above the form is LULALA, i.e. -LALA with the LU- concord of enumeration, singular; KHA- is the corresponding plural concord.

NOUNS: Class 4: ESHI - EBI
(Page 20)

In many parts the singular of this class is pronounced as ESI-; in others, the plural is EFI-; but the spelling should be ESHI-EBI, which represents the pronunciation of the majority.
(Page 21) Nouns in this class usually indicate things, often man-made things; e.g. ESHISAALA is a thing made from a tree (OMUSAALA), i.e. a stool; ESHIHAANWA is a thing given (from the passive of HAANA = give) i.e. a present; the plurals of these words are respectively EBISAALA and EBIHAANWA.
Before you look at the list of Concords and Possessives that follows the next exercise, try and work them out for yourself as we did with the other three classes.

Class 5: I(N) - TSI(N)
(Page 21)

This class has more variations of form than most of the others; the reason for this is an uncertainty as to what is really the prefix; not a conscious uncertainty in the minds of this generation, but misconceptions during the growth and development of the language. (Page 22) The real prefix originally was NI-, with I- as the pre-prefix, giving INI-; the second I was lost, leaving only N- as the prefix, and I- the pre-prefix. These were so different from the usual forms of prefix and pre-prefix that they were often treated as simply a prefix and even the N- was often dropped, so that the I- of the pre-prefix was used as a prefix. And then as other classes had an initial vowel (pre-prefix) that could sometimes be dropped, by false analogy this class too was sometimes supplied with an initial E-, and sometimes a -Y- was put after it to make a glide from the E- to the -I, giving EYI. That is an attempt to reconstruct part of the history of this class; the following are the present facts:

1. The prefix is sometimes I- and sometimes IN-, and in the plural TSI- or TSIN-. When the N is there it nasalizes the following consonant, if it is one that nasalizes; otherwise the N is dropped. We can say that the prefix sometimes does not include the N, as sometimes a consonant that should nasalize does not do so:
    induukho = in-tuukho    season (tuukha = reach the right time)
    itaywa   = i-taywa      rooster
If the prefix had been IN- in the second example the T would have nasalized, giving INDAYWA.

2. These prefixes may have an initial vowel E-, particularly when the word is closely associated with the preceding one; e.g. "the child of the cow" is OMWANA WEING'OMBE (= WA-EING'OMBE; the A of WA is elided as usual, but the E of the false pre-prefix is inserted).

    Sing.           Plur.           Meaning                                     Stem
    imoni           tsimoni         eye                                         -moni (bona)
    itaha           tsitaha         lamp                                        -taha
    imbeba          tsimbeba        rat                                         -beba
    inzu            tsinzu          house                                       -tsu
    isiongo         tsisiongo       water-pot                                   -siongo
    inzokha         tsinzokha       snake, worm                                 -yokha
    ing'ombe        tsing'ombe      head of cattle (cow, bull, etc.)            -kombe
    ingubo          tsingubo        cloth, dress, garment                       -kubo
    indubi          tsindubi        small basket                                -lubi
    inyeeni         tsinyeeni       fish                                        -nyeeni

ING'OMBE from -KOMBE (compare INGUBO from -KUBO: in the former case the sound as in "sing", in the latter as in "single") illustrates the same principle as ENIMA from -LIMA; that when nasalization forms a nasal compound such as MB, ND, NG, and the following syllable also begins with a nasal, the plosive (B, D, G) may be dropped. IMONI also illustrates this, as it is from a verb in use in some parts of the language area, BONA = see; this would give IMBONI, but the B is dropped because of the N in the next syllable. - In INZOKHA, although other forms of the word show the stem to be -YOKHA, it is treated as if it were a vowel stem (see p. 17).

Concords and Possessives:
    Pron.Conc.:     Sing.   (y)i- or chi-*          Plur.   tsi-
    Poss.Part.:             ya                              tsia
    Subj.Conc.:             yi- (y-)                        tsi-

Possessives:
    my                      yanje                           tsianje
(Page 23)
    your                    -yo                             -tsio
    his/her                 -ye                             -tsie
    our                     yeefwe                          tsieefwe
    your                    yeenywe                         tsieenywe
    their                   yabwe                           tsiabwe

* If the full original form INI- is considered, the Pron.Conc. would be (as it often is) CHI-; it is because the N is so often dropped out that the more usual form is YI-. For the Subj.Conc., and in forming Possessives and Demonstratives, the YI- form is used; in the Objectival Concord (which we have not yet dealt with) and in one or two other ways the CHI- form is used.

Class 6: OLU - TSIN
(Page 23)

Nouns in this class often indicate things that are long or tall in proportion to their width.
    omusaala        tree            olusaala        a stick
    eshibia         iron            olubia          a railway line

Plurals are formed as in Class 5; but the prefix is always TSIN-, so (i) if the stem begins with a consonant that nasalizes, remember to make the change: e.g. OLUKULU = range of mountains; plur. TSINGULU; (ii) if the stem begins with a consonant that does not nasalize, the N is dropped; e.g. OLUSAALA, stick; plur. TSISAALA; (iii) with a vowel stem, if the first consonant is a nasal, use TSINY-; if it is not a nasal, use TSINZ-; e.g. OLWAKHO = boundary; plur. TSINZAKHO; OLWANDA = rock; plur., TSINYANDA.

Reference has already been made in the section on numbers to the -LU concord of enumeration; the following uses of it should be noted and learnt:
    khandi lulala           once
    leelo luno              today; this very day
    luno lukhola khataru    this is the third time

This class is also used for names of languages (OLULIMI = tongue, language); OLULUYIA, OLUNGERESA, OLUSWAHILI.

(Page 24)

-----oo0oo-----

DEMONSTRATIVES
(Page 29)

There seem to be four simple demonstratives in Oluluyia, of which the three in most common use are formed as follows:
(Note: PC = Pronominal Concord; IV = Initial Vowel)

Class   Noun            PC          this (PC - no)  that (IV-PC-o)  that yonder (PC-lia)
1       omundu          u-, o-      uno             oyo*            ulia
        abandu          ba-         bano            abo             balia
2       omusaala        ku-         kuno            okwo            kulia
        emisaala        chi-        chino           echio           chilia
3       (e)lichina      li-         lino            elo**           lilia
        amachina        ka-         kano            ako             kalia
4       eshindu         shi-        shino           eshio           shilia
        ebindu          bi-         bino            ebio            bilia
5       (ey)ing'ombe    (y)i-       yino            eyo             yilia
        (e)tsing'ombe   tsi-        tsino           etsio           tsilia
6       olusaala        lu-         luno            olwo            lulia
        (e)tsisaala     tsi-        tsino           etsio           tsilia

* Notice the Y in this, which does not appear in the PC.
** One would expect this to be ELIO; but the I seems to have been dropped completely.

There is another form of the Demonstrative, which appears to be formed from (Y) - IV - PC, giving such forms as YABA, OKU, YICHI, ELI, YAKA, etc. This is probably a form of "this" referring to things near at hand, but not actually within reach; but I am not yet quite sure of this. Don't try to use it until you have heard it in use; but listen for it, and try to list and analyse its occurrences.

Word order: Demonstratives follow possessives, but usually precede other adjectives:
    omusaala kuno omurambi              this tall tree
    ebindu bieefwe bino ebilayi         these good things of ours

-----oo0oo-----

VERB "TO BE"
(Page 30)

The verb TO BE has two quite different stems in Oluluyia - -LI and -BA - which are used in different tenses. For the present we will only deal with the Present Tense, which is formed from the -LI stem.

Class 1:
    1st         endi        (I am)              khuli       (we are)
    2nd         oli         (you are)           muli        (you are)
    3rd         ali         (he/she is)         bali        (they are)
Class 2:
    omusaala    kuli        (it is)             emisaala    chili   (they are)
Class 3:
    lichina     lili                            amachina    kali
Class 4:
    eshindu     shili                           ebindu      bili
Class 5:
    inzu        yili                            tsinzu      tsili
Class 6:
    olusaala    luli                            tsisaala    tsili

The suffix -HO is often used with the verb TO BE to indicate either HERE, or the impersonal THERE. Thus OMWANA ALIHO could mean either THE CHILD IS HERE, or THERE IS A CHILD HERE.
(Page 31)
    Abandu baliho?      Are people here? or Are there (any) people?
    Ee, baliho abanji.  Yes, there are a lot.
HANO is more definite than -HO as specifying a place:
    Abandu bali hano.   The people are here.

Exercise 20
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. Where are you?
2. We are here.
3. We are Sara's children.
4. Where is that book of mine?
5. It is here.
6. Is there (any) mud?
7. Yes, there is.
8. Is your (s.) son there?
9. Yes, he is.
10. Are there many sheep?
11. Yes, there are many.
12. Is there (any) milk?
13. Yes, there is.
14. Where are my dogs?
15. They are here.
16. Where is your (s.) medicine?
17. It is here.
18. Are there many people?
19. Yes, there are many.
20. There is a lot of work.
21. I am a teacher.

-----oo0oo-----

COPULA NI
(Page 31)

We will now deal with this a little more fully. The examples given in Exercise 20 are all correct uses of the verb -LI, although in some cases the Copula NI could be used instead of it. In the many cases where NI is interchangeable with or preferable to the full form of the verb TO BE, it can stand for that verb in any number or noun class; it is usually confined to the 3rd person. The verb TO BE is used with -HO and -YO suffixes and with other locative constructions (e.g. with WHERE, HERE, THERE), and with 1st and 2nd person. But in most other cases the Copulative Formative NI is used in preference to the form in -LI or -BA, particularly in the present tense.
    oyo nomwami                 that is the chief
    omwalo nomurambi            the river is long
    eyo neingubo yanje          that is my dress
    eyo neyanje                 that is mine.

As has already been seen, the I is dropped before a following vowel; and with nouns of class 5, sing. and plur., and class 6 plur., the false pre-prefix E- is inserted. Occasionally the NI is pronounced as a separate word for emphasis, and then it may be written so. If the following noun is spelt with a capital letter, the NI is joined as usual and the capital retained; e.g. niTomasi; nOmusula.
Note that NI usually indicates identification, and the verb TO BE location.

Vocabulary
    nende           and, with
    ne              but, and
    na              and, with
    khandi          and, again
    bulaano         now
    leelo           today

Be very cautious in the use of words for "and"; they cannot be used in every place where an English "and" is used; note in particular that NENDE and NA are very seldom used to join clauses; never use them in this way until you have studied the section on "AND" further on in this book. NENDE and NA join nouns that stand for different things; KHANDI, when they refer to the same person or thing.
    abakhaana nende abasiani            girls and boys
    ing'ombe neimbusi                   a cow and a goat
    Omwami wanje, khandi Nyasaye wanje. my Lord and my God
In the last example, the use of NENDE would have implied that there were two different people.

-----oo0oo-----

COALESCENCE AND ELISION
(Page 32)

To refresh your memory, let us revise the rules governing coalescence (when two adjacent vowels combine to form a new one, e.g. a-e = ee) and elision (the dropping of a vowel).
First, the vowel of a Poss.Part. (-A), NI copula (-I), or NA conjunction (-A) is elided before the vowel of a following word, and the two are written conjunctively; note that when this elision occurs the initial E in the (E)LI-, (E)I-, and (E)TSI- classes is used:
    ka omwana                   of the child                    komwana
    kwa eshindu                 of the thing                    kweshindu
    ni abaana                   it is the children              nabaana
    ni abaana                   they are children               nabaana
    ni (e)litaala               it is the village               nelitaala
    ihaywa na (e)imbako         the axe and the hoe             ihaywa neimbako
    ni inzu                     it is the house                 neinzu
    indubi ya tsinyeeni         a basket of fish                indubi yetsinyeeni
    omwana wa Otero             Otero's child                   omwana wOtero

Nouns with Vowel Stems
If the vowel in the prefix is the same as that in the stem, write both:
    aba-ana                     children                        abaana
    omu-upi                     one who beats                   omuupi
Vowel I in the prefix is always written:
    eshi-alo                    country                         eshialo
(Page 33)
Vowel U becomes semi-vowel W:
    omu-oyo                     heart                           omwoyo
Vowel A coalesces with the following vowel as follows:
    a-e = ee        aba-echi        pupils                      abeechi
    a-i = ee        aba-itsa        friends                     abeetsa
    a-o = oo        aba-onooni      sinners                     aboonooni
    a-u = oo        ama-ulu         nose                        amoolu

Verbs with Vowel Stems
The vowel of any prefix or infix coalesces with the initial vowel of a verb stem in the same way as that of a prefix with a noun stem. (Note that we are not yet dealing with the case of adjacent vowels in two affixes, as such a case has not yet arisen).
    ba-ahula                    they set apart                  baahula
    shi-itsa                    it is coming                    shiitsa
    ya-eka                      he is learning                  yeeka
    ka-itsa                     they are coming                 keetsa
    ba-upa                      they beat                       boopa
    khu-enya                    we want                         khwenya

SOME MORE ADJECTIVES
(Page 38)

    -siro           heavy                       -cheni          strange, novel
    -bofu           blind                       -lamba          whole
    -akhanyu        red or reddish

As already noted, BULI = EVERY is different from other adjectives in two ways: it is invariable (i.e. it does not take a concord), and it precedes the noun, which drops its initial vowel.
    e.g.    buli mundu      every person
            buli shindu     every thing

Exercise 23

1. He writes letters every day.
2. The blind woman is answering every question.
3. Every child is bringing a broom.
4. They are carrying heavy stones to build the church.
5. Every boy is eating a whole orange.
6. The old woman is carrying a red cloth.
7. Every teacher is teaching his class.
8. The cows are crossing over the river.
9. That baby is heavy; this one is light.
10. We are crossing over the road to see our blind friend.

-----oo0oo-----

TO HAVE
(Page 39)

This is formed from the verb "to be" (LI, BA) followed by "with" (NENDE); that is, "I have" is literally "I am with"; and often the meaning is more of association than of actual personal ownership as we understand it.
Note that the difference between "okhuba nende" and "to have" is reflected in the translation of the ordinary English "to be with". "I am with my friend" usually means that I am accompanying him in some rather subordinate capacity; "endi nende omwitsa wanje" would mean "I have brought my friend." (Note that "itsa nende" is regularly used for bringing something with you.)

    I have                  endi nende          we have             khuli nende
    you (s.) have           oli nende           you have            muli nende
    he/she has              ali nende           they have           bali nende
    it (omusaala) has       kuli nende          they (emisaala)     chili nende
      (lichina)             lili nende            (amachina)        kali nende
      (eshindu)             shili nende           (ebindu)          bili nende
      (inzu)                yili nende            (tsinzu)          tsili nende
      (olusaala)            luli nende            (tsisaala)        tsili nende

Vocabulary
    -nga? (sometimes -inga?) = how many?
    Note that these take the Pron.Conc.; e.g.
        Abandu banga?       How many people?
        Emisaala chinga?    How many trees?

-----oo0oo-----

TENSES
(Page 40)

As there are far more verb tenses in Luyia than in English, the meaning of each is more restricted. There is no general past tense; any one of the past tenses indicates roughly how long past you are referring to. This indication is sometimes relative rather than absolute; e.g. the Near Past tense indicates the near past period of time, usually within the preceding 24 hours or so; but in speaking of years, it would be used of last year; in speaking of generations, it would be used of the last generation; of wars, of the last war; and so on. In the following notes this aspect may not be emphasised again, as the more general use is, of course, with reference to the ordinary passage of days and weeks.
It is important to realise at the outset that each of these tenses has a very definite time significance. If, for instance, you ask whether a certain event took place, and in asking use the Near Past tense, you would receive a negative answer if it had happened a week or so ago. To ask "Did you go?" in the Near Past would not be taken as having any reference to his having gone last week.
There is much in the significance of the Luyia tenses that I do not yet understand. For instance there are tonal differences that distinguish tenses, but I do not know much about them. Then the past tenses appear to be used not only to indicate an action that took place in the past, but sometimes one that began in the past and is still going on in the present, or of which the results still remain. It is similar to the English perfect, but not identical with it. Thus EMANYILE = I KNOW, though the verb is in the Near Past tense; it apparently implies that I have known for some time. Again ASINJIILE = HE IS STANDING; this may be because the verb refers to the act of rising to the feet, or it may be similar to the previous example. Much further investigation is necessary before any very definite statement of the full significance of the tenses can be made.

Let us revise the method of forming the
Present Tense: (Subj.Conc. - Stem of Verb)
    khola = make / I am making
    Singular                                    Plural
    ekhola                                      khukhola
    okhola                                      mukhola
    akhola                                      bakhola
(Other classes)
    kukhola                                     chikhola
    likhola                                     kakhola
    shikhola                                    bikhola
    yikhola                                     tsikhola
    lukhola                                     tsikhola

(Page 41)
Near Past Tense: (Subj.Conc. - Stem of Verb with final -A changed to -ILE)
    I made (today and possibly yesterday)
    ekholile                                    khukholile
    okholile                                    mukholile
    akholile                                    bakholile
    kukholile                                   chikholile
    etc.

In the Near Future tense, instead of the final -A the verb ending is -E; and in addition NA- is put in front even of the Subj.Conc.
Near Future Tense: (NA- - Subj.Conc. - Stem of Verb with final -A changed to -E)
    I shall make (later today, or tomorrow)
    nekhole                                     nakhukhole
    nokhole                                     namukhole
    nakhole                                     nabakhole
    nakukhole                                   nachikhole
    etc.

ALL, ANY, ALSO
(Page 42)

The word that is variously translated thus in English is formed from the Absolute Pronoun (with slight modifications) plus -SI. We have not yet dealt with the Absolute Pronoun, but the following are the main forms of the word for ALL, etc.:
    siesi       I also (Isie siesi)             fwesi       we also, all of us
    wesi        you also, you whoever           mwesi       you also, all of you
                  you are
    yesi        he also, any(one)               bosi        they also, all of them
    kwosi       it also, any (omusaala)         chiosi      they also, all (trees)
    shiosi      it also, any (eshindu)          biosi       they also, all (things)

IMPERATIVE
(Page 43)

Singular Imperative: An order given to one person is represented by the simple stem of the verb, except that with a vowel stem, Y- is prefixed:
    okhukhola       to make, do         Khola.          Do (that).
    okhutsia        to go               Tsia.           Go.
    okhweya         to sweep            Yeya.           Sweep.
    okhwala         to spread out       Yala imeesa.    Set the table.

Plural Imperative: An order given to more than one person is as above, with the final -A of the verb stem changed to -E:
    Khole.          (All of you) do it.
    Linde.          (All of you) wait.
    Tsie.           (All of you) go.
    Yeye.           (All of you) sweep.
    Yale imeesa.    (All of you) set the table.

-----oo0oo-----

Negative Imperative, Singular: The stem of the verb, with ORA- prefixed and the particle TA following; with vowel stems, coalescence takes place between the -A- and the initial vowel of the stem, according to the rules given on pp. 32-33. Note that the TA usually comes at the end of the sentence. Note also that in most areas the ORA- prefix is pronounced as OLA-.
    Orakhola ta.            Don't do it.
    Oratsia ta.             Don't go.
    Oreeya ta.              Don't sweep.
    Oraala imeesa ta.       Don't set the table.

Negative Imperative, Plural: As for the singular, except that the subjectival concord is used, i.e. MU- instead of O-, with -RA- as before. Notice that in this case the final vowel does not change to -E. (There is a form MURA...E, the negative of the subjunctive, which is used as a more polite way of giving an order; e.g. MUREETSE TA = YOU NEEDN'T COME, in contrast to MUREETSA TA = DON'T COME. But the straight-out imperative has the final -A).
(Page 44)
    Murakhola ta.           Don't (any of you) do that.
    Muratsia ta.            Don't (any of you) go.
    Mureeya ta.             Don't (any of you) sweep.
    Muraala imeesa ta.      Don't (any of you) set the table.

INTERROGATIVES
(Page 44)

    wina? (pl. beena?)      who?                        shiina?         what? which? what kind?
    heena?                  where?

                                                        olwashiina?)

* For "Why?", it seems that generally MBUSHIINA is used following the simple form of the verb, and OLWASHIINA is generally used at the beginning of a sentence, with a NI form of the verb, which will be dealt with in a later section. Although these are the more usual positions and uses of the two forms, they appear to be interchangeable.

You will notice that these are all formed from an -INA stem - U-INA, BA-INA, SHI-INA, HA-INA. LIINA is really the LU- concord of time or enumeration, giving originally LU-INA, or LWINA; but the U has been assimilated to the following I, giving a long II sound. (This frequently happens; AFWILE is usually pronounced as AFIILE, and the name OLWIRA as OLIIRA.)

    Omundu oyo niwina?              Who is that person?

    Abaana bali heena?              Where are the children?
    Batsiile mbushiina?             Why did they go?
    Olwashiina nibatsiile?          Why did they go?

RELATIONSHIP TERMS

As in most languages, the words for "my mother" and "my father" are the sounds the baby first makes - MAMA and PAPA (in some districts TATA); note that the two syllables are evenly stressed, and that the A sound is short, being somewhat similar to the U in the English word BUT.

These words are used only for "MY mother" and "MY father". For "THY mother", half of MAMA (MA-) is used, with the usual enclitic -WO, giving MAWO = THY MOTHER; note that the W is very lightly sounded; it might almost be spelt MAO. FATHER and MOTHER with all other possessives are formed from quite different stems: MOTHER from NYINA (which can be used alone for HIS or HER MOTHER, or THE MOTHER OF - NYINA YOHANA = JOHN'S MOTHER); and FATHER from SA, a stem that is never used by itself, although some dialects use ISE. THY FATHER is formed from SA-(W)O, with the W dropped, = SO. HIS or HER FATHER is SAMWANA = SA-OMWANA, the father of the child; and HIS or HER MOTHER similarly is NYINAMWANA. OUR FATHER is SA-IFWE, = SEEFWE; YOUR FATHER, SA-INYWE, = SEENYWE; THEIR FATHER, SA-ABWE, = SABWE; and the words for MOTHER are NYINEEFWE, NYINEENYWE, NYINABWE. Here are all the forms:

            Mother          Father
my          mama            papa
thy         mawo            so
his, her    nyinamwana      samwana
our         nyineefwe       seefwe
your        nyineenywe      seenywe
their       nyinabwe        sabwe

In English, for each individual there is only one person to whom the term MOTHER or FATHER refers; that is because the unit of society is the family - the husband and his one wife and their children; they are the "inner circle" of relations, and the set of relationship terms they use of each other (husband, wife, father, mother, son, daughter, brother, sister) are not used outside that family group.

African society is not based on this inner circle of the family, but on the wider unit of the clan; and relationship terms reflect that fact. It must be recognised at the outset that no single unqualified relationship term in English corresponds exactly to any single unqualified term in Oluluyia. If we examine some of them this will become clearer:

PAPA. It is not only my own father that I call PAPA, but all men of his generation within his clan, i.e. his brothers and cousins, so PAPA may mean father, or uncle (on my father's side), or second cousin, etc. If I want to specify FATHER, and not uncle, etc., I have to say "my father who begot me". It is no mere matter of language that I call all the others by the same name; they all have the same responsibility to me as my father, and I owe them all the same respect.

MAMA refers to my own mother ("the mother who bore me"), to her sisters, cousins, and others of her generation within her clan, and also to the other wives of my father and the wives of his brothers and cousins; so it may mean mother, maternal aunt, second cousin, step mother, etc.

OMWANA WANJE (lit. my child), OMUKHAANA WANJE (my girl), and OMUSIANI WANJE (my boy), can be used by a man of any person within his clan of the generation below his own - that is, his own child or the child of any fellow-clansman of his own generation, a niece or nephew or second cousin; this means that one I call OMWANA WANJE may easily be older than I am. A woman may use these terms in referring to her own children, her sisters' children, her cousins' children, and the children of her co-wives and the wives of her husband's brothers and cousins. So OMUSIANI WANJE may mean my step son, nephew, or second cousin; and so with OMUKHAANA WANJE.

OMWANA WEEFWE, OMUSIANI WEEFWE, and OMUKHAANA WEEFWE are often loosely translated as BROTHER and SISTER; this is usually quite incorrect, as only a very small proportion of those I call OMWANA WEEFWE are my sisters or brothers. It really means "a person of my own generation within our clan"; and it is also used as one of the terms for people of my own generation in my mother's clan (i.e. the children of her brothers and male cousins, those she in turn calls OMUSIANI WEEFWE), and for the children of my father's sisters. So it should be translated as COUSIN far more frequently than as BROTHER or SISTER. Note that the plural possessive is part of the term; OMUSIANI WEEFWE should be translated as MY brother or cousin; though it may also be used by parents jointly of their son; OMUSIANI WANJE would be my son or nephew. Note also that it is far more usual to say OMWANA WEEFWE than OMUSIANI WEEFWE or OMUKHAANA WEEFWE.

The clan (OLUYIA) consists of the descendants of one man; people of the same OLUYIA cannot intermarry. In this patrilineal society a person belongs to the father's clan; a girl of course will be lost to the clan when she marries (hence the "dowry", or equilibrium guarantee, IKHWE) so as far as clan membership is concerned she is referred to by a special term, OMUKOKO. Also each clan has a proper name which for its ABAKOKO is usually written with a NA- prefix; thus a man of Butere is OMUNGULU, but a girl is NANGULU (note, not a Nangulu); a man of Marama is an OMUKHULA, but a girl is NAMUKHULA. This proper name is called an INONO.

Two other important pairs of terms are those for the mother's brother and the father's sister. The latter is called SENJE, and she in turn calls her brother's child OMUSENJETSANWA; while the mother's brother is called KHOOTSA, and he calls his sister's child OMWIHWA. (The terms brother and sister have been used here for brevity; they should be "those my mother calls OMUSIANI WEEFWE", and "those my father calls OMUKHAANA WEEFWE".) Note the following forms:

            Khootsa             Senje
my          khootsa             senje
your        khootsawo           nyakhusenjewo
his/her     khootsanyene        nyakhusenjenyene

Note that a person is known as an OMWIHWA of the place from which his mother came; and in connection with him it is referred to as "ebukhootsanyene".

All those my mother or father calls PAPA, I call KUUKA; and those they call MAMA, I call KUUKHU. All those in turn call me OMWITSUKHULU.

A husband calls each of his wives OMUKHASI WANJE or OMUTEESHI WANJE, and she calls him OMUSAATSA WANJE; while OMUKHASI WEEFWE is the term used by other members of the clan in speaking of the wife of one of its members, who was of course an OMUKOKO from another clan. The first wife a man marries is called OMUKHASI OMUKHULUNDU; and his favourite wife (often the same one) is OMUKHAYE. The term OMUKHAYE is also used by others of a woman who is in the true sense of the word a lady - a good housewife, of good character, and given to hospitality. The word is also generally adopted in these days for the one wife of a monogamist. MUKHA is used for "the wife of", or Mrs.; MUKHA YOHANA = John's wife, or Mrs. John. A woman often refers to her husband as OMWAMI WANJE; and although OMWAMI used only to be used of an important man, it is becoming generally adopted as an equivalent of the English "Mr."

As each wife of a man has her own separate house the children of one wife (full brothers and sisters) can be called ABOMUNZU NDALA (= of one house). Children of the different wives of a polygamist can be called ABAANA BOMUSAATSA MULALA. One of these expressions would have to be used to translate the English "they are brothers", as there is nothing directly corresponding to it in Luyia.

There are very many more relationship terms than those given here; but these are the simplest and most common, and should be learnt. They should also serve to illustrate the difference in usage between the two languages, and the necessity of approaching the whole question of relationship terms from an African standpoint and not from the familiar European one.

-----oo0oo-----

NEAR PAST TENSE
of polysyllabic verbs

If the stem of a verb contains more than two syllables the near past tense is usually formed by lengthening the penultimate vowel and changing the -A to -E, instead of by adding the usual -ILE, unless the penultimate vowel is already long, in which case it takes the usual ending.

e.g.
hulila          hear            embuliile           I heard
sungana         talk to         khusungaane         we talked to each
                  each other                          other
khalaka         cut             bakhalaache         they cut
kasia           arrange         khukasiye*          we arranged
manyia          show            akhumanyiye*        she showed us

but
handiika        write           ahandiichile        he wrote
huluukha        rest            bahuluushile        they rested
lekhuula        release         bamulekhuulile      they released him

* The Y represents the second I in the lengthening, because of the following vowel.
