-----oo0oo-----

OBJECTIVAL CONCORD

The objectival concord is used with a verb when the object itself is not named (e.g. neMUlole = I shall see him); it is also often used even when the object has been named; in this case it sometimes indicates that the object is some definite person or thing, its insertion or omission making the distinction made in English by the use of the definite or indefinite article, "the" or "a"; e.g. ENGULILE ING'OMBE = I bought a cow; ENJIKULILE ING'OMBE = I bought the cow; but this usage is not invariable. The objectival concord is the same as the pronominal concord, and differs from the subjectival concord only in the singular of class 1 and class 5. It is always inserted immediately before the stem of the verb.

Class               Sing.           Plur.
1. omu-aba  1st     -n-*            -khu-
            2nd     -khu-           -mu-
            3rd     -mu-            -ba-
2. omu-emi          -ku-            -chi-
3. (e)li-ama        -li-            -ka-
4. eshi-ebi         -shi-           -bi-
5. i(n)-tsi(n)      -i-, -chi-      -tsi-
6. olu-tsin         -lu-            -tsi-

e.g.
andola              he sees me
alindola            he will see me (-li-, Far Future)
akhulola            he sees you (s.), or he sees us
amulola             he sees her or he sees you (pl.)
abalola             he sees them
akulola             he sees it (omusaala)
achilola            he sees them (emisaala)
ailola  )
   or   )           he sees it (ing'ombe)
achilola)
atsilola            he sees them (tsing'ombe)

* For 1st Sing.Obj.Conc., "me", see section following the next exercise.

Exercise 33
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. This person is touching me.
2. The crowd passed him.
3. The spears pierced him.
4. They brought two baskets.
5. They brought the two baskets.
6. The children heard us.
7. They fed me with fruit.
8. We told them.
9. I told you.
10. He will remember you (s.).
11. She brought the potatoes.
12. They bought that dress.
13. He told me (about) that matter.
14. He cut the bread.
15. They will see us.
16. I showed you my house.
17. He uncovered the plate.
18. We read those letters.
19. He painted that wall.
20. Those girls will visit me.
21. She swallowed all the food.

1st Pers.Sing.Obj.Concord, English ME.

As shown above, this is -N-, and is shown by nasalizing the initial consonant of the verb stem. Where this consonant does not nasalize (i.e. if it is M, N, F, W, S, Y, KH, or SH) the obj.conc. is omitted, being indicated in speech by raising the tone of the preceding syllable. Sometimes -YA- is used for this concord before a consonant that does not nasalize; but I am not sure of the rules governing its use. Occasionally both forms are used in the same word, i.e. -YA- and nasalization.

e.g.
bamanya             they know me        or  bayamanya
kwasamba            it burnt me         or  kwayasamba

Note that in a verb stem starting with YI-, the Y usually represents H, and nasalized to MB:

e.g.
bambiniliye         they took it out for me (yinia)
mumbila heena?      where are you taking me? (yila)

When the subj.conc. is A-, it becomes YA- before a 1st sing.obj. concord.

e.g.
amulutsi            he saw her
but yandutsi        he saw me

When the 1st sing.obj.infix follows the 2nd sing.subj.pref. O- (= you), this changes to U-; i.e. when YOU do something to ME.

e.g.
umbooleele          you told me
ungoonyile          you visited me
ungalushiile        you have come back to me
umanyile orie?      how did you know me?
naumbeelesie shiina? what will you give me?

When the 1st sing.obj.conc. precedes a verb with a vowel stem it becomes -NZ-; it is also sometimes heard as -ND-, particularly before I, but it is probable that this form is dropping out of use; I have not yet found the rules governing its use. If the next syllable begins with N or M, the infix usually becomes -NY-, though this too is apparently becoming optional in most cases.

e.g.
yanzosiye           she washed me
yanzechesia         she is teaching me
yanzibiliile        she forgot me (or andibiliile)
yanzibaalila        she directs, teaches me (or andibaalila)
yanyimile           she refused me

Reflexive Object

The reflexive object, -self, is expressed by -I- :

e.g.
nzifwala (enz-i-fwala)      I dress myself
yeebisa (ya-i-bisa)         he is hiding (himself)
beelanga (ba-i-langa)       They call themselves...

Note that this is a reflexive objectival infix, and not a different form of the verb; thus the above are all still disyllabic (having two syllables), and form their near past tense in the ordinary way; e.g. NZIFWALIILIE, not NZIFWAALE.

Exercise 35
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. She anointed herself with oil.
2. She had a bath (lit. she bathed herself).
3. He calls himself a teacher.
4. They will help themselves.
5. They look after themselves well.
6. They hid themselves.
7. They tied themselves (up) with a rope.

Often the word for SELF (-ENE with the pron.conc.) is added, as in the following:

yeehaana omwene khumilimo       he gave himself to the work
beechamisia abeene              they pleased themselves
omuliango kwiyikaale okwene     the door has shut itself
inzokha yiluma eyene            the snake bites itself.

Very often the insertion of -ENE is optional; practice will show where it is most natural to insert it. The answers to the following exercises will be the more natural usage, but its insertion or omission will not make the answer wrong:

Exercise 35 (contd.)
8. He healed himself.
9. What will salt flavour itself with?
10. Don't trouble yourself.
11. The sheep arranged themselves in a line.
12. Ask yourself this question.
13. He bathed (himself) in the river.
14. A man (person) can't create himself.
15. The dog covered itself with mud.
16. You are deceiving yourself.
17. The child knows (how) to feed himself.

19. The cat licked itself.
20. Look at yourself!

-----oo0oo-----

VERBS WITH VOWEL STEMS - SUMMARY

The following is a summary of the rules we have already had relating to verbs with vowel stems:

i. If the vowel of the Subj. or Obj.Conc. or Tense Infix is the same as the initial vowel of the Stem immediately following, both are written (but see notes on Class 1 concords below):
ba-ahula            they separate           baahula
khu-ba-ahula        we separate them        khubaahula

ii. Vowel A in prefix or infix coalesces with the initial vowel of a verb stem as follows:
ba-eka              they learn              beeka
a-ba-ira            he kills them           abeera
ba-ora              they warm themselves    boora
ba-umbakha          they build              boombakha

iii. Vowel I in prefix or infix is always written:
bi-akuushe          they fell               biakuushe
a-chi-endile        he fetched them         achiendile

iv. Vowel U becomes semi-vowel W, except before another U:
khu-itsa            we are coming           khwitsa
khu-umbakha         we are building         khuumbakha
a-khu-echesiye      he taught us            akhwechesiye

But note the following concords for Class 1:
1st Pers.: EN- before a vowel stem becomes ENZ-, unless the next syllable begins with N or M, in which case it may be either ENY- or ENZ-, but usually the former. Sometimes END- is heard. Note that the -E is often dropped in speaking.

eka         learn               enzeka          I learn
aya         herd                enzaya          I herd
imba        sing                enyimba         I sing (sometimes enzimba)
ima         deny, refuse        enyima          I refuse
enya        want                enyenya         I want (but more commonly enzenya)

2nd Pers.: O- before a vowel stem becomes W- :
eka         learn               weka            you are learning
onoona      sin                 wonoona         you are sinning

3rd Pers.: A- before a vowel stem becomes YA-, the A- coalescing:
ula         arrive              yoola           he is arriving
enda        bring               yeenda          he is bringing

-----oo0oo-----

LOCATIVES

In English, phrases indicating a place are formed by using a preposition - e.g. on the table, in the house, under a chair. In Luyia we use Locative Prefixes, of which the following are the most commonly used:

KHU- may mean to, on, upon, about, from (on), etc.
e.g.
endeele khumeesa                I put (it) on the table.
atsiile khushiiro               he has gone to the market
emureebile khumwanawe           I asked him about his child
arulile khushitali              he got off the bed

MU- means in, within, from (inside), etc.
e.g.
ali munzu                       he is in the house
arulile munzu                   he came out from the house

HA- means at, the place of, etc.
e.g.
yéekhaale hango                 he stayed at home
arulile habulafu                he came out into the open

Note that when prefixing a Locative Prefix to a noun the preprefix is dropped. In Class 5 it is the real preprefix that is dropped (i.e. sing. I-, plur. TSI-), and not just the initial E-, although occasionally the TSI- is retained in the plural if it is necessary to distinguish between singular and plural. Also, in the singular of Class 3 the preprefix is dropped, and the prefix appears as I- and not LI-, except before a vowel stem, when the LI- is retained. It will be noticed that this class always seems to drop a letter more than the others (LI- as compared with OMU-, ABA-, etc.; and now MWI- instead of MULI-, as compared with MUMU-, etc.). This peculiarity is not confined to Oluluyia; e.g. in Swahili, where the Luyia OMU- is cut down to M-, ABA- to WA-, and EMI- to MI-, the LI- prefix has been dropped altogether, so that it has "kasha langu" for "my chest", with plural "makasha".

e.g.
                    Sing.               Plur.
on the tree         khumusaala          khumisaala
in the house        munzu               munzu (or mutsinzu)
in the cup          mushikombe          mubikombe
in the village      mwitaala            mumataala
in the boat         muliaro             mumaaro
on the board        khulubaho           khumbaho

(note specially the words underlined)

E- is used before proper names to indicate at, to, from, etc.; it is used after the possessive particle when used with a place name - i.e. we say "people of at-Kakamega", not just "people of Kakamega".

e.g.
basooma eMusanda                they read (i.e. go to school) at Musanda
yoombashile inzu eNamasoli      he built a house at Namasoli
abandu beButere                 people of Butere
amaluka keMumias                the dukas at Mumias

EWA-, the locative form of the possessive particle, is prefixed to common or proper nouns to indicate "at the place belonging to . . .", etc. The A coalesces with the initial vowel of the noun.

e.g.
yila ewomufundi                 take it to the fundi's (place)
batsiile ewaYonasani            they have gone to Jonathan's

EWA- prefixed to the possessive forms of the absolute pronoun (see p.15) is used without a following noun to correspond to the English "at our place", "home", etc. It is almost always used in the plural form, though a married woman would use the singular to indicate her own house, and a man the village of which he is head. Thus for a married woman EWEEFWE is her father's home, from which she came to be married. When the 3rd plur. form is followed directly by the name of a person it indicates "....'s home". The 2nd and 3rd sing. take the reduplicated forms EWUWO, EWUWE.

Abacheni baliho eweefwe.        There are visitors at our place.
Bakoniile ewabo Keya.           They slept at Keya's.
Batsia eweenywe bulaano.        They are going to your place now.
Omukhasi wOtero atsiile ewabo.  Otero's wife has gone to her old home.

ENA- means "somewhere round about", etc.
enakanisa                       somewhere at the church
enamurwe weshitali              somewhere about the head of the bed
eneesoomelo (= ena-i)           somewhere about the school

ENAMUSAATSAWE is used in speaking of a woman to explain that she is at her husband's home, in contrast to EWABWE, at her father's home.

BU- is another locative prefix, that occurs more frequently as part of a noun, particularly a place name, than in forming an adverbial phrase. There are however two common and useful expressions: EBULEBE = at such-and-such a place (LEBE can be used for "so-and-so"; ISAA INDEBE = at a given time; etc.); and one we have already had - EBUKHOOTSANYENE = at his mother's old home - literally, at his uncle's place; the uncles and other male members of the mother's family of course stay on in the clan lands.

Note that where the Poss.Part. precedes a Locative, there seems to be a tendency to use the initial vowel of the Poss.Part.; e.g. AKOMUSHITABO.

Exercise 37

-----oo0oo-----

There are also locative suffixes in use, as follows:
-KHWO, meaning on, up, to, about, from (on), etc.; it often precedes the KHU- locative prefix, although this usage is becoming less common.
e.g.
rakhwo                          put it (on) there
shilikhwo khumeesa              it is on the table (the -KHWO is often omitted)

-MWO, meaning in, within, inside, from (in), etc.; it often precedes the MU- locative prefix - but again this is becoming less common.
e.g.
alimwo                          he is inside
ramwo mwisanduku                put it in the box

-HO, -YO, meaning roughly here and there respectively. As the uses are not exactly parallel to those of the English words given, the student is advised to observe carefully when each is used, though he will not go far wrong in using them as suggested.
Attention has already been drawn to the use of -HO with the verb to be, corresponding to the English "there is". Note that the copula NI is never used with locatives
*aliho (never niho)             he is here
emunyoohleyo                    I found him there
amatoyi kaliho amanji           there is a lot of mud
* an old form YUHO, or UHO, may occasionally be heard for ALIHO.

The -KHWO and -MWO suffixes would quite often be translated into English by the use of THERE or HERE, as in the following:
rakhwo khumeesa                 put it there on the table.
yinjisiamwo mulwina             put it there, in the hole

Note that these suffixes are very often preceded by the Applied form of the verb, for which see the appropriate section.

NEGATIVE CONSTRUCTION

With verbs in the indicative mood (that is those that state a fact, as distinct from the imperative, which gives an order; the subjunctive, where the action is not an accomplished fact, but one merely thought of; the infinitive, which is the naming of the action, not saying it was done, etc. - e.g. Indic., I went; Imper., Go!; Subj., I may go; Infin., To go), the negative is formed by prefixing SHI- to the verb and adding the particle TA, or TAWE, at the end of the sentence or clause. The -RA- negative, already met with in the imperative (ORAKHOLA TA), will be dealt with later; it is used with other moods of the verb.

* shinima ta                I am not digging
  shiolima ta               you are not digging
  shialima ta               he is not digging
  shikhulima ta             we are not digging
  shimulima ta              you are not digging
  shibalima ta              they are not digging
* shinzitsa ta              I am not coming (now)
* shinenzitse ta            I shall not come
* shinzitsile ta            I did not come.

* Note that the initial E- of the 1st Sing. is dropped.

-----oo0oo-----

FAR PAST TENSE

This tense, which usually indicates a time more than, say, four or five days ago, is formed by the use of the tense infix -A- between the subj.conc. and the stem of the verb (or the obj.conc. if there is one). When the vowel of the subj.conc. is A, the two coalesce to form a short a; they are therefore not both written; i.e. BA-A-KHOLA = they did, is written BAKHOLA; I before A is written, and U becomes W, as before. In the 1st pers.sing., EN- becomes ND- before the -A- tense infix. With vowel stems coalescence takes place as previously explained.

            khola - do                      itsa - come
ndakhola            khwakhola           ndeetsa             khweetsa
wakhola             mwakhola            weetsa              mweetsa
yakhola             bakhola             yeetsa              beetsa
kwakhola            chiakhola           kweetsa             chieetsa
liakhola            kakhola             lieetsa             keetsa
shiakhola           biakhola            shieetsa            bieetsa
yakhola             tsiakholo           yeetsa              tsieetsa
lwakhola            tsiakholo           lweetsa             tsieetsa

Exercise 41

-----oo0oo-----

FAR FUTURE TENSE

This tense, which usually indicates a time a week or more ahead, is formed by using the tense infix -LI- between the subj.conc. and the stem of the verb (or the obj.conc. if any).

In the 1st person singular, EN- coming before -LI- should, of course, become ENDI-; and occasionally this form is heard; but a reduplicated form, NDILI-, has become much more common.

            khola - do                      itsa - come
ndilikhola          khulikhola          ndiliitsa           khuliitsa
olikhola            mulikhola           oliitsa             muliitsa
alikhola            balikhola           aliitsa             baliitsa
kulikhola           chilikhola          kuliitsa            chiliitsa
lilikhola           kalikhola           liliitsa            kaliitsa
                    etc.                                    etc.

5. He is very ill; he will not recover.
6. She did not remember to take in the maize.
7. He sat in the door-way.
8. They sat and talked in the house.
9. My arm will swell.
10. Your leg will ache.
11. The rain will not stop.
12. This cup will not break.
13. We did not visit them.
14. We are ill; we will not be able to come to your place.
15. My Father called me.

Remember that these two past tenses, or the two futures, convey entirely different time-ideas to the hearer. For instance, you might ask "Wakhola?" = "Did you do it?", and be given a negative answer; that would mean that the person asked did not do it in the period covered by the tense used - i.e. several days ago or longer; he may have done it since, but you did not ask that. Or if you ask "Nokalushe?" and receive a negative answer, it merely means that the one asked will not come back today or tomorrow. (See section on "Uses of '-KHWO' for the way of expressing "never".

N.B. IN ALL FOLLOWING EXERCISES USE THE FAR PAST AND FAR FUTURE, UNLESS ANOTHER TENSE IS INDICATED

-----oo0oo-----

THE ABSOLUTE PRONOUN

In many places where in English we would use a pronoun, in Luyia the same idea is conveyed by the use of a pronominal concord. Thus we say "I came", and "I" is a separate word; it can stand alone; e.g. "Who is there?" "I". The Luyia equivalent to "I came" is ENZITSILE; and EN- cannot be taken out of the word and stand by itself - it is not a word, only part of one. But occasions do arise when we need a pronoun that will stand alone, or can be combined with other words and concords in various ways. We have already met and supplied this need in forming the possessives. We had a possessive particle and wanted to attach it to a pronoun; and the pronouns we used were certain forms of the Absolute Pronoun. Of this pronoun Doke says that it is "that type of pronoun which, while indicating a certain noun (or person) does nothing further than indicate it, and in no way describes or limits it." Other words in the sentence may tell what is being done by or to the person or thing referred to, but the Absolute Pronoun merely indicates it. This will be clear from the examples following.

The forms of the Abs.Pron. given on p.15 are modified forms as used with the Poss.Part. The usual forms are as follows:

            Sing.                           Plur.
Cl.1    1st Pers.   isie    I, me           ifwe    we, us
        2nd Pers.   iwe     you (thou, thee) inywe  you (ye)
        3rd Pers.   ye      he, she, him, her bo    they, them

In other classes it is formed from the Pron.Conc. plus O:
Class   2   kwo     chio
        3   lio     ko
        4   shio    bio
        5   yo      tsio
        6   lwo     tsio

The following are the chief uses of the absolute pronoun:
(a) Alone, for emphasis:
Isie endi omulamu               I am well (whether others are or not)
Bo nibo abakhola                It was they who did it.
Ye ananjile                     He called me
Kwo nikwo                       It is it, quite definitely

(b) With the copula NI-, meaning it is, etc.
Niye wina?                      Who is he? (It is he, who?)
Nisie Wane                      I am so-and-so (It is I, so-and-so)
Ing'ombe niyo eyachisena        It was the cow that trampled on it
Eshitambaya eshio nishio eshiyia That cloth is new

(c) With NA- = and, meaning "and it", "as for it", "and then it", etc., followed by the Consecutive Construction (which will be dealt with later; it is formed with a NI- before the verb). Note that this use often indicates a change of subject, that is grammatically, as in "The boy was ill, and his father went to fetch medicine" - "Omusiani yalwala, naye samwana natsia okhwenda omusaala."

Nakwo omusaala nikukwa          And then the tree fell
Omwana naye niyeenya okhumulonda And the child (for his part) wanted to follow him
Nasi (=na-isie) endutsi mbu nobubii I also consider it bad. or As for me, I consider it bad.

(d) BA or LI verb stem, followed by NINA plus abs.pron., implying possession or close association - the same as "okhuba nende", p.39. In Class 1, 1st and 2nd sing., the forms are NINASI and NINAWE; the rest are formed regularly - NINAYE, NINEEFWE, etc.

Juma ali heena? Endi ninaye     Where is Juma? He's with me.
Ingokho yili heena? Endi ninayo. Where is the fowl? I've got it.
Bali ninako amabuyu amanji.     They have lots of eggs.

(e) Possessives are formed from the possessive particle agreeing with the thing possessed plus the absolute pronoun for the possessor. In Class 1 as we have seen the forms of the abs.pron. used are considerably modified. In other classes the possessives are quite regularly formed.

Omusaala nende amasaka kakwo    The tree and its branches
Omwana nende likondilie         The child and her sheep
Likondi nende omwana walio      The sheep and its child

Note again the construction - poss.part. agreeing with the thing possessed and immediately following it (omwana WAlio); abs.pron. agreeing with the possessor - -LIO agreeing with LIKONDI.

RELATIVE CONSTRUCTION

Direct relative construction, which in English is formed by the use of "which" or "who" - that is, where the relative is the subject of the verb - is in Luyia formed by the use of special Relative Concords, formed from the Pronominal Concord of the noun class with the Initial Vowel prefixed to it. It may be noted that some speakers do not seem to use the Initial Vowel, or to use it only occasionally; but it should always be used in the written form of the Relative Concord.

Class   Prefix.     Pron.Conc.      Rel.Conc.
1       omu-        u-              ou-
        aba-        ba-             aba-
2       omu-        ku-             oku-
        emi-        chi-            echi-
3       (e)li-      li-             eli-
        ama-        ka-             aka-
4       eshi-       shi-            eshi-
        ebi-        bi-             ebi-
5       (e)i-       yi-             eyi-
        (e)tsi-     tsi-            etsi-
6       olu-        lu-             olu-
        (e)tsi-     tsi-            etsi-

e.g.
Omundu ousinjiile               The person who is standing
Imbusi eyilwalile               The goat that was ill
Nisie ouliitsa                  It is I who will come
Amachina akakwile               The stones that fell
Ebindu ebiakora                 The things that were lost

Note that in the Near Future, where the Tense Prefix NA- precedes the subj.conc., the relative concord occurs twice; in its full form before the NA, and without the initial vowel after the NA; except in the sing. of the personal class (Class 1), the letter A precedes the NA.

nisie ounotsie                  it is I who shall go
niwe ounotsie                   it is you who will go
niye ounotsie                   it is he who will go
nifwe abanabatsie               it is we who shall go
ninywe abanabatsie              it is you who will go
nibo abanabatsie                it is they who will go

nishio eshianashitsie           it is it that will go       (eshindu)
niyo eyanayitsie                do.                         (ingokho)
nikwo okwanakutsie              do.                         (omupira)
nilio elianalitsie              do.                         (likondi)

Indirect Relative Construction, which in English is formed by the use of WHOM and WHOSE - i.e. in objectival and possessive relationship - is formed in Luyia by the use of the possessive particle, usually with the initial vowel. This is written separately from the following word; that is, when the relative is the subject of the verb it is joined to it; when it is not, it is written separately, so that it does not obscure the subject.

e.g.
Omusaatsa owa ndalola           The man whom I saw
Omukhasi owa omwanawe yafwa     The woman whose child died
Omusaala okwa lisaka liakwo likwile The tree whose branch fell
Ing'ombe eya beera khushiiro    The cow that they killed in the market
Ing'ombe eyabeera khushiiro     The cow that killed them in the market

(In the last two there is also a difference in tone, but we do not indicate this in any way in writing.)

Note the following construction, which appears to resemble a relative, but is really the use of the older 3rd sing.subj.concord U instead of the more usual A; it seems to be used mainly in answering a question, or where in some way the person has just been referred to. It usually seems to be used to say something that is not what was expected, or that has not been allowed for.

e.g.
Yohana ashili okhwitsa?         Hasn't John come yet?
Wakheetsa bulaaho.              He has just come now.
Omwana aliho noho?              Is the child there?
Winyooshe.                      He has gone home.
Shimulutsi Linani ubeele namatsayi khumunwa? Don't you see that the Linani has blood on his mouth?
(From a story, where the ground squirrel is being wrongly accused of killing a sheep which the Linani has killed).

-----oo0oo-----

"RA" NEGATIVE

With certain forms of the verb, instead of the usual SHI negative, RA is inserted between the subj.conc. and the tense infix. This has already been seen in the negative imperative; it is also used with the relative, both direct and indirect. Note that it immediately precedes the tense infix.

omwana ouraasooma khale ta      the child who did not read long ago
omundu ourasunjile ta           the person who did not speak
omundu owa ndaraalola ta        the person whom I did not see
  (or owa endaalola ta)
omukhasi owa abaanabe bareetsile ta the woman whose children did not come.

The RA negative is used with other forms of the verb as shown in the examples below; these forms of the verb will be dealt with in later sections:

Conditional
noba norakhola ta ....          if you don't do it ....

Subjunctive
mbu arakhole ta ....            that he should not do it ....

Consecutive
nibarakhola ta ....             and they did not do it ....

After SHINGA OLWA
shinga olwa arakholiile ta ..   that he had not done it ....

Negative Infinitive
oburakhola ta ....              not to do, not doing, without doing

Participial
narakholiile ta ....            not having done

"Not yet" Forms
Arashiakhola ta                 He has not yet done it
Beetsile nibarashiakhola ta     They came before doing it

With the 1st sing. the form should of course be ENDA- (=EN-RA); but as with the far future the reduplicated form NDARA- is becoming more common.

.... eshia endaakula ta )
.... eshia ndaraakula ta )      which I did not buy.

7. The man who did not marry more than one wife is a chief.
8. That is the pot (lit., that pot, it is it) that she did not make.
9. The child whose cup did not break drank milk.
10. The boy who is not laughing is not happy.
11. The woman who does not open her book does not know (use far past) how to read.
12. The lamp that you did not put out burnt all night (all, with day or night, is TULU).
13. The person who was not called to the feast is annoyed.
14. The man who did not help me will not get any wages.

-----oo0oo-----

QUESTIONS

In English questions are usually indicated by changing the order or form of words (e.g. He is ill. Is he ill? - They came last night. Did they come last night?) This is never done in Luyia. In asking a question the word order remains unchanged, but there is a change in tone; the sentence starts on a higher tone, or sometimes gradually rises to the higher tone just before the last syllable, which is usually spoken with a falling inflection. Frequently, particularly where there is any doubt as to whether the sentence is a statement or a question, the word NOHO? or NOHOMBA? is placed at the end; this word in other positions is translated as "or"; its use in the question is to imply that the statement made is being queried.
OLI OMULAMU = You are well.  OLI OMULAMU? = Are you well?  Or one may ask OLI OMULAMU NOHO? = You are well - or (aren't you?).

e.g.
Alwala? or Alwala noho?         Is he ill?
Otsia bulaano? or Otsia bulaano noho? Are you going now?
Nibo abanababache ing'ombe? or  Is it they who will
Nibo abanababache ing'ombe noho? skin the cow?

There are two kinds of questions asked with the negative:
1. An ordinary negative statement, with the tone used alone indicating that a question is asked; this implies that a negative answer is expected.
Shionyoolile ta?                Didn't you get it?

Note that the answer will actually be EE = Yes; i.e. agreeing with your negative statement.

2. A statement using the SHI- but usually without the final TA, implying that an answer in the affirmative is expected; sometimes it is introduced by NE. This type of question is usually a somewhat indignant repudiation of some accusation that has been made or implied, or something of the kind.

Eshitabo shianje shili heena?   Where is my book?
Shioli iwe omwene oubukuule?    Isn't it you yourself who took it?

Otsia heena?                    Where are you going?
Ne shinzia ingo?                Aren't I just going home?

Shikholiile ta?                 Didn't I do it?
(implying "Why, I must have forgotten!")
Ne shikholiile?                 Didn't I do it?
(implying "Why are you asking about that? Haven't I already done it?")

It is sometimes followed by NIINA - SHIKHOLILE NIINA? - but that sometimes has a rather petulant and even an insolent sound, so it should not generally be used.

Exercise 48
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. Have you finished?
2. Did you drink your medicine?
3. Will you come tomorrow?
4. Didn't you buy (any) meat?
5. Where is my cup? - Isn't it on the table?
6. Isn't my cup on the table?
7. Did they tell you to go home?
7. Sweep the room. - Haven't I swept (it)?
8. Will you come to our place tomorrow?
9. Won't you come back tomorrow?
10. Haven't you gone to the market today?
11. Have you read that letter?
12. Do you know (how) to read?
13. Don't you know (how) to read?
14. Do you want to go?

-----oo0oo-----
SUBJUNCTIVE

The subjunctive is formed from the stem of the verb with the final -A changed to -E, and with the appropriate subj.conc.:

ekhole          that I may do, let me do, may I do?, etc.
okhole          that you may do ....
akhole          that he may do ....
khukhole        that we may do ....
mukhole         that you may do ....
bakhole         that they may do ....
                etc.

The subjunctive is used in the following ways:

i. As a more polite form of the imperative:
Yitsa!          Come here!          Witse.          Please come.
Lekha!          Stop!               Oleshe.         Please stop.

ii. When two imperatives are joined by "and" in English, in Luyia the second should be in the subjunctive:
Tsia oleere eshitabo.               Go and bring the book.
Buukha ochende.                     Stand up and walk.

iii. In asking or giving permission - May I? Am I to ...? Let us ... etc.
Embukule?       Am I to take it?    May I take it?
Alole?          Is he to see it?    May he see it?
Otsie.          You may go.
Khutsie.        Let us go.
Khulaame.       Let us pray.

iv. Where the purpose of an action is expressed in English by to, and, in order that, etc.:
Yeetsile akhukhoonye.               He has come to help us.
Nenzie endole.                      I shall go and see.
Leera khulie.                       Bring it and let us eat.

The conjunction MBU (= that, etc.) is often used with the subjunctive; it could be used in each of the three examples immediately above; and as in the following:
Aleerile ingokho mbu khukule.       He brought a fowl for us to buy (that we should buy).
Yeenya mbu otsie.                   He wants you to go.

Note that MBU is also used to introduce direct or indirect narration, or a list, or in other places where in English a colon might be used - and in many places where it would not.

Exercise 49
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. Please go to the market.
2. Please cook this fowl.
3. Please bring that hoe over there.
4. May I see?
5. Yes, you may see.
6. May they enter now?
7. Yes, they may enter.
8. Are we to wait?
9. Yes, you may wait.
10. Are we to go and bring the maize?
11. Yes, go and bring it.
12. They have come to see the child.
13. They want you to visit them.
14. He saved up his money to buy books.
15. We stopped speaking that we might hear you.
16. Taste and see.
17. Come back and eat.
18. He travelled far that he might visit his friends.
19. You may open your eyes now.
20. Please make tea.
21. I came to bring you some eggs.

Note that the use of the subjunctive is the correct way of making a polite request, and so corresponds to the English "Please". Many people who have learnt English (and English people who are not very familiar with Luyia) try to use CHAMA (= to please) in this way; but that is a foreign usage, and is unnecessary.

-----oo0oo-----

INTERMEDIATE PAST TENSE

This tense, which usually indicates a time roughly more than a day and less than a week ago is formed from the subj.conc. - A - stem with final -A changed to -ILE. Note that the rules relating to palatalization and to polysyllabic stems apply as shown in the notes on the Near Past tense.

This tense, like the Near Past, may often be translated by the English perfect, indicating an action begun or performed in the past, but of which the results or the resulting state still exists.

            khola - do
ndakholile          khwakholile
wakholile           mwakholile
yakholile           bakholile
kwakholile          chiakholile
liakholile          kakholile
                    etc.

-----oo0oo-----

INTERMEDIATE FUTURE TENSE

This usually indicates a time roughly more than a day and less than a week ahead; it is formed from the subj.conc. - AKHA - stem with final -A changed to -E.

            khola - do
ndakhakhole         khwakhakhole
wakhakhole          mwakhakhole
yakhakhole          bakhakhole
kwakhakhole         chiakhakhole
liakhakhole         kakhakhole
                    etc.

-----oo0oo-----

VERB "TO BE"

The various tenses of the verb "to be" (and therefore of "to have") are formed quite regularly. There are two points to be kept in mind:

i. There are two stems used for this verb, BA and LI. The LI stem is used in the Present and Far Past tenses, in the Consecutive construction with NI-, and in the Participial mood. The BA stem is used in most other cases.

ii. In the Near and Intermediate Past tenses the -A of the BA stem, instead of being elided, coalesces with the -I- of the -ILE suffix to form -EELE. This also occurs in some other monosyllabic verb stems, e.g. HA, HWA, NYWA, RA.

Far Past    Int.Past    Near Past   Present Near Fut.   Int.Future  Far Fut.
ndali       ndabeele    embeele     endi    nembe       ndakhabe    ndiliba
wali        wabeele     obeele      oli     nobe        wakhabe     oliba
yali        yabeele     abeele      ali     nabe        yakhabe     aliba
khwali      khwabeele   khubeele    khuli   nakhube     khwakhabe   khuliba
mwali       mwabeele    mubeele     muli    namube      mwakhabe    muliba
bali        babeele     babeele     bali    nababe      bakhabe     baliba
kwali       kwabeele    kubeele     kuli    nakube      kwakhabe    kuliba
chiali      chiabeele   chibeele    chili   nachibe     chiakhabe   chiliba
liali       liabeele    libeele     lili    nalibe      liakhabe    liliba
                                    etc.

14. I shall be hungry.
15. I had three small stools which my mother gave me.
16. They were good boys; I know they will do the work well.
17. He had ten goats and seven cows.
18. Did they have a good teacher?
19. Go and bring me the book that you had in the house.
20. I think he will be the chief of that country.

-----oo0oo-----

CONTINUOUS or HABITUAL MANNER

This is not a separate tense, but it indicates the manner in which an action is performed in any tense. It can mean the same as the English continuous; i.e. ACHENDANGA can mean HE IS NOW WALKING; but it more usually indicates an action that is habitual - i.e. ACHENDANGA, when used of a child, would mean HE KNOWS HOW TO WALK; of a man it would probably mean HE TRAVELS. ASOOMANGA may mean HE IS AT THIS MOMENT READING A BOOK, but it more frequently means HE GOES TO SCHOOL, or HE KNOWS HOW TO READ. These two meanings seem to be differently distributed in the different dialects; in one the more common meaning may be the continuous and in another the habitual; but it seems that with the majority of the Abaluyia the habitual sense is more common. Note that in English the simple present tense usually gives this idea of habitual action, and should generally be used to translate the habitual form of the Luyia verb; thus:

Habitual                            Present
enjendanga      I walk              enjenda         I am walking (now)
akonanga        he sleeps           akona           he is asleep
khulimanga      we dig              khulima         we are digging
batsiitsanga    they go             batsia          they are going

Khulimanga eweefu khatsotso; ne bulaano khulima omukunda kwomusoomesia.
We dig at home every day; but now we are digging the teacher's garden.

Batsiitsanga khushiiro khuLwakhane; ne leelo batsia okhukoonya omukhaana wabwe.
They go to the market on Thursdays; but today they are going to visit their sister.

For practical purposes it may be taken that the Habitual or Continuous manner is formed by inserting -ANG- after the final consonant, the final vowel or vowels (including semi-vowels) being those of the particular tense or form used. (Actually it is formed by suffixing -NGA to the verb; but the rather complicated series of assimilations etc. that occur with certain forms makes it simpler for beginners to use the above rule.)

Exercise 52
Translate into English:
1. Abaana balia basoomanga eBusakami.
2. Khukulanga inyama khushiiro.
3. Beendanga amatsi ehale.
4. Ateekha ichai mbu khunywe.
5. Omukhaana ulia alela omwana; ne omusiani uno niye oumulelanga khatsotso.
6. Omukhasi uno alukhanga bulayi.
7. Alanga omwanawe.
8. Akhulanganga mbu abaanabe.
9. Akhoonyanga omusiani wabwe.
10. Papa asooma ibarwa.
11. Afulukanga obusela mutsuli.
12. Emukoonyanga omwitsa wanje ewabwe.
13. Khukhung'ananga mukanisa buli Muhongo.
14. Omurwe kukhomaka; tsimoni tsinzunanga.
15. Babohanga tsing'ombe mwitaala.
16. Tsingwe tsiiranga amakondi.
17. Papa, yitsa; ingwe yilia likondi.
18. Abuukhanga khusaa ndala.

Habitual of Verbs with Monosyllabic Stems

With verb stems of only one syllable the usual Habitual ending is -ITSANGA; in some cases the -I- coalesces with the -A of the stem to form EE:

ba          be                  beetsanga
ha          give                heetsanga
hwa         finish              hweetsanga
nywa        drink               nyweetsanga
khwa        pay dowry           khweetsanga
ra          put                 reetsanga
sia         grind               sieetsanga

In other cases the -A is elided (dropped):
fwa         die                 fwitsanga
kwa         fall                kwitsanga
lia         eat                 liitsanga
ria         fear                riitsanga
yia         get hot             yiitsanga
tsia        go                  tsiitsanga

Exercise 53
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. He is always ill. (Can you express this in two different ways?)
2. I grind the maize; my sister makes the obusuma.
3. I am grinding my grandmother's millet.
4. Water gets hot quickly on the cooking stones.
5. We eat obusuma; you eat bread.
6. He doesn't come to read; he is afraid of the teacher.
7. Isaya is engaged to (= paying dowry for) my cousin.
8. We are eating obusuma and meat.
9. Many children die every year.
10. Drink a lot of water every day.
11. The milk gets finished up quickly.
12. Those teachers don't drink beer.
13. John always puts his shoes under the bed.
14. We are going to the market.
15. My sister goes to the market every Thursday.
16. I am going to the shops to buy some sugar.
17. The boys are there every day.
18. Drunk men fall easily.

Habitual with Tenses other than the Present

This indicates an action that was or will be habitual at some other time; it is formed in the same way, as will be seen in the following examples:

Ndahulilanga ako khale.             I used to hear about that long ago.
Khwamuhandiichilanga tsibarwa tsinyinji. We used to write him lots of letters.
Ndilikhukoonyanga buli mwesi.       I will visit you every month.

Certain phonetic changes take place in some words when they are put into the Habitual form. We have already seen that adjacent letters may affect each other; e.g. when two vowels come together one may be dropped (ELISION), or the two may join together to make a new sound (COALESCENCE); an I or E can change the pronunciation of certain consonants (PALATALIZATION), and so can an N (NASALIZATION). There is still another process by which two sounds coming close together (not necessarily side by side) tend to become similar or identical. This is called ASSIMILATION. When it is the first of the two sounds that affects the following one, it is called Progressive Assimilation; when it is the second sound that affects the preceding one, it is called Regressive Assimilation.

In the forms of the Habitual that we have so far considered, the final vowel of the verb stem has been -A, the same as the vowel of the -ANG- infix. But when the final vowel of the simple form of the verb is other than -A, regressive assimilation takes place, the -A- of the -ANG- of our rule being assimilated to that final vowel. This final vowel is usually -E, which would make the ending -ENGE; but the front vowel E palatalizes the NG to NJ, so that the ending is -ENJE. With two stems, the LI stem of the verb "to be" and the LUTSI stem of the verb "to see", the final vowel is -I, giving an ending -INJI.

(As was stated previously, our rule is a practical one that does not follow the original course of the changes involved. For those who are interested in the historical changes, it was really a progressive assimilation, the -A of the suffix -NGA being assimilated to the final vowel of the verb stem preceding it.)

Int.Past        Near Past       Near Fut.       Int.Fut.        Subjunct.
ndakholilenje   ekholilenje     nekholenje      ndakhakholenje  ekholenje
wakholilenje    okholilenje     nokholenje      wakhakholenje   okholenje
yakholilenje    akholilenje     nakholenje      yakhakholenje   akholenje
                                etc.

-li:            ndalinji, walinji, yalinji, etc.
-lutsi:         ndalutsinji, walutsinji, yalutsinji, etc.

Exercise 54
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. I have been looking for that book all day (day-time = eshiteere).
2. She grinds on a grinding stone.
3. They will be waiting for us tomorrow.
4. She has been working here two days.
5. The children are afraid of leopards.
6. He used to pass here every day.
7. They eat at home, but they sleep at the old woman's place.
8. He will be reading in Standard 4 next year (= the year which is coming).
9. We used to live in (= e) Butsotso.
10. The boy who is ill has been vomiting a lot.
11. People have been passing here all day.
12. He is always here.
13. He used to be our teacher.
14. I will help you once every week.
15. I used to see him (a few days ago).

-----oo0oo-----

PASSIVE

The passive (that is, when the subject of the sentence is not the doer of the action, but the one acted upon) is usually formed by inserting -W- before the final vowel of the verb:

alangwa                             he is being called
alanjilwe                           he was called (or invited)
alangwe                             he may be called
ingwe yeerwa                        the leopard was killed
emilimo chiakholilwe                the work was done
yalutswi                            he was seen

Listen carefully for the occasions when the passive is used. You will find that very often where a passive would be used in English a Luyia speaker will reverse the sentence and use the active voice; e.g. where we might say "That dress was made by my mother" it would be more natural in Luyia to say "My mother made that dress".

Note that the noun indicating the agent may follow immediately after a passive verb, though usually it is preceded by NENDE (= by, with); the insertion of NENDE is a more modern use, and its omission is now most common in idiomatic expressions:

yalumwa inzala                      he was pained (by) hunger, i.e. he was hungry.
yatiilwa oburima                    he was seized (by) anger, i.e. he was angry.

Exercise 55
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. She has a pain in her stomach (lit. she is pained the stomach)
2. He was falsely accused by the teacher.
3. The child was born yesterday.
4. The cow was tied with a big rope to the tree.
5. The things will be brought tomorrow.
6. He was ordered to return the books quickly.
7. This book isn't liked by many people.
8. The work was done quickly.
9. The child was carried by his mother.
10. All the shoes were thrown (away).
11. This house was built by the carpenter who made my table.
12. He was heard by the children.
13. These eggs which I have brought will be bought tomorrow by the chief's wife.
14. The sheep that was lost was seen on the road yesterday.
15. The sheep is being skinned now.
16. The boys are wanted.
17. I was told that you wanted me.
18. The words were read in the church.

-----oo0oo-----

In most verbs of one syllable a passive ending -IBWA is added; and A - IBWA gives -EEBWA:

ha          gives   heebwa          be given
khwa        "       khweebwa        be engaged (of the girl)
nywa        "       nyweebwa        be drunk
ra          "       reebwa          be put
sia         "       sieebwa         be ground

(SIA is not monosyllabic, but it is treated in the same way as the others given.)

In LIA and RIA, the A is elided, giving LIIBWA, RIIBWA.

Causative forms (which will be fully dealt with later) form the passive by inserting -BW- instead of -W- before the final -A:
yahonibwa                           he was healed (caused to recover)
biasitibwa                          they were moved

Exercise 56
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. The lamps are being put out.
2. The children were taken out from (lit.in) the house.
3. I was shown the path to (lit.of) Musanda.
4. All the maize will be eaten.
5. I was given a dress.
6. This medicine may be drunk three times every day.
7. The millet was ground so that we might eat obusuma.
8. The books were put on the table.
9. We were asked a lot of questions.
10. She was engaged to a man from Bunyole (Note: the whole of the phrase underlined is represented by locative E).
11. Let them be given some food.
12. The matter was arranged long ago.
13. The cups may be put on the table.
14. The plans (= words) were changed.

Habitual with Passive

The habitual manner of a passive form may be formed according to the rule already given by inserting -ANG- after the final consonant; thus KHOLWA would give KHOLANGWA; a regressive assimilation takes place, the semi-vowel W following the consonant affecting the vowel which precedes it, changing the A to U, and giving the final form KHOLUNGWA.

omwana yalindungwa bulayi           the child was always well looked after.
obusuma bwafukungwa munyungu yino   obusuma used to be made in this pot.
arangalungwa muno                   he is carefully supervised
alikoonyungwa khatsotso             he will be visited regularly

(The origin of this form was probably as follows: KHOLWA with the suffix -NGA would give KHOLWANGA, a form still in use in some areas; as this would not be very obviously a passive, a second -W- would tend to be inserted before the final vowel, giving KHOLWANGWA; a regressive assimilation would give KHOLWUNGWA, and coalescence between the W and the U gives the final form.)

Exercise 57
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. These books are read everywhere (= hosi hosi).
2. Maize is planted every year.
3. Food is kept (i.e. put away) in the granary (= eshiachi).
4. Sacrifices are offered frequently.
5. Food is tasted by the person who cooks it.
6. Rubbish is thrown (away).
7. The door is always fastened at night.
8. The maize is being harvested now.
9. We used to be helped by the school-children.
10. The cups used to be put on the table.
11. Leopards are very much feared.
12. Quail are eaten, but crows are not.
13. Obusuma is cooked in a cooking-pot.
14. The boys are taught to read.

-----oo0oo-----

THE APPLIED FORM

"The applied form of the verb is used to indicate the action when applied on behalf of, towards, or with regard to, some object." (Doke.) As this is often indicated in English by the use of a preposition this has often been called the "Prepositional Form"; but Applied is preferable. It is formed by inserting -IR- or -ER- between the final consonant and the following vowel or semi-vowel of the verb. When the preceding vowel (in the stem of the verb) is A, I, or U, the infix is -IR-; when it is O or E, the infix is -ER-; thus:

        A           E           I           O           U
        IR                      IR                      IR
                    ER                      ER

kaba        divide              kabila          divide between, share
hela        be enough           helela          be enough for
yila        take                yilila          take to, or take for
boola       speak               boolela         speak to, tell
kula        buy                 kulila          buy for

It will be noticed that this form can make an Intransitive verb (one that does not take an object), Transitive (i.e., that it will take an object); and it can enable a transitive verb to take two objects; therefore it often takes an objective infix:

yaboola                             he spoke
yakhuboolela                        he told us
ndakula ingubo                      I bought a dress
ndamukulila ingubo                  I bought her a dress
yakasia ebindu                      he arranged the things
yangasilia ebindu                   he arranged the things for me
yakhusoomela eshitabo               he read the book to us
yamutsiila samwana                  he went for (or to) his father

Exercise 58
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. I got him a knife.
2. She looked after the children for us.
3. He went back to them.
4. They grumbled about the food.
5. The children brought me some eggs.
6. The woman made tea for us.
7. He read the letter to us.
8. I was surprised at the things which I saw.
9. He opened the door for us.
10. We sang songs for them.
11. I lit the lamp for him.
12. I gave the things back to him.
13. He took a stone out of my shoe (lit. He took out for me a stone in the shoe.)
14. He fed my dogs for me yesterday.
15. He works for me.
16. Come and visit us tomorrow; we will make tea for you (s.).
17. Put this money away for me.
18. Pick me some fruit.
19. She made obusela for the children.
20. Pray for me.

The following points should also be noted with regard to the Applied form:

i. It is sometimes (but not always) used with a locative suffix or phrase to indicate the place where the action occurred, or where it habitually occurs:
ndakholelayo emilimo                I worked there
akholelanga munzu                   he does it in the house
inzu eya okhuteeshelamwo            kitchen

And read again the note on p.11, note (b), regarding LI- nouns formed from the applied form of the verb.

ii. It is similarly used to indicate time:
omuyika okwa ndeebulilwamwo         the year in which I was born

iii. With some verbs of motion (e.g. ilukha, pulukha, tsia) it conveys the idea of motion towards; the simple stem of the verb may convey the idea of motion from:
ilukha                              run; run away from
ilushila                            run to

iv. The infinitive of the applied form following a possessive particle can be used to indicate the purpose for which a thing is used; note that in this case the poss.part. usually has the initial vowel, as shown:
omubano okwokhukhalachila           a knife for cutting with

v. The applied form followed by ELALA is used to indicate that the action is complete and final:
yabahesilia elala                   he destroyed them utterly
ndabamalila elala                   I finished them all up

vi. There can be a double applied form, as in the following:
yafwa                               he died
yakhufwila                          he died for us
yakhufwililayo                      he died for us there

vii. Note the use of the passive of the applied form:
yaleerelwa ibarwa                   he was brought a letter
omwanawe yamufwila                  her child died (on her!)
yafwirwa omwanawe                   she was bereft of her child

viii. Some verbs which are always intransitive in English may be used transitively in Luyia - i.e. they may take an object; therefore the English preposition is not represented by an applied form:
khulakhulinda                       we will wait (for) you (the applied form is used for minding ... on behalf of someone else.)
bamutsekha                          they laughed at him (the applied, TSESHELA, implies definitely derisive, objectionable laughter.)
