Translate into Oluluyia:
1. This is the sewing room.
2. That duka is the one where (= in which) I bought the plates.
3. This is a book to learn reading from (= in which to learn to read).
4. He went to his father.
5. He ran away from the leopard, but the others ran towards it.
6. A pencil is a thing for writing with.
7. He was completely lost.
8. Shut the door properly (completely).
9. They will be completely satisfied (i.e. with food).
10. Spread out a mat for me there.
11. He had a letter written to him.
12. She had a dress made for her.
13. They did not wait for me.
14. She looked after the children for me.
15. The children laughed at the things they were told.
16. The boys are going home; they will untie the cow for you in the morning.
17. Please put out that lamp over there for me.
18. Please make me some obusuma.
19. I sinned against my brother.
20. Yesterday I sinned against my brother.

-----oo0oo-----

"NI" FORMS OF THE VERB

There are several forms of the verb introduced by NI-. In many cases it is very difficult to distinguish between the various uses; in fact to the Omuluyia there is often no definite dividing line; one idea merges into the other, and often the two exist alongside each other. This should be borne in mind in considering the following.

The Consecutive Construction

This has been called the Narrative Tense; but as it can refer to future as well as past time the term Consecutive seems preferable; and in itself it has no particular time significance, so it is not a tense. It is formed from NI - Subj.Conc. - Stem of verb; and it indicates a series of actions continuing in the tense already shown by the preceding verb.

The six examples following are taken from stories by an Omuluyia writer:

Omukhaana yabiikha ebindu, niyee-kala omuliango natsia mushisi-kashie.
The girl put the things away, and shut the door, and went to her room.

Omusiani yachesia ario tsing'ombe, natsisinjisia mubiko biatsio, niyeenjila munzu, nibamuheelesia obusuma.
The boy drove the cows, and put them into their sheds, and went into the house, and they gave him obusuma.

Isie ndakhachese nimbakala nimbiikha mushiachi.
I shall harvest, and spread it out to dry, and put it away in the granary.

Yeelukha tsimbilo okhulibukanila, nalishiesia natsatsahala, nabukula indayi okhuchinga nayila munzu, nalimanyia eshisaala eshiokhwikhalakhwo.
She ran quickly to meet it, and greeted it joyfully, and took its bag to carry and took it into the house, and showed it a stool to sit on.

It appears that sometimes this construction is used with the habitual ending -ANGA, as in the following examples; it might sometimes be possible to explain these as Participial (see next section), but certainly not always:

Yakholanga ario nachamisinjia abaana.
He used to do that and please the children (or pleasing ...)

Omukhaana yateekhanga ebiliibwa, nayenga amalwa, nafulukanga obusela, nateekha amakhanga, nafuka obusuma nabiishila linani.
The girl used to cook the food, and brew the beer, and make the gruel, and cook the guinea-fowls, and make the obusuma, and put it away for the dragon.

Note: This construction is used mainly when the subject of successive clauses is the same; but it will be noticed that in the second example given the last clause has a different subject. But I believe it is correct to say that the consecutive construction cannot be used with a subject that has not already been mentioned.

Exercise 60
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. He came into the house and sat down and started to eat.
2. She lit the lamp and put it on the table.
3. I shall get up early tomorrow and do my work and go to visit the teacher.
4. Yesterday he wrote us a letter and said that he wants to go to Nairobi.
5. The boy climbed on to the roof, and fell and broke his leg.
6. The women will dig, and plant, and weed, and harvest.
7. He will recover and go back to his work.
8. The children sang songs and played games and got thoroughly tired.
9. She went there, and looked, and found four eggs, and brought them home, and gave them to her mother.
10. I bought a book and read it and gave it to my brother.
11. The train goes to Kisumu and comes back again three times every day.
12. I swept the house, and cooked the food, and looked after the children.

Participial Mood

There are several somewhat differing uses of a NI construction with different tenses of the verb which may be included under the above heading. The following are some possible divisions:

i. Akin to the English use of the Present Participle, used with the Present or Present Habitual of the verb:
Beetsa nibachinganga ebimwelo     They came carrying baskets
Achenda niyeemba                    He is walking along singing
Beekhaale nibasungana               They sat talking to each other

ii. Similar to (i), but with Near Past, when it may be translated by an English adverb or adverbial phrase:
Yatsia nasinyiishe okhushila        He went very angrily
Beekhala nibahooleelile             They sat in silence
Yakhola nalutsi                     He did it deliberately (lit. seeing)

iii. Similar to (i), but with other tenses of the verb; often used after such verbs as NYOOLA (find), LOLA (see), LEKHA (leave):
Yanyoola abaana nibakhulile         He found the children grown up
Emuleshile niyakhamala okhulia      I left him just finished eating
Ndabanyoola nibatsiile              I found them gone

iv. Equivalent to an English adverbial clause of time:
Nemurume niyeetsile                 I shall send him when he comes (lit., when he has come)
Akhulachile okhukhola nikhwakhachelela ingo She told us to do it as soon as we returned home

v. Similar to (iv), but usually with a NI form of the verb "to be" as auxiliary, to indicate a condition:
Noba nomulola, omuheelesie eshitabo shino. If you see him, give him this book.
Nikhuba nikhutsia, khulichelela khandi. If we go we will come back again.
Naba nararunga ta, alibohwa.        If he doesn't pay, he will be imprisoned.

vi. Following the verb "to be", where it can be used to form a very large variety of compound tenses:
Yali natsiile                       He had gone (i.e., long ago he had already been gone some little time)
Yabeele natsiile                    He had gone (i.e., a few days ago, he had already been gone some time)
Yali niyakhatsia                    He had gone (long ago he had just gone)
Yali natsiitsanga                   He used to go (long ago)
Yabeele natsiitsanga                He used to go (quite recently)
Nabe natsiile                       He will have gone (by this evening)
Aliba natsiile                      He will have gone (at some future date)
Embeetsanga nindwala                I am continually being ill

Negative
With all these NI forms of the verb, the -RA- negative is used.
Beetsa nibaramanya likhuwa ta.      They came knowing nothing.
Ndamunyoola narabuushile ta.        I found him not up.
Naba naraliho ta, okalushe.         If he is not there, you may come back.

Exercise 61
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. They came running.
2. He did (it) cheerfully (i.e. being happy).
3. She walks (about) coughing.
4. He sat quietly.
5. I found the herd-boy missing.
6. I met her walking along (lit. on) the road.
7. The teacher found the water-pot broken.
8. We shall see them tomorrow when they have finished.
9. We sat there and talked while they were cooking.
10. When there is enough milk (i.e. when the milk is enough), bring it here.
11. The baby lay on the bed, crying.
12. I left her sitting by the fire-place.
13. We will come when we have finished eating.
14. If you want this book, you may take it.
15. He is in school now; he will be able to help you when the children have gone home.
16. If you are ill, don't come tomorrow.
17. We will sit and talk when the cows have been tied up.
18. If the water is boiling, we will make the tea now.
19. If my aunt gives me a fowl, I shall have fourteen.
20. If he has no money he will not be able to buy clothes.
21. He used to come to visit us.
22. If you don't milk the cows we will not have any milk. (shela = to milk).

-----oo0oo-----

IMMEDIATE PAST

There is still another simple past tense, indicating something that has just happened - the Immediate Past. It is formed from the Subj.Conc. - AKHA - Verb stem; that is, it differs in form from the Intermediate Future only in the final vowel, which in the Immediate Past form does not change. This tense is only used when there is a stress on the fact that the action has taken place just now; e.g. in the first example a person would normally announce his arrival by "Enzitsile"; the -AKHA- form emphasizes the fact that he was not there a short time previously.

Ndakheetsa bulaano.                 I have just come now.
Amatsi kakhapapachila.              The water has just started to boil.
Yakhabaho bulaano.                  He was here just now.

Exercise 62
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. I have just finished cooking the food.
2. She has just arrived now.
3. The letters have just gone.
4. The children have just gone into school.
5. I have just seen her passing.
6. We have only just heard the news now.
7. The fowl has just been killed now.
8. He has just told me now that he cannot come.
8. It is my cow; I bought it just now.
9. I arrived there when she had just finished her work.
10. Write the letter as soon as you have heard any news.

-----oo0oo-----

"ABOUT TO" COMPOUND TENSE

There is a tense formed from the present tense of the verb "to be" plus the infinitive of the verb, which means that the action is just about to take place, as in the following:

Endi okhwitsa.                      I am just coming now. (or) I'll come all right - I am just getting ready.
Ali okhutsia.                       He is just about to go.

This construction can also be used to indicate that an action was or will be just about to take place at some given time, by using a NI form of the verb "to be", followed by the infinitive:

Ndamunyoola nali okhukona.          I found him just about to go to bed.
Ndali nindi okhutsia.               I was just about to go.
Khurulile nibali okhulia.           We came away when they were just about to start their meal.

Another construction that can be considered with the preceding is one using the verb ENYA followed by the infinitive. This seems to indicate that something is apparently about to happen.

Omulwale yeenya okhufwa.            The invalid seems to be at the point of death.
Yali niyeenya okhufwa.              He seemed to be at the point of death.
Wenya okhukwa?                      It looks as if you are going to fall!

Still another form that may be considered in this section is that using ITSA followed by the infinitive. It is almost the exact equivalent of the English GOING TO, etc., implying intention or determination.

Shimanyile ta, ne enzitsa okhumanya. I don't know, but I'm determined that I shall.
Yeetsa okhukhola.                   He'll do it all right.
Beetsa okhwitsa.                    They're coming.

Exercise 63
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. The cows are just about to go into the village now.
2. Come quickly; your father is at the point of death.
3. We were just about finished.
4. The visitors are due to arrive now.
5. The leopard was just about to kill me, but my cousin shot it. (shoot = lasa).
6. That child looks as if it is going to be ill.
7. The house seems to be about to fall down.
8. This woman seems to be about to have her baby.
9. It looks as if it is going to rain (= as if rain is about to fall).
10. He was just about to get up.
11. Don't be afraid - your mother will come back.
12. They don't know Luyia, but they are going to.
13. He is going to build a house here.
14. We are going to read lots of books next year.
15. I found them just about to cook their evening meal.
16. When I reached there this morning, they were just about to send someone to call me.

-----oo0oo-----

A N D

There are many different constructions, each of which would be translated into English by using "and". The following are some of the most common:

NA-, joining two nouns.
omukhasi nomwanawe                  the woman and her child
tsing'ombe netsimbusi               cows and goats

NE-, joining clauses; it is usually written (and pronounced) conjunctively. It sometimes represents "but", in which case it is usually not joined. It is sometimes used to begin a sentence.
Yakula ing'ombe, nolunyuma-khwo nakula tsimbusi tsine. He bought two cows, and later on he bought four goats.
Nolwa yalola ario .....             And when he saw that .....
Yali nabakhasi abanji; ne mulala khubo yali omukumba. He had many wives; but one of them was barren.

NINA-, joining two absolute pronouns, or a noun and a pronoun:
isie ninawe                         you and I (lit. I and you)
abaana ninasi                       the children and I

NENDE, as NA- above, joining two nouns that stand for separate things:
omukaate nende isiagi               bread and butter
abasiani nende abakhaana            boys and girls

NENDE, joining one or more infinitives to a preceding verb. This is different from the consecutive construction in NI-; that shews verbs referring to a sequence of actions; the infinites with NENDE merely name the actions, without any indication of time or sequence.
Alisia, nende okhureenya, nende okhutsia amatsi, nende okhuteekha.
She will grind, and gather firewood, and go for water, and cook.

Alisamulanga nende okhuchelela.
He will go in and out freely. (lit. He will be going out and returning.)

Note that NA- may also be used in this way - Alisia, nokhureenya...

KHANDI, joining two nouns referring to the same thing or person:
Omwami wanje, khandi Nyasaye wanje. My Lord and my God.
Nomwimilisi khandi nomundu omuchesi. He is a leader and a wise man.

KHANDI, joining two clauses which both in meaning and structure are parallel or similar, with no definite time sequence:
Yamanya okhusooma, khandi yamanya okhulima. She knows both how to read and how to dig.

NI- of the consecutive construction, as given on p.78.

Exercise 64
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. He has tables and chairs and beds in his house.
2. Men and women both may come to the meeting.
3. You (pl.) and they will all go to the feast tomorrow.
4. He is my brother and my teacher.
5. Tomorrow we will walk, and we will play games, and we will rest.
6. We went for a walk (i.e. walked), and then played games, and rested.
7. We read, and wrote letters, and sat and talked.
8. Tomorrow we will read, and then write letters, and then sit and talk.
9. This is my house and also my duka.
10. He has sheep and goats and cattle.
11. She has two children, but they do not live at home.
12. I saw him going into the house, and later on I saw him coming out from it.
13. We and you together will be able to finish that field.
14. He was ordered to come, but he refused.
15. At our school we learn reading, and writing, and sewing, and many other things.
16. She got up and lit the fire and cooked a meal.

-----oo0oo-----

NOUN CLASSES

In the noun classes which have not yet been dealt with, comparatively few new stems will be met, except in class 9, OBU-; the other classes consist almost entirely of words derived from stems either of verbs, adjectives, or of nouns more regularly occurring in other classes.

Class 7
AKHA - ORU
Diminutive Class

Any noun may be put into this class, by substituting the AKHA-, ORU- prefixes for the normal class prefixes, to indicate that the thing referred to is tiny, or in some cases insignificant. To name a person or thing in the diminutive class, e.g. to call a man AKHASAATSA = a tiny man, is called OKHUNANIKHA; it is usually done in rather a contemptuous way, and may be most insulting. But in referring for instance to a young baby as AKHAANA it may be rather a term of endearment and almost of praise, as long as it does not seem to mean that it is abnormally small.

Usual Form          Sing.           Plur.           Meaning
omwana              akhaana         orwana          tiny child
omundu/eshindu      akhandu         orundu          tiny person/thing
linyonyi            akhanyonyi      orunyonyi       tiny bird
eshisika            akhasika        orusika         very small room
omwitsa             akheetsa        orwitsa         little friend

In the following words, note how the initial consonant of the stem, previously nasalized by the IN- prefix, reverts to the original form when the prefix is changed:
ingokho             akhakokho       orukokho        little fowl
ing'ombe            akhakombe       orukombe        little cow
inzu                akhatsu         orutsu          small house
indabushi           akhalabushi     orulabushi      little club

In the Pluralia Tantum, i.e. words in the AMA- class, usually referring to liquids, which have no singular form, the diminutive form is, of course, in the Plural:
amatsi                              orutsi          a tiny drop of water
amafura                             orufura         a tiny drop of oil

Pron.Conc. and Subj.Conc.: S., KHA-   Pl., RU-

Possessives, Absolute Pronoun, Demonstratives, etc., are built up by using the rules already given; they may be verified by reference to the Table of Concords at the end of the book.

There is a special class of enumeration, which has already been referred to in the notes on numbers and on Cl.6 nouns. The word KHANDI (= times), although similar in form to Cl.7 nouns, is really in this class. It is used for both singular and plural, with singular concords in LU- and plural concords in KHA-. In many dialects the noun itself is usually omitted; sometimes it is heard with an initial A-.

khandi lulala       or  lulala                      once
khandi khabili      or  okhuula khabili or khabili  twice
khandi khanga?      or  khanga? or okhuula khanga?  how many times?

Exercise 65
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. It is only a very small matter that I want to tell you.
2. She gave me a tiny dress that was not (big) enough (for) me.
3. There was a tiny drop of blood on his leg.
4. He brought us a very small quantity of firewood (add -TITI after the Cl.7 noun).
5. There were just a very few small embers in the fire-place.
6. Do I want to live in that tiny country?
7. He had a tiny drum in the house.
8. Go and look for our little herd-boy.
9. It's a tiny little path.
10. Yesterday he was digging with a tiny hoe.
11. I have told you four times to do that work.
12. He was beaten twice.
13. How many times have I told you to be quiet?
14. I only want a tiny piece of cloth.
15. The fowl had five minute chickens.
16. That tiny girl won't be able to carry that load.

Class 8
OBU-
Abstract

There would seem to be two main identifiable groups of nouns belonging to this class: (i) Abstract nouns, and (ii) Singularia Tantum; there are also others which do not appear to belong to either group:

i. Abstract nouns, formed from verbs, adjectives, and nouns:
obuleemu            lack, shortage      from    v. leema (lack)
obuhuluushi         rest, holiday        "      v. huluukha (rest)
obulwale            illness              "      v. lwala (be ill)
obuheeli            love                 "      v. heela (love)
obufumu             prophecy, etc.       "      n. omufumu (witchdoctor)
obuyinda            wealth               "      n. omuyinda (rich man)
obuchesi            wisdom               "      adj. -chesi (wise)
obulala             unity                "      adj. -lala (one)

Note that two forms of nouns may be derived from verbs by the use of OBU-, an active and a passive form:
polygamy            v. halikha = take more than one wife
  obuhalishi        (of man) taking more than one wife
  obuhalikhwi       (of woman) being taken as one of several wives.
call                v. langa = call
  obulanji          (of caller) calling
  obulangwi         (of one called) calling, i.e. being called

When a verbal noun is required the infinitive (Cl.9) is usually preferable to the OBU- form unless it is really an abstract idea that is needed; Europeans tend to use the OBU- in very many cases where its use sounds unnatural and wrong to the Omuluyia. e.g. WRITING as a subject in school is an activity, not an idea, and so OKHUHANDIIKA is better than OBUHANDIICHI; the latter could, perhaps, be used at times for naming the subject; but it would never be used in referring to a person's hand-writing - that is his OKHUHANDIIKA, not his OBUHANDIICHI.

ii. Singularia Tantum, i.e. collective nouns found in the singular form only; they fall into two groups:

a. Masses of uncountable objects:
obwonya             locust hoppers
obwoba              mushrooms; fungus
obwoya              hair of the body, human or animal
obunyasi            grass

b. Materials, matter without form, viscous liquids:
obuusi              cotton threat
obusuma             stiff porridge (the ordinary staple diet)
obusela             gruel
obwongo             brain
obutunduli          marrow
obuushi             honey
obulimbo            gummy sap of a certain tree
obutooche           thick sap, as of bananas

There are a number of other common words, including the following, which do not appear to belong to any of the above groups:
obwina              a hole (in the ground)
obulalo             foot-bridge; fish traps across stream
obuliba             a trap
obuyingo            a bow (for shooting arrows)
obweni              brow; face
obwelu              floor; main room of house

Although there are no separate forms for singular and plural of nouns in this class it should be noted that in most cases when it is necessary to indicate the singular a form may be used in OLU-; e.g. OLUNYASI = one blade of grass (OBUNYASI); OLUUSI = a thread of cotton (OBUUSI); OLWOYA = a single hair (OBWOYA). Some other words may have a plural form in AMA-; e.g. AMEENA = a number of holes (as, e.g. those dug for the uprights of a house).

Pron.Conc. and Subj.Conc.: BU-

-----oo0oo-----

Class 9
OKHU-
Infinitive

All verbs in their Infinitive form can be used as verbal nouns. Quite often the OBU- and OKHU- nouns from a given verb stem are equally suitable for use; but read again the note given in the preceding section, on OBU- nouns.

Pron.Conc. and Subj.Conc.: KHU-

Exercise 66
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. I don't like sweeping.
2. His writing is bad.
3. That is a very bad illness.
4. Wealth brings trouble.
5. There was a shortage of food in the country.
6. I don't like going short of food.
7. Rest is good.
8. I want a rest.
9. I saw lots of locust hoppers on the road yesterday.
10. There are edible mushrooms (i.e., which are eaten), and there are inedible ones.
11. I have just bought this honey at the market.
12. He dug a hole and hid his money in it.
13. Give me some cotton to sew my dress with.
14. They brought us help (noun ending in -I) from (i.e. which comes from) the chief.
15. I want one thread of cotton.
16. They caught a lot of fish in the fish-traps yesterday (catch = tiila).
17. We drink gruel every day.
18. Bones have marrow.
19. Reading hurts my eyes.
20. Eating is what he likes doing.
21. Faith brings salvation (the noun for "faith" ends in I).
22. Bondage awaits me.

Note that OBU- nouns from verb stems usually (but not always) have the final A changed into I; this means that a verb ending in IA will usually have its OBU- noun ending in II. Note too that in some cases the OBU- form has a specific meaning that is not just the abstract idea of the verb, as in the first example below:
obukusii            price (kusia = buy)
obwayi              herding; and because herding was often done in turns, one's turn in any job is often called "obwayi" (aya = herd)
obwechesii          teaching (echesia = teach)
                    etc.

-----oo0oo-----

Class 10
HA-
Locative, = place, or at

The general noun HANDU or HABUNDU is used for a place, and any noun put into this class indicates place or position. The HA- pref. is often used to form an adverbial phrase, in which case concords agree with the noun used; e.g. HABILENJEBIE = at his feet (-BIE agreeing with EBILENJE); but sometimes the form with the locative is used as a noun, taking new (Class 10) concords; e.g. HALWANYI HALABILE = the courtyard is clean.

hango               at home             (ingo)
halwanyi            in the courtyard    (olwanyi)
habwelu             the main body of the house (obwelu)
hamachina           at the ancestral stones (amachina)

Pron.Conc. and Subj.Conc.: HA-

-----oo0oo-----

Class 11
MU-
Locative, = in

This class is quite distinct, in form and meaning, from both the OMU- classes. It has no O- preprefix, though where coalescence would be expected to occur is is often supplied, by false analogy; e.g. "a pleasant face" would be MUMONI MULAYI; but "the face is pleasant" would be MUMONI NOMULAYI.

NOTE ON BOTH LOCATIVE CLASSES: It should be observed that the locative prefix is placed before the whole noun, and not substituted for the existing prefix; compare this with the note on p.54.

The MU- locative prefix usually indicated "inside-ness"; e.g. MUNZU = in the house; MUSHISANDA = in the cup. It is, like HA-, often used to form an adverbial phrase; e.g. MUNZU INDAYI = in a good house; MUSHISANDA ESHIKHONGO = in a large cup. Occasionally the form with the locative is used as a substantive (a noun), taking Class 11 concords; in this case it sometimes has a special meaning, as in the second example given. Compare these two pairs of sentences:

i.  Beetsile munzu yanje.               They came into my house.
    Munzu muno mulimwo eshiilima.       Inside-this-house there is in it darkness. i.e. This house is dark inside; or, It is dark in this house.

ii. Eshindu shiinjiile mumoniyo?        Has something got into your eye?
    Mumonimwo mubiiyile.                Your face is dirty.

The following are some examples of words formed with MU- that are commonly used as class 11 nouns:
mumbafu             the side            (tsimbafu = ribs)
mumoni              face                (tsimoni = eyes)
mumuliango          verandah            (omuliango = doorway)
mumbali             places, districts   (? tsimbali)
mungoloobe          evening
mutsuli             morning
mukamba             morning
mukwaha             armpit              (sometimes ikwaha)
munzile             field where obule has been harvested

-----oo0oo-----

Class 11a
?
KHU-
Locative, = on

Theoretically, at least, there is a similar class with KHU- concords; but in actual fact one does not hear them - or so rarely and indefinitely that it hardly seems advisable at this stage to regard them as a regular class. If they do occur, they would be formed in all respects as Cl.11, with KHU- instead of MU-.

-----oo0oo-----

Class 12
OKU - EMI
Augmentative (Giant) Class

As with the Diminutive class, any noun may be put into this class by changing the prefix; such a change usually indicates abnormal size. To name a person or thing in the augmentative class (e.g. to call a man OKUSAATSA) is called OKHUCHICHIMA; it is frequently done derisively - that great, gawky creature! - though it may also be used to express amazement and respect for size.

Usual Form          Sing.           Plur.           Meaning
omundu/eshindu      okundu          emindu          very large person or thing
eliaro              okwaro          emiaro          very large boat
inzu                okutsu          emitsu          very large house
indabushi           okulabushi      emilabushi      very large club

Pron.Conc. and Subj.Conc.: S., KU-    Pl., CHI-

Exercise 67
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. Whose is that great big child over there?
2. He found a tiny worm on the path.
3. He was grumbling about the shortage of food.
4. The old woman was crying about her blindness.
5. He was very surprised when (= olwa) he saw the size (bigness) of the mushrooms in my basket.
6. That arrangement does not please me.
7. He had faith.
8. Deceit brings sorrow.
9. Children, sit down on the grass.
10. They brought me gruel.
11. Did you get many mushrooms?
12. No, we didn't find one.
13. Your (s.) writing is not good.
14. The children's laughter annoys him.
15. The main part of the house is small, but there are a lot of rooms.
16. If you tell him, he will come this evening.
17. My armpit is paining (me).
17. A great big cow ate all our grass.
18. He bought an enormous drum in the market, and brought it home, and gave it to the chief.
19. What are those great big things over there?
20. Maize is planted in all districts of the country.
21. That is the place where they make baskets.
22. He sat at the table writing a letter.

-----oo0oo-----
ADJECTIVES

Here is a further list of adjective stems in common use:

-angu           light (in weight), easy, cheap
-bisi           fresh, raw
-cheni          strange, foreign
-chesi          wise, clever
-chishi         unripe
-holo           humble, gentle, mild
-khasi          female
-khaya          empty, meaningless, with nothing added
-khulundu       important, chief, senior, oldest
-lamba          complete, whole, perfect
-lunji          straight
-lulu           fierce, bitter, angry
-manani         poor
-milamili       sweet
-nyelele        narrow, thin (of a person, only one naturally slim)
-siro           heavy
-yia            new                         ) some people make this dist-
-yiakha         new, novel, of a new kind   ) inction; others say these
                                            ) are synonyms.
-yinda          rich

All these, of course, are used with the adj.conc. - OMUHEMBE OMUCHISHI; AMACHINA AMASIRO; OMUHANDA OMUNYELELE. But there is another very important adjective that takes relative concords, with or without the preprefix; it is the stem -NDI, meaning other, some, certain, etc. (But note that "other people's things" are EBINDU BIABEENE). As in English, it can be used alone as a noun or pronoun, in which case it retains the preprefix; this is usually dropped when the word is used adjectivally. Occasionally the adjectival form precedes the noun; this seems to indicate something "other" in the sense of being different or far removed.

omukhasi undi           a certain woman; another woman; some woman
omuhanda kundi          another road; a certain road
abandi beetsile         some (or some others) have come
abandi basunga mbu niye some people say it is he
omundu undi yambeelesiye someone gave it to me
atsiile mushindi shialo he has gone into another country altogether

Although the number of real adjectival stems in the language is limited, there are various ways of expressing an adjectival idea; the following are some of the most common:

i. By using the poss.part. followed by an adverb, noun, etc.:
    abandu bakhale          people of long ago; ancient. (khale)
    ebindu biabeene         other people's things (omwene = owner)
    amakhuwa katoto         true words (toto = truly)
    omwana wahakari         the middle child (hakari = between)
    amakhuwa kamabiswi      secret, confidential matters (mabiswi = secretly)
    oluchendo lwakahala     a slow journey (kahala = slowly)
    omundu wamayinje        an argumentative person (amayinje = arguments)

ii. By using a relative clause, particularly with a neuter verb (for neuter verbs see later section):
    omundu owamanyikha      a well-known person (manyikha = be known)
    ebindu ebilolekha       visible things (lolekha = be visible)
    amakhuwa akahulilikha   audible words (hulilikha = be audible)
    omundu oufumanga        a famous person (fuma = be far-famed)

iii. By using passive forms of the verb:
    omundu omwisiikwa       a trustworthy person (isiika = trust)
    omwana omuheelwa        a beloved child (heela = love)
    omundu omusubilwa       a trusted person (subila = trust)

iv. By using a word formed from the stem of the verb with the final -A changed to -E, as explained under Class 2 nouns.
    omundu omwibule         a free-born person (ibula = give birth)
    inyanga indobole        a chosen day (lobola = choose)
    omundu omurie           a relation who must be treated with formal respect (ria = fear, respect)

v. Adjective stems can often be formed from verbs by adding -FU; if the verb ends in -RA or -LA, the -FU may be substituted for this; otherwise -FU may be added after the -A, or the -A may be changed to -E or -I:
    -tofu           lazy, weak          from    tora        become weak
    -khomefu        fat                   "     khomela     get fat
    -nyifu          cold, wet             "     nyira       get cold, wet
    -leng'aafu      equal                 "     leng'aana   be level, equal to
    -bambalifu      broad                 "     bambala     spread out
    -enjefu         ripe                  "     enga        ripen
    -sangafu        happy                 "     sangala     be happy

vi. Adjective stems can be formed from many verbs by simply changing the final -A to -U:
    -tundumu        warm                from    tunduma     get warm
    -umu            dry                   "     uma         get dry
    -tinyu          hard, firm, difficult "     tinya       be hard, get stuck
    -yiu            warm and fresh, as
                      fresh milk          "     yia         get hot
    -fuhu           blunt                 "     fuha        get blunt
    -memu           sharp                 "     mema        get sharp, be sharp

It should be noticed that there is in Luyia nothing corresponding to the English "comparison of adjectives"; each adjective has only the one form, with no differing "degrees". When an actual comparison of qualities is required it is often done by using the verb OKHUSHILA or OKHUYOMBA (= to exceed); or by some such statement as "of (or in) these, this is the big one". The following are examples of the ways in which such comparisons may be made:

Tomasi nomurambi okhushila omusiani wabwe.      Thomas is taller than his brother.
Isie ekhushila oburambi.                        I am taller than you.
Mubaana baSara, Tomasi niye omurambi.           Thomas is the tallest of Sarah's children.
Niye omurambi okhubayomba bosi.                 He is the tallest of them all.
Niye oubayomba bosi oburambi.                   He is the tallest of them all.
Abashila bosi oburambi.                         He is the tallest of them all.

But generally there is no need felt to indicate comparison; NIYE OMURAMBI = he is the tall one, is sufficient.

Notice also the following:
Abandu abashila obunji bali nende emikunda chiabwe.     Most people have their own fields.
Mumukanda kweefwe, abashila obunji bamanya okhusooma Olungeresa. In our class, most of them can read English.

Exercise 68
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. Carry this tiny child for me; he is very light.
2. We don't eat raw eggs.
3. The fat boy eats lots of obusuma.
4. This river is broader than that one.
5. We do not drink boiled (i.e. cooked; formed by method iv) milk; we drink it when it is fresh.
6. John is the heaviest boy in the school.
7. Give it to someone else (= another person).
8. Mary is more humble than Rhoda.
9. We are to have a new kind of lesson tomorrow.
10. He has another house at Kakamega.
11. We followed along the narrow path and reached (to) the new school.
12. They eat a whole bunch of bananas every day.
13. Some woman gave me a ripe mango.
14. Their food is sweeter than ours.
15. He went (= followed) by a different path.
16. Bring me the sharpest knife.
17. Most of our fowls got lost.
18. Those oranges are very dry.
19. If you take other people's things, that is stealing.
20. Some of the cotton (thread) was burnt in the fire.
21. Knitting is very difficult.
22. No, it is easy.
23. If you wear wet clothes, you will be ill.
24. Is that news true?
25. He lives in the middle house.
26. That is the famous John!
27. He is the most trusted person in the village.
28. Most cows have horns.

-----oo0oo-----

CONTINGENT MOOD

This is used to indicate that something would happen if something else happened. It is in two forms: a present tense form, called by Werner in Swahili the "Possible Contingent", and a past tense form, called the "Impossible Contingent". Both are indicated by an infix -AKHA-, and the latter by the addition of the -ILE suffix. The pre-requisite condition is usually indicated by a NI conditional clause; but it may merely be introduced by some such word as FWANA (perhaps), or HAUNDI (possibly, under certain conditions). The contingent clause is sometimes introduced by KHANE, and sometimes the preceding clause also. The contingent clause takes the ordinary SHI- negative. Note that polysyllabic verbs form the Imposs.Cont. by doubling the penultimate vowel and changing the final A to E, as is usual with the near past.

Possible Contingent:
    Nimba nimulola ndakhamuupa.         If I saw him I should hit him.
    Nindi isie, ndakhakula.             If it were I, I should buy it.
    Haundi yakhatsia.                   Possibly he may go.
    Fwana ndakhanyala.                  Perhaps I may be able to.

Impossible Contingent:
    Nimba nimulutsi ndakhamuupile.      If I had seen him (recently) I should have hit him.
    Nimba nindamulola ndakhamuupile.    If I had seen him (long ago) I should have hit him.
    Khane nobeeleho, khane shiyakhakholile ta. If you had been here he would not have done it.
    Fwana khwakhatsiile.                Perhaps we might have gone.

Exercise 69
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. If we should find him we should tell him.
2. If it were he, he would come here.
3. If I had been there, I should have helped you.
4. If it had been I (yesterday) I should have gone in.
5. If they come they may be able to help us to harvest.
6. Perhaps she would look after the children for you.
7. Her husband died long ago; if the doctor had been here, he might possibly have recovered.
8. They would have come in if they had known that we were (= are) inside.
9. If I had any pain (use pass. of TSUNA) I should take (= drink) the medicine.
10. The books may possibly have been hidden in the box that is at my place.
11. Perhaps they would have helped us to dig the hole.
12. If I had not seen her yesterday I should have gone to Kisumu.
13. He was here this morning; if we had known about the meeting we should have told him.
14. If the cows had not been taken to the grazing-ground, you would have seen them.
15. If he had planted this field, he would have harvested a lot of maize.
16. I may possibly be able to buy a sheep at the market.

-----oo0oo-----

USES of "-KHWO"

You will have noticed how frequently this suffix appears in Luyia. Some of its uses have already been explained:

i. Possessive, Class 9 (Infinitive), 2nd sing.:
    Okhuchendakhwo          Your walk.

ii. Locative = on, etc.:
    Niinakhwo               Climb up on it

iii. Absolute Pronoun, class 9 (Infinitive):
    Okhwo nikhwo            That is it

iv. Meaning "about" (this is really the same as ii above):
    Ndahulilakhwo           I heard about it
    Okhuboolelekhwo         Tell us about it

v. A further common use is to modify or tone down the sense of another word, usually a verb, although adjectives, adverbs, and even nouns can be so modified:
    Aliilekhwo.             He has had something to eat (but not eaten heartily).
    Khulimilekhwo.          We dug for a bit.
    Neinditikhwo.           It is fairly small.
    Akholile bulayikhwo.    He worked fairly well.
    Eshiilimakhwo.          A bit dark (lit. a bit of darkness).

vi. With a negative verb this has the effect of intensifying the negative; this is of course similar to the English usage, as will be seen in the following:
    Shinjamilekhwo ta.      I am not the least bit pleased.
    Shiyeenyilekhwo ta.     He didn't want it in the slightest.
    Shikhwalolakhwo ta.     We didn't even catch a glimpse of it.

vii. With the far past tense this usually gives the sense of "never", as contrasted with the simple negative, which merely means "not at that time".
    Shindatsia Nairobi ta.      I didn't go to Nairobi (then).
    Shindatsiakhwo Nairobi ta.  I have never been to Nairobi.

Note that two of these may on occasion be suffixed to the same verb; or another locative may follow a -KHWO ending:
    Ndahulilakhwokhwo.          I heard something about it.
    Shindachendakhwokhwo ta.    I have never walked on it at all.
    Wamenyakhwoyo?              Did you ever live there?

Exercise 70

1. Shimumanyilekhwo ta.
2. Okhuboolakhwo shikhuli okhulayi ta.
3. Rakhwo.
4. Okhulila khwomwana nikhwo okhwanjonyiye.
5. Endi omulamukhwo.
6. Abaana bakhukhoonyilekhwo.

Translate into Oluluyia:
1. The clothes have dried a bit.
2. He is not very well (i.e. he is a bit ill).
3. The fruit has gone a little bit bad.
4. Yesterday he annoyed me rather with his grumbling.
5. It was your coughing that woke me.
6. I have never accused that woman falsely.
7. He didn't eat anything at all.
8. He laughed a little when I told him about it.
9. When I have finished writing this letter I shall sleep for a while.
10. Open it a bit.
11. She never rests.
12. If he hears about it he will be surprised.
13. I have never heard a word about it.
14. Have you ever been there?
15. Don't do it at all!
16. He worked there for a while.
17. Don't climb up on it at all.
18. He has never seen the sea.

-----oo0oo-----

EMPHASIS

In English the emphasis in a sentence can be placed on any desired word by the use of stress or force of utterance in speech, and indicated in writing by the use of underlining or of italics, as in the following examples:

    I did not see you.          is a mere statement of a fact.
    I did not see you.          (whether others did or not). [emphasising 'I']
    I did not see you.          (emphasising the negative - a flat denial) [emphasising 'not']
    I did not see you.          (though I may have heard you, or in some other way known you were there). [emphasising 'see']
    I did not see you.          (although I saw others). [emphasising 'you']

In Luyia emphasis cannot be put on a particular word or idea by stress; it is usually done by repetition of the word to be emphasised in some other form, or perhaps by the addition of some other word or particle to the sentence. Some usual methods of doing this can be illustrated from the sentence already used. The sentence as a simple statement of a fact, with no particular emphasis, is SHIKHULUTSI TA. The different ideas in the sentence can be successively emphasised as follows:

i. By using the absolute pronoun:
    Isie shikhulutsi ta.        I did not see you. [I emphasised]
    Shikhulutsi iwe ta.         I did not see you. [you emphasised]

ii. By using -KHWO to intensify a negative:
    Shikhulutsikhwo ta.         I did not see you. [not emphasised]

iii. A negative can also be intensified by adding NAHATUTU TA = not in the least:
    Shikhulutsi nahatutu ta.    I did not see you. [not emphasised]

iv. A verb can be emphasised by placing its infinitive at the beginning of the sentence:
    Okhulola shikhulutsi ta.    I did not see you. [see emphasised]

v. Emphasis can be placed on a noun, rather in the sense of "a mere ...", by repeating it with NA- in place of the preprefix:
    Omukhasi namukhasi yakhanyala?      Would a mere woman be able to?
    Ingubo nangubo yikhunyasie yirio?   Should a mere garment give us all this trouble?

vi. A somewhat similar emphasis can be placed on a verb, implying "simply ....", or "just went on ...", by repeating the verb stem with BU- in place of the subj.conc.:
    Yalima bulima.              He just went on digging.
    Tsia butsia.                Just go.

Other methods of indicating emphasis will be discovered by the student. The later section dealing with the Ideophone will show how certain words can be intensified by using the appropriate ideophones with them.

Exercise 71
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. I don't know. [I emphasised]
2. No mere man (= person) could do that work.
3. I will not tell you. [not emphasised]
4. She sews well. [sews emphasised]
5. He came into the room, but they just went on talking.
6. They didn't tell us that he is ill. [us emphasised]
7. You (s.) know how to speak Swahili. [You emphasised]
8. A mere child would be able to lift that box.
9. We were not invited to the feast. [We emphasised]
10. They did not buy us any clothes. [not emphasised]
11. She asked me about it; she did not tell me. [asked / tell emphasised]
12. She didn't give you any money. [you emphasised]
13. The man didn't dig the field; he weeded it. [dig emphasised]
14. Would a boy be able to chop down that tree? [boy emphasised]
15. Go on, speak; don't be afraid.

-----oo0oo-----

THE CAUSATIVE FORM

Doke defines the causative as "A verbal derivative form which expresses that the subject causes the action to be done rather than doing it himself". There is no corresponding form in English; it is often expressed by using "make" or "cause" before the verb, or by using a different verb, or in some way recasting the sentence; or the simple verb may be made to do double duty; examples of these will be seen below.

Two causative forms are known in Luyia; one of these, with endings in -IKHA, -EKHA, has dropped out of general use; that is, it does not seem to be used nowadays to form causatives, although some words formed in this way are in regular use:
    ema         stand           emekha      erect posts for wall of house
    bunga       come together   bunjikha    gather into a pile
    (obsolete stem)

It is probable that this causative form enters into that dealt with in section 4 below.

The usual Luyia causative form ends in -IA; but there are various different ways of forming it. These have probably not yet been fully tabulated, but the following two rules will cover the great bulk of the verbs in the language:

1. Most simple dissyllabic verbs, that is verbs of two syllables (except those ending in -LA and -RA) form the causative by inserting -I- before the final vowel -A. Note that this causes palatalization of a preceding K, KH, NG, or H.
    buutsa      sit and talk        buutsia     entertain; keep...company
    chonya      become tired        chonyia     tire
    hona        recover; escape     honia       heal; save
    laama       pray                laamia      lead in prayer
    biiha       go bad              biiyia      spoil
    teekha      cook                teeshia     cause to cook, i.e. marry (of man)
    sunga       speak               sunjia      cause to speak
    runga       pay                 runjia      make...pay; charge
    fwimba      swell               fwimbia     cause to swell
    huya        move house          huyia       turn out of one's house
    henda       worry (ints.)       hendia      worry (trans.)
    sita        move (intr.)        sitia       move (trans.)
    manya       know                manyia      explain, demonstrate, show

2. Most polysyllabic verbs (it may be noted that these are all derived forms), and also most dissyllabic verbs ending in -LA and -RA, form the causative by changing the final syllable to -SIA.
    kula        buy                 kusia       sell
    bala        count               basia       count things, number
    khula       grow                khusia      make ... grow
    kora        be lost             kosia       lose
    asamula     sneeze              asamusia    make ... sneeze
    ahukhana    differ from         ahukhasia   differentiate; distinguish
    bayila      go to live with     bayisia     take girl as wife without pay-
                  a man                           ing the regular IKHWE.
    ikura       be satisfied        ikusia      satisfy
    ambukha     cross over          ambusia     take across
    mayana      become evil         mayasia     spoil; corrupt
    huluukha    rest                huluusia    make rest; give rest

There are quite a number of verbs of which the causative is not formed simply by following these two rules. The following supplementary rules, although not complete and definite, will give some guidance:

3. Some monosyllabic verbs (verbs of only one syllable) form the causative by changing the final -A into -I and adding -SIA.
    fwa         die                 fwisia      cause death of (e.g. poison)
    kwa         fall                kwisia      drop; knock over
    ria         be afraid           riisia      frighten
    lia         eat                 liisia      feed
    But note:
    nywa        drink               nyweesia    give a drink to ...

4. Some verbs appear to have double causatives; that is, they probably form their present causative not from the simple stem of the verb, but from an old causative form with ending -IKHA or -EKHA. An alternative explanation is that these may have been formed from the applied form of the verb.
    chama       be pleased          chamisia    please
    huka        be surprised        huchisia    surprise
    hulila      hear, obey          hulilisia   make...hear, or obey
    holeela     be quiet            holelesia   quieten
    loba        refuse              lobesia     prevent; cause to refuse
    eka         learn               echesia     teach

Note that the use of -I- or -E- in these endings depends on the vowel in the verb stem, as previously explained.

5. Some verbs add -SIA to the stem
    handala     be coarse           handalasia  make coarse, harden
    ara         split               arasia      split

6. With some verbs phonetic changes occur that do not occur with other apparently similar verbs.
    chenda      travel              chesia      drive
    fwinda      shine (sun)         fwisia      cause (the sun) to shine
    But -
    henda       worry (intr.)       hendia      worry (tr.)
    landa       creep               landia      spread
    handa       stick (intr.)       handia      stick (tr.)

    buukha      arise               buusia      arouse
    ikha        come down           isia        bring down
    but -
    teekha      cook                teeshia     cause to cook, marry (of man)

    hamba       catch (fire)        hafia       light (a fire)
    but -
    fwimba      swell               fwimbia     cause to swell

    tiba        be lost             tifia       cause to be lost
    laba        become white        lafia       whiten
    but -
    fuba        spoil through       fubia       cause to spoil through
                  cooling                         cooling.
    itsuba      swear               itsubia     cause to swear

Causative with other forms of the Verb
These are formed according to the rules already given, amplified where necessary as shown below.

HABITUAL MANNER of the CAUSATIVE FORM: Look again at the rule for forming the Habitual. As stated there, -ANG- is inserted after the final consonant of the verb; i.e. in HONIA we would get HONANGIA, which would palatalize to HONANJIA; regressive assimilation then takes place, the -A- of the infix being assimilated to the -I- following, giving HONINJIA. Similarly -
    sitia           becomes         sitinjia
    ahukhasia          "            ahukhasinjia
    basia              "            basinjia
    laamia             "            laaminjia
    echesia            "            echesinjia

PASSIVE of a CAUSATIVE is formed by inserting -BW- instead of the usual -W- before the final vowel.
    honia           becomes         honibwa
    sitia              "            sitibwa
    ahukhasia          "            ahukhasibwa
    basia              "            basibwa
    echesia            "            echesibwa

APPLIED FORM with the CAUSATIVE: There are two possible constructions to be considered here:
i. When the applied is formed from the causative, and not the reverse, it is formed according to the usual rule; when the infix is -ER-, following -E- or -O- in the verb stem, the -E- of the infix is assimilated to the -I- of the causative ending, making the ending -IRIA in every case.
    honia           becomes         honilia         make well for ...
    sitia              "            sitilia         move for ...
    ahukhasia          "            ahukhasilia     differentiate for ...
    basia              "            basilia         count them for ...
    echesia            "            echesilia       teach for ....

ii. If, however, the causative is formed from an applied form, the rule for forming causatives, Rule 2 on p. 98, gives an ending -ESIA after a verb with -E- or -O- in the stem. Some of the examples given under Rule 4 may illustrate this; the following certainly does:
    kona            lie
    konera          sit, of a hen on eggs (lit. lie on)
    konesia         set a hen on eggs

THE RECIPROCAL FORM

The reciprocal form, indicating that an action is performed by two or more parties to each other, is derived from the simple stem of the verb by inserting -AN- between the final consonant and the following vowel or vowels.
This form is also sometimes used of an action performed jointly.

    sunga       speak               sungana     speak to each other
    tiila       hold                tiilana     hold on to each other
    khoonya     help                khoonyana   help each other
    hela        be enough           helana      be equal (enough for each other)
    manya       know                manyana     know each other
    laka        order, promise      lakana      make an agreement together
    upa         beat                upana       beat each other, fight
    khola       do                  kholana     do together
    chenda      walk                chendana    go along together

RECIPROCAL with CAUSATIVE
i. Reciprocal from the Causative:
    kusia       sell                kusania     sell to each other
    liisia      feed                liisania    feed each other
    itsusia     remind              itsusania   remind each other

ii. Causative from Reciprocal:
    ahukhana    differ from each other  ahukhasia   differentiate
    kabukhana   separate (intr.)        kabukhasia  separate (tr.)
    kulana      exchange                kulasia     cause to exchange

RECIPROCAL with APPLIED
i. Reciprocal from Applied:
    kabila          give a share to     kabilana        divide between themselves
    kulila          buy for             kulilana        buy for each other
    handiichila     write to            handiichilana   write to each other

ii. Applied from Reciprocal:
    sungana     speak to each other     sunganila   speak to each other at ...
    soolana     quarrel                 soolanila   quarrel about ...

-----oo0oo-----

ADVERBS

In the preceding pages numbers of adverbs have already been given in the short vocabulary lists. There are many others in use, most of them being formed from verb, adjective, or noun stems, with the use of appropriate prefixes. Many of the words used as adverbs may be used in the same form as nouns, the differentiation depending on the context; e.g. ESHIRONYO may mean "tidiness" or "tidily"; OLUKUNGU, "showing-off", or "ostentatiously"; HANGO, "home" or "at home".

Most adverbs that are primarily indicative of place are also sometimes used of time, as indeed is done in English ("before", "after", etc.); e.g. HABWENAHO may mean "just there", or "just then"; HALALA may mean "together", either in place or in time.

Some of the most usual adverbial formations are illustrated in the following lists. It should be noted that except where an E- locative has been prefixed (and probably with the ESHI- prefix used adverbially), there is a definite tendency to drop the preprefix usually associated with the prefix, although in most cases it may be used. Where the stem is in use in another form, it has been given in the third column.

PLACE
imbeli              in front                        -mbeli = first, leading
inyuma              behind                          olunyuma = after
(e)hale             afar
indangu             behind the house
habwenaho)          just there                      -ene = self;  ho/yo = there
ebweneyo )
emwalo              down, under
mulwalo             below, beneath, in the space under
ikulu               up, above (direction)
hekulu )            above, up on)   location        eshikulu = hill
emukulu)            above, up on)                   likulu = sky, heavens
elwanyi             outside                         olwanyi = courtyard
hasi                down, below                     isi = earth;  bottom
musi                right deep down in
hakari              between                         mukari = inside
halala              together                        -lala = one
halubeka)           at the side                     olubeka = side
elubeka )

NOTE: When any of the above is followed by such a phrase as "of the house", etc., the locative concord e.g.
    imbeli weinzu           in front of the house
    elwanyi welitaala       outside the village
    mulwalo weimeesa        under the table

In the following list attention is drawn to two very useful and fairly general methods of forming adverbs: 1, from verbs, with a MA- prefix; and 2, from nouns, with ESHI- prefix. It seems that in both cases the use of the initial vowel is optional, though it is most commonly as shown.

  (this adverb, from a vowel stem, usually seems to take the initial vowel)

It should be noted that frequently an adverbial construction is used in Luyia when in English an adjective would be used; e.g. YACHENDANGA ESHIHECHENYE = "he was going about nakedly", i.e. "he was naked".

Adverbs may be formed from adjectival (and other) stems by using the prefix BU-; OLU- and KA- are also often adverbial prefixes.

THE IDEOPHONE

In addition to the types of adverbs listed above there is the large group of Ideophones, a common Bantu characteristic; Doke describes the ideophone as "A vivid representation of an idea in sound". He goes on to say, "The ideophone is in Bantu a special part of speech, resembling to a certain extent in function the adverb, together with which it is classified as a descriptive."

The following are some of the ideophones in common use in Luyia; the first three given can be used to intensify words covering a very wide variety of meanings; all the others seem to be limited in application to words conveying one idea or group of very closely associated ideas. It seems that some of these, at least, have not undergone all the phonetic changes that have befallen the rest of the language - that they are probably closer to the Ur-Bantu form; e.g. while the verb "to get hot" has changed to YIA, the ideophone is PIA; "ten" has become EKHUMI, but the ideophone is KUMO.

po          much; very              Nomulayi po - She is very good
pe          completely; absolutely  Beetsile bosi pe - The whole lot of them came
kupu        (same)                  Asoomile kupu - He read the whole thing
koro        with lose, swallow      Tsing'ombe tsikorile koro - The cows are completely lost
kuru        empty                   Munzu nomukhaya kuru - The house is absolutely empty
kutu        finished                Aliile kutu - He ate up every scrap
kumo        ten                     Likhumi kumo - Exactly ten
            - but                   Ing'ombe kumo - a polled cow (i.e. hornless)
khamu       disappear               akhamile khamu - He disappeared completely
pa          bitterness              Nomululu papapapa - It is frightfully bitter
pia         hotness                 Kayiile pia - It's boiling hot
pup      )
paaa     )  a sudden, heavy fall    Naye hasi pup! - Down he came, crash!
pukuru   )
ta          hardness, firmness      Shitinyile tatatata - It has set quite hard
ti          blackness               Inzu yiiliime ti - The house is pitch dark.
tsererere   whiteness               Indafu tsererere - spotlessly clean, or dazzlingly white
tsi         cold, damp              Amatsi amanyifu tsi - Icy cold water
tu          fulness                 Abandumwo tu! - Crammed full of people
                                    Yitsuule tu - Filled to the brim

Exercise 75
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. Take the stones behind the house.
2. He lives far away.
3. The dress was absolutely black.
4. I sat between Mary and Sarah.
5. Put it aside.
6. Don't leave (it) outside; put it inside the house.
7. I knew that long ago.
8. Have a look at it every now and then (verb koonya).
9. He had a pure white goat.
10. At first strangers frightened him; but afterwards he got used to them (NALA = get used to).
11. He likes to eat standing.
12. Make her sit down on the floor.
13. This medicine is terribly bitter; the baby refuses (= loba) to drink it.
14. Keep on trying (lit. Do it perseveringly), and then (kho) you will get used to it.
15. We found the children naked.
16. John makes the children stand, but Thomas does it differently.
17. I heard them speaking quietly.
18. Our house is very tall.
19. How many cows are there? Just ten.
20. This water is boiling hot.
21. They have all gone, every one of them.
22. Our grain stores are absolutely empty.
23. This obusuma is as hard as a rock.
24. The house was icy cold.
25. Drink it carefully, it's frightfully hot.
26. Lie on your back.
27. They were walking in the European manner.
28. They eat in the Indian style.
29. Our books are missing; they have completely disappeared!
30. I don't like him; he is conceited.

-----oo0oo-----

THE "LA" FUTURE

There is a future tense formed by inserting LA between the subj.conc. and the stem (or obj.conc. if any), but it seems to have different meanings in different dialect areas. In some areas it is an Immediate Future, corresponding to the -AKHA- Immediate Past; this purpose is also served by the ALI OKHUKHOLA construction.

In other areas it serves as a Future of Intention; that is, its main significance is that of a definite intention that the action shall be performed, without indicating just when it will be. This use has been called an "Indefinite Future", but that term is rather misleading; admittedly it is indefinite in that it does not seem to indicate any approximate position in time; but I believe that that is because in using it the exact time is of only secondary importance; the emphasis is on the intention. - But remember that there are the two different usages.

The Habitual Manner of the -LA- Future is used to indicate something that will be happening regularly as from now onwards; e.g. ALAKHUKOONYANGA = He will be visiting you regularly.

It will probably be best to use the ordinary -LA- Future as a Future of Intention until further investigation shows whether the other significance is more general; it undoubtedly fills a need.

It is formed from the subj.conc. - LA - verb stem. In the 1st Sing., EN- coming before -LA- should, and usually does, give ENDA-; but there is a tendency, as with the far future, to use a reduplicated form, in this case NDALA-. This form is being used mostly in translation at present, but it remains to be seen which will become the generally accepted form. It should be used in these exercises. In the older form the initial E- is usually dropped in pronunciation, but the N is given almost syllabic value; it is spoken on a low tone, the tone rising on the DA, giving a tone pattern for NDAKHOLA, .. ; when NDAKHOLA indicates the far past tense the tone pattern is . .

khola - do
ndalakhola          khulakhola
olakhola            mulakhola
alakhola            balakhola
    kulakhola           chilakhola
               etc.

Exercise 76
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. If you don't come back the teacher will be annoyed.
2. Don't worry; I will come quickly.
3. If you send him to Butere he will certainly get lost on the way.
4. Don't chase the leopard; it will kill you.
5. We will be sleeping at home.
6. As from today (= Okhurula olwa leelo) the teacher will be leading them in prayer every day.
7. Just wait; the train will come.
8. He will get his wages all right.
9. He has gone to Kampala, but he will come back.
10. When he returns he will be working for us.

-----oo0oo-----

"STILL" and "NOT YET" TENSES

The infix -SHI- inserted between the subj.conc. and the stem of the verb indicates that the action is still in progress:
    Ashisoomanga eMaseno        He is still at school at Maseno
    Bashilima                   They are still digging
    Eshilwala                   I am still ill

Compound tenses may be formed by using the appropriate tense of the verb "to be" with the NI form of the "still" tense:
    Ndali nishimenyangayo       I was still living there (long ago)
    Nembe nishikona             I shall still be asleep.

The "not yet" idea may be expressed in either of two ways:
i. The most common method is by using the -SHI- form of the verb "to be", followed by the infinitive:
    Ashili okhwitsa             He hasn't come yet
    Khushili okhuhulila         We haven't heard yet

ii. Sometimes it is formed from the subj.conc. - RA negative - SHI - A - the verb stem:
    Arashiahulila ta            He has not heard yet.

The NI form of either of the above gives the sense of "not yet having done", or "before doing"; in some cases this form of the verb with -RASHIA- implies "not at all", or "not within the period that is in mind", whereas the -SHILI OKHU- construction has no backward look; e.g. YEETSILE NASHILI OKHULIA would mean "He came before he had his meal", whereas YEETSILE NARASHIALIA TA would probably mean "He came before he had a meal at all that day".

    Barulile nibarashiamulola ta            They went away before they saw him
    Shibalakalukha nibarashianyoo-          They will not come back until they
      la omushara ta.                         have got their wages.

-----oo0oo-----

OTHER DERIVED FORMS
of the
VERB

It is probable that all Bantu roots originally consisted of two syllables, in the form Consonant - Vowel - (Consonant or Nasal Compound) - Vowel; e.g. BALA, KONA, SIKHA, LONDA. Various phonetic changes through the centuries have in many cases altered that original form, so that now some roots begin with a vowel (e.g. ITSA, AYA), and others are monosyllabic (e.g. BA, HA). Further, various derivative suffixes have been added, changing or modifying both the form and the meaning of the word. In some cases this change probably took place in the old original language from which all the Bantu languages have sprung. By comparing many living forms of Bantu languages Meinhof, a German scholar, has worked out a large number of words in the forms in which they probably existed in this hypothetical parent language, which he calls Ur-Bantu. He deduces that there were already many derived forms in Ur-Bantu; for instance he gives a root GAKAMA, meaning "to gape"; in Luyia we have ASAMA, with the same meaning. He gives GIKUTA, "become satisfied", the origin of the Luyia IKURA, which means the same. So both those words (and many others) had already acquired an extra syllable in Ur-Bantu. In the case of some other words the change has probably occurred within Luyia, but long ago, and the original Luyia root has dropped out of use, leaving only the derived forms; for instance, KALUKHA and BOTOKHANA are obviously derived forms, from roots KALA and BOTA; but I have not found these roots in use in the language at the present time. In still others these suffixes may be added at will, giving a large range of words from a simple stem.

These derivative suffixes are in most cases easily recognized, and occur in similar forms in most Bantu languages; the significance of each is usually quite definite. Examples of derivative suffixes have already been seen in the -WA Passive ending, the -ILA of the Applied form, the -IA indicating the Causative, and the -ANA of the Reciprocal. Some other common and easily recognized forms are given below.

NOTE: It will be observed that in the following, -KHA endings are always intransitive; this does not of course apply to the -IKHA causative already dealt with in the appropriate section.

-----oo0oo-----

NEUTER

This has sometimes been called the Stative, but Doke recommends the use of the term Neuter Form. He says "In Bantu the term is used for that derivative form of the verb which indicates an intransitive state or condition, without any special reference to an agent determining that condition. In many cases the force of the English suffix -able or -ible expresses this neuter form; in others the idea of 'get' or 'become', as opposed to the passive use of 'be', seems to be the nearest equivalent." In Luyia the ending is -EKHA or -IKHA in place of the -A of the simple stem. As in the case of the Applied form, E in the suffix follows E or O in the stem, and I follows A, I, or U.

(a) Corresponding to English -able, -ible
    bala        count               balikha     be countable
    chinga      lift, carry         chinjikha   able to be carried
    lola        see                 lolekha     be visible

(b) Corresponding to English become, get
    ng'oona     beautify            ng'oonekha  become beautified
    khuula      remove teeth        khuulikha   (of teeth), come out
    manya       know                manyikha    become known

Exercise 78
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. Meat is obtainable at the market.
2. His voice is inaudible.
3. The letter was illegible.
4. That word is unpronouncable.
5. There were countless locusts on the road.
6. That box is too heavy; it can't be carried.
7. "Maker (v. longa) of all things, visible and invisible.
8. I have heard that the matter is known to the people who live in that village.
9. He appeared to them in the upper room.
10. The old woman's teeth have all come out, so now she cannot eat maize.
11. A house built on a hill cannot be hidden. (don't use passive).
12. The sea is immeasurable.
13. I tried to arrange the matter, but it was impossible.
14. The work got done, but we do not know who did it.
15. These days, milk is unobtainable.

-----oo0oo-----

REVERSIVE

This form indicates an undoing, a complete reversal of the action. With transitive forms (those that take an object) the suffix is -ULA, -ULULA (or -OLA, -OLOLA after O in the stem, but not after E); and in the intransitive it is -UKHA, -ULUKHA (or -OKHA, -OLOKHA after O). Both in this and in the next section, the ending is sometimes UULA or OOLA; e.g. ALUULA, FWINDUULA; LONDOOLA, CHINGUULA. This is probably a contraction of ULULA and OLOLA, but so far I have found no rule governing its use.

Stem                        Transitive                          Intransitive
bisa        hide            bisula      reveal                  bisukha     be revealed
bunga       gather up       bungulula   shake off               bungulukha  drop off
tsoma       insert          tsomola     withdraw                tsomokha    come out
boha        tie up          boholola    untie                   boholokha   come untied
reka        set (trap)      rekula      release (trap)          rekukha     be sprung

4. Untie the cow.

13. He has shut his eyes; tell him to open them.
14. I tied the door up securely, but it came untied.
15. Shake this mat out.

-----oo0oo-----

REPETITIVE or INTENSIVE

There is another form of the verb exactly similar to the Reversive in construction, but quite different in meaning; it indicates a continuation or repetition or intensifying of the action.

All the suffixes given under the Reversive are also used for the Intensive; this does not mean that in either of these forms all the suffixes are necessarily used with all verb stems. As the same suffix can intensify one stem and reverse another, it is necessary to become familiar with the actual usages; the examples given should be learnt, and the vocabulary studied. Examples asked in Exercises 79 and 80 are correct usages in their respective sections; note that, for instance, BISULA is never the intensive of BISA, nor is CHINGUULA ever the reversive of CHINGA.

Stem                        Transitive                          Intransitive
chinga      lift            chinguula   lift on high            chinguukha  be raised up
ela         select          elula       sort out
fula        singe                                               fululukha   wither up
londa       follow          londoola    follow along            londokhana  lie alongside
                                          (e.g. course of river)              (combined with reciprocal)
bomba       subside                                             bombolokha  go right down

-----oo0oo-----

EXTENSIVE

The suffix -AKA "indicates that the action is extended in time or space or repeated extensively" (Doke).

linda       wait            lindaka     wait on!
khupa       strike          khupaka     beat; thresh
khoma       knock           khomaka     throb
rema        chop down       remaka      clear new ground

-----oo0oo-----

PERFECTIVE or PERSISTIVE

In many Bantu languages a suffix in the form of a reduplication of the applied suffix (-ILILA or -ELELA) indicates that the action has been carried on to completion. In Luyia this form often means no more than that the action has been carried on persistently or gradually; therefore the second name given above would seem to be preferable.

hwa         come to an end  hwelela     come gradually to an end
lola        see             lolelela    oversee; supervise
linda       wait            lindilila   go on waiting until .. is ready
lwala       be ill          lwalilila   go on being ill; ail
londa       follow          londachilila chase until one overtakes
                              (combined with Extensive)

Note the following:
    fuchila answer          fuchiilila  agree - i.e. go on answering
    This should, by the rule, be FUCHILILILA, but the first L has been elided.

Note also:
    tsia        go          tsiililila  go on
    ria         be afraid   riililila   be nervous or shy

There is also a form with ending -ILISIA, -ELESIA, which looks as if it should be the causative of the preceding; but it seems to have practically the same significance as the -ILILA, -ELELA forms. As far as I know, any given verb will be found only in one of these forms, not in both.

mala        finish (i.e. do the whole thing)    malilisia   finish up (something already started)
runa        search                              runilisia   make a thorough search
reeba       ask a question                      reebelesia  question closely
henga       look                                henjelesia  keep a sharp look-out
hunya       smell (tr.)                         hunyilisia  smell out
khomba      lick                                khombelesia lick clean; also flatter in order to gain something

-----oo0oo-----

DENOMINATIVE VERBS

Verbs may be formed from noun and adjective stems by the addition of certain suffixes; these verbs are known as denominative verbs. There are four main suffixes, as follows:

i. -LA = be, become
    -bofu           blind                   bofula          be or become blind
    -kofu           old                     kofula          grow old
    kuukhu          grandmother             kuukhula        work slowly like an old woman
    -tofu           lazy                    tofula          be lazy

ii. -KHA = be, become
    eshimuli        a flower                mulikha         be in flower; bloom
    -lalu           mad                     lalukha         go mad
    -lunji          straight                lunjikha        be straight; straighten out
    -lamu           well, alive             lamukha         come to life; revive

iii. -HA = be (having become)
    -cheni          strange                 cheniha         visit; be a stranger at ..
    -inji           many                    injiha          be many; multiply
    -khongo         big                     khongoha        be big (having been small)
    -chesi          wise                    chesiha         have acquired some sense
    -manani         poor                    mananiha        have become poor

iv. -HALA = become
    -chesi          wise                    chesihala       become wise
    omukumba        a barren woman          kumbahala       become barren
    -nyelele        narrow                  nyelehala       become narrow
    omuyinda        a rich person           yindihala*      become rich

    * Note the change in the second vowel in this word.

-----oo0oo-----

FREQUENTATIVE

This form, indicating that the action is done over and over again, is a reduplication of the verb stem.

    sunga       talk                        sungasunga      talk and talk
    khaaba      search                      khaabakhaaba    keep on searching
    fuba (intr) spoil in cooking            fubafubia       continually spoil food
    saba        ask for                     sabasaba        be continually begging

POSITION

The ending -AMA, though apparently not used in forming words now, can be seen in many existing words; it usually indicates the taking up of a position; the following are some examples:

    inama           bow down
    bunama          bow down
    fumama          lie on the face
    kalama          lie on the back
    sikama          kneel
    borama          lie stagnant
    sikhama         lie stagnant

-----oo0oo-----

GENERAL NOTE
on Derived Forms of the Verb

Remember that all verbs of more than two syllables are derived forms. Make a habit of noticing the endings, and if they are among the ones that have been given here, see how the words fit in with the meanings given. If they are different - and there are others; for instance FUMIRA, SELENJERA, and a few other words seem to have a -RA ending - take a note of them, and when you have a number of examples see if you can find out the significance of the ending.
