Exercise 84
When you have corrected Ex. 83 from the answers at the back of the book, leave it for a couple of days; then working from the answers, translate it all back into Luyia. This time you will, of course, correct it from this page.

-----oo0oo-----

MUST, OUGHT, etc.

The varied meanings of this group of English words cover a wide range; the main ones can be put into two main groups:

1. What is fitting or appropriate or expected. This idea is usually conveyed by the word KHOYA, or sometimes KWANA. The latter leans more to the side of worthiness, and is not as common as KHOYA.

    Okhoyile okhutsia bulaano.)         You must/ought to go now.
    Okwanile okhutsia bulaano.)
    Omulwale akhoyile okhusilikhwa.     The sick man must/ought to have treatment.
    Ekhoyile okhumalilisia emilimo mbeli. I must finish up my work first.

This word is also often used with KA- agreements, = "it is neccessary (fitting, advisable, etc.) that you should ..."
    Kakhoyeele mbu emalilisie emilimo mbeli.    I must finish up my work first. (It is necessary that I should...)
    Akamukhoyeele ngakano, mbu akalusie imbusi, niyeeyama obwibibwe. What he should do is this: he must return the goat, and confess his theft.
    Kakhoyeele mbu omuheelesie eshihaanwa.      It would be fitting that you should give her a present.

2. Compulsion. There are two main ways of saying this:
(i) OKHUULA, either with OLWA and the infinitive, or with the subjunctive:
    Okhuula olwa witsa.)                You must come. (i.e. I order you to)
    Okhuula witse.     )
    Okhuula olwa arunga obushuru.       He must (i.e. is compelled to) pay tax.
    Okhuula arunje obushuru.

(ii) KHATIMBA with the subjunctive. This is not as common as the preceding, nor is it as strong:
    Khatimba okhoonye mawo.             You must help your mother.
    Khatimba oreme omusaala okwo.       You must chop that tree down.

Note that the two ideas, of what is fitting and what is compulsory, are not at all clearly differentiated, but very much merge into each other. For instance "You must get to school early" means both "It is expected that you should get to school early", and "Just you see that you get to school early", the context indicating which is the stronger idea. So it could be any of the following:

    Okhoyile okhuula mwisoomelo khale.
    Kakhoyeele mbu wule mwisoomelo khale.
    Okhuula olwa wula mwisoomelo khale.
    Okhuula wule mwisoomelo khale.
    Khatimba wule mwisoomelo khale.

Generally the first two are the best; the last three, and especially the use of OKHUULA, are a strong command, and would be used to one who obviously didn't intend to try to be early.

Exercise 85
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. You must come and see us.
2. He ought to try do the work himself.
3. It would be appropriate for you to write him a letter.
4. You must sweep this room before we have breakfast.
5. They must look after their clothes; if they don't look after them they will be punished (kuuma = punish).
6. We should pray before we start our work.
7. Boys must pay more than girls.
8. Each person must buy his own food.
9. They started digging before you got up; they ought to knock off early.
10. You must drink this medicine.
11. They must start early, or else (= nebutswa) they will be late.
12. You ought to save your money to buy a bicycle.

-----oo0oo-----

MALA with other Verbs

The verb MALA (= finish) is often used in conjunction with another verb to form a compound tense. (It may be noted that it would not be correct in these constructions to call MALA an auxiliary; grammatically it is the chief verb in the sentence and the second verb is subordinate, being an infinitive, a subjunctive, or a NI- participial form. MALA is said to be used in a Deficient sense, as it requires the second verb to complete the meaning, and that other verb is called a Complementary verb.

It is used in two different ways:
i. With the Infinitive, in the sense of "already"; or it may be translated by an English perfect, or sometimes simply "finished".
    Emalile okhumuboolela.                      I have told him.
    Yali namalile okhushela ing'ombe.           He had already milked the cow.
                                                or He had finished milking the cow.
    Nonyoole namalile okhubuukha.               You will find him already up.

ii. With the Subjunctive or the NI- Participial form, to mean "in the end", "after all", or "actually"; the two forms seem to be identical in meaning, and are interchangeable.
    Yamala nakula ingubo eyo. )                 In the end she bought the dress.
    Yamala akule ingubo eyo.  )
    Enziileyo, ne shimalile nimulola/emulole ta. I went there, but I didn't actually see him.
    Shindamala nimbalinda/embalinde ta.         I didn't wait for them, after all.

Exercise 86
Translate into Oluluyia:
1. They have already washed the clothes.
2. We had already gone into school.
3. You will find that they have already moved.
4. In the end they didn't bring the maize.
5. He said he was going to chop down the tree, but he didn't actually do it.
6. In the end we agreed that he should stay there.
7. Did she actually give you the money?
8. They were very frightened, but the leopard didn't get in after all.
9. They were invited to the party, but in the end they didn't go.
10. He worked hard all day, but in the end he was very tired.

-----oo0oo-----

WHY?, BECAUSE, THEREFORE, etc.

The stem CHILA = be the cause, is used in forming many words for these ideas, as is shown below:

Nishiina eshichila noshindile?          Why are you late? (What causes you to be late?)
Shichila embeele nemilimo.              Because I had work to do.
Niye owachila ninguumwa.                It was his fault that I was punished. (It was he who was the cause of my being punished.)
Nishiina eshichilile nokalukha?         Why have you come back?
Shichila enzibiliile ebitabo.           Because I forgot my books.
Kho niko akachilile!                    So that was why!
Yalwala; niko akachila nareetsa ta.     He was ill; therefore he didn't come. (That was why .....)

There is a slight difference between NISHIINA ESHICHILA? and MBUSHIINA? or OLWASHIINA?, for "Why?" The former implies that some outward circumstance was the reason - the latter, that it was a reason arising within the person himself; e.g. if a child remains at school when the others have gone home, the teacher might ask him, OSHINDILE HANO MBUSHIINA? - "Why have you delayed here?", assuming that he wants something, or has made some arrangement. When he gets home his mother would ask him NISHIINA ESHICHILILE NOSHINDA? = "What has made you late?", assuming that someone or something at school prevented him from coming home at the usual time. But generally speaking the difference is insignificant, and the words can usually be regarded as interchangeable.

When SHICHILA is used for BECAUSE it implies a definite idea of a cause or reason. In English we sometimes use the word "for", with less emphasis on cause: "He came to see me because I was ill" would imply that my being ill was his only reason for coming. This in Luyia would be YEETSA OKHUNGOONYA SHICHILA NDALI OMULWALE. "He came to see me, for I was ill", means almost the same, but there is less stress on my illness being the cause. This corresponds to the Luyia YEETSA OKHUNGOONYA OKHUBA NDALI OMULWALE, which literally means something like "it being that I was ill".

Usually SHICHILA and OKHUBA can be regarded as interchangeable; but as SHICHILA is more definite than OKHUBA, often one is preferable to the other.

Exercise 87
Translate into Oluluyia: (Use NISHIINA ESHICHILA rather than MBUSHIINA or OLWASHIINA unless you feel that the latter would definitely be preferable) -
1. Why isn't she here? Because she wasn't invited; she has gone somewhere else.
2. Why are you annoyed? Because that girl lost my book.
3. Why did they go home? Because they were tired.
4. He was ill because of the bad water.
5. It was my fault that they didn't know.
6. That is my uncle; it is he who was responsible for my going to school.
7. I heard the news yesterday evening; that is why I am happy.
8. Why do you want me to tell you about it? Because it is you who saw it happen. (happen = ikhola).
9. It was because of his height that he was not able to hide easily.
10. It was because of the rain that we didn't come.
11. It was because of you that I recovered from my illness.
12. The car broke down (= onoonekha); that was why they were late.
13. The food is all finished; that is why we are hungry.
14. It is the stagnant water that is the cause of so many people being ill.

-----oo0oo-----

NOTES on the TABLE of CONCORDS

CLASSIFICATION OF NOUNS

There is a standard classification of Bantu nouns - Bleek's. His numbering is shown in the second line of figures at the top of the sheet; and in the top line we have the Luyia classification. This has been arranged as far as possible to fit into Bleek's framework, that is as far as class order goes.

It will be noticed that while Bleek gives every separate prefix a class number, in Luyia we have put singular and plural together to form a class, as this seems more helpful in a practical classification.

-----oo0oo-----

HOW? THUS

The idea of these words showing inflexion is an unfamiliar one to the English-speaking student, but the following examples should make the usage clear:

    Nokhole orie?               How will you do it?
    Nekhole endio.              I will do it this way.
    Nakhumanye khurie?          How shall we know?
    Namumanye murio.            You will know thus.
    Bakhanyala barie?           How could they?

The essential part of these words, -RI, was probably originally a verb meaning "say"; it is used with the appropriate subjectival concords to introduce direct speech or any quotation; note that MBU is used in the same way:

    Yasunga ari, "Nenzitse".            He said "I shall come".
    Eshitabo shiboola shiri ...         The book says ...
    Nabo bari, "Nifwe".                 And they (said), "It is we".
    Ndi, "Otsia heena?" Naye ari, "Enzia eweefwe." I said, "Where are you going?" And he answered, "I am going home".
    Nasunga mbu, "Nebianje".            And he said, "They are mine".

"Thank you." The usual expression corresponding to the English "thank you" is one merely expressing approval: ORIO. "That's right"; "you are right to do that". It will of course take the plural form if it is addressed to more than one person; and it can be used in the 3rd person if it expresses approval of the action of someone who is absent. As it expresses approval rather than gratitude it is very often used where the English "thank you" would be inappropriate; e.g. "Orio okhulima", said by a passer-by to a man who is digging his own field; "Orio okhwitsa okhulaama", to a person who has come to church. When a verb is required corresponding to the English "to thank", OKHUUPA ORIO is used; MBU is often inserted before ORIO.

These notes of course refer to the grammatical aspect; for that relating to etiquette enquires should be made elsewhere.

Papa akhuleereele eshihaanwa.           Father has brought us a present.
Ario muno.                              That was good of him.
Murio okhwitsa okhungoonya.             Thank you (pl.) for coming to see me.
Shiokhupanga mbu orio ta?               Don't you say "thank you"?
Tsia omuupile mbu orio.                 Go and say thank you to him.

-----oo0oo-----

"HERE IT IS", ETC.

These are all formed with the use of the NI copula; but the -I is dropped out, so that only N- is left, and that causes the following consonant to nasalize according to the ordinary rules.

Constructions of the three forms are given below; as frequently happens, Class 1 singular is irregular, so it is given lower down, after the regular construction has been demonstrated.

NOTE: PC = Pron.Conc.; IV = Initial Vowel

Here it is: n(i) - PC - IV - "this"
Cl. 1 plur.     n   ba      a       bano    = mbabano       ) Note that
Cl. 2 sing.     n   ku      o       kuno    = ngwokuno      ) when vowel
      plur.     n   chi     e       chino   = njiechino     ) of PC is
Cl. 3 sing.     n   li      e       lino    = ndielino      ) the same as
      plur.     n   ka      a       kano    = ngakano       ) IV, one is
Cl. 4 sing.     n   shi     e       shino   = shieshino     ) elided.
      plur.     n   bi      e       bino    = mbiebino      )
Cl. 1 sing.     n   ku      o       (w)uno  = ngwowuno      )
                                etc.

There it is: ni - PC - "that"
Cl. 1 plur.     n   ba      abo     = mbabo
Cl. 2 sing.     n   ku      okwo    = ngwokwo
      plur.     n   chi     echio   = njiechio
Cl. 1 sing.     n   ku      oyo     = ngwoyo
                                etc.

There it is yonder: ni - PC - IV - "that yonder"
Cl. 1 plur.     n   ba      a       balia   = mbabalia
Cl. 2 sing.     n   ku      o       kulia   = ngwokulia
      plur.     n   chi     e       chilia  = njiechilia
Cl. 1 sing.     n   ku      o       (w)ulia = ngwowulia
                                etc.

Keya ali heena? Ngwoyo.                 Where is Keya? There she is.
Tsing'ombe nzietsilia.                  There are the cows over there.
Abasoomesia mbabano.                    Here are the teachers.
Mbabo.                                  There they are.
Likhuwa ndielino.                       This is the matter.

-----oo0oo-----

"SELF" and "ALONE"

Many people seem to forget to differentiate between the English "I did it myself" and "I did it by myself". The following examples should make the difference clear:

Ndakhola omwene.                I did it myself.
Ndakhola sienyene.              I did it by myself (i.e. alone).
Yeetsile omwene.                He came himself (and didn't send somebody else instead).
Yeetsile yenyene.               He came by himself (i.e. alone).
Khwakhola abeene fwenyene.      We did it ouselves, and without anyone else's help.

-----oo0oo-----

LIKE

This is usually SHINGA (or SHINGANA, or sometimes NGA) for nouns in all classes, but it can be treated as a verb, "to be like", with the appropriate subjectival concord. In the SHINGA form it is frequently preceded by the verb FWANA = to resemble, look like, appear to be.

Afwana shinga iwe.                      She is like you.
* Ali shinga omukholi weefwe.           He is like a servant to us.
Afwana shinga ouli okhufwa.             He looks like a person who is going to die.
Beenga abeenya okhutsia.                They look as if they want to go.
Yeengana omwana weefwe.                 (He is like our brother (in appearance)
                                        (He is like a brother to us.

* or, He resembles our servant (in appearance); the former meaning could also be expressed more accurately, ALI SHINGA OMUKHOLI KHWIFWE.

There is an idiomatic use of SHINGA followed by OLWA (which by itself means "when"); together they may be the equivalent of the English conjunction "that", or "how", as in the following:

Yamboolela shinga olwa yeenya okhutsia. He told me that he wants to go.
Yamboolela shinga olwa akhoonyanga nyina. He told me how he helps his mother.
Manyia shinga olwa okholanga.           Show me how you do it; or Show me what you do.
Endutsi shinga omwana oyo olwa yeetsa okhuba omutofu. I can see that that child is going to be lazy.

-----oo0oo-----

Exercise 83 (p.112)
1. Now clouds have increased in the sky; no stars can be seen.
2. If it is possible we will finish up this work tomorrow.
3. I looked everywhere (I searched thoroughly) for the fowls, but they simply weren't there.
4. My tooth throbbed all night.
5. He lords it over his companions, and they respect him / are afraid of him.
6. Ripe bananas are peeled by hand, but unripe ones are peeled with a knife.
7. That child has become completely stupid.
8. All that was arranged long ago; and now it is spoilt / has gone wrong.
9. They help each other by carrying each other's loads.
10. Clean this room for me; it is very dirty.
11. Their grandfather was a Marama man; but they have been absorbed into the Luo (lit. swallowed up).
12. That work is hard; I don't think it can be done.
13. He had no field, so he was taken as a tenant on someone else's land.
14. If you had unfastened the door you would have got out.
15. That is unbelievable; you are accusing him falsely.
16. He took the cows out and brought them back, passing / going through our village.
17. He was lying on his back with his leg raised.
18. He is very poor; there is noone to help him; and he is an old man.
19. They hid the things, burying them in a field; and then someone dug them up.
20. He was caught stealing.

-----oo0oo-----

Exercise 85 (p.113)
1. Okhoyile okhwitsa okhukhukoonya / okhukoonye.
2. Akhoyile okhuteema okhukhola emilimo omwene.
3. Kakhoyeele mbu omuhandiichile ibarwa.
4. Okhuula olwa weya / Okhuula weye / Khatimba weye eshisika shino nikhurashialia ebiliibwa biomutsuli ta.
5. Okhuula olwa balinda tsingubo tsiabwe; nibaratsilinda ta balakuumwa / beetsa okhukuumwa.
6. Khukhoyile okhulaama / okhusaba nikhurashiachaka emilimo chieefwe ta.
7. Abasiani bakhoyile okhurunga tsishilingi tsinyinji okhushila abakhaana.
8. Buli mundu akhoyile okhukula ebiliibwabie (omwene).
9. Bachaka okhulima iwe norashiabuukha ta; bakhoyile okhulekha khale./ kakhoyeele mbu baleshe khale.
10. Okhuula onywe omusaala kuno.
11. Bakhoyile okhuchaka khale, nebutswa balashinda.
12. Kakhoyeele mbu obiishe amapesako okhukula / okule indika.

Exercise 87 (p.115)
1. Nishiina eshichila naraliho ta / nabulaho? Shichila shiyalangwa ta; utsiile haundi.
2. Nishiina eshichila nosinyiishe? Shichila omukhaana oyo yakosia eshitabo shianje.
3. Nishiina eshiachila / eshichilile nibatsia ingo / nibeenyokha? Shichila bali / babeele nibachonyile.
4. Amatsi amabii niko akachila nalwala (this could also be "akachila yalwala").
5. Nisie owachila nibaramanya ta.
6. Oyo niye khootsa; niye owachila nisooma / ndasooma.
7. Embulile amakhuwa ako mungoloobe; niko akachila / akachilile nisangala / nindi omusangafu.
8. Nishiina eshichila niwenya mbu isie ekhuboolelekhwo? Shichila niwe owalola nikeekhola.
9. Oburambibwe nibwo obwachila naranyala okhwibisa bwangu ta.
10. Ifula niyo eyachila nikhureetsa ta.
11. Niwe owachila nihona / ndahona mubulwale bwanje.
12. Omutoka kwoonoonekha; niko akachila (if you are thinking of the reason) / nikwo okwachila (if you are thinking of the car) nibashinda.
13. Ebiliibwa bihweele pe; niko akachila nikhuli inzala.
14. Amatsi akaboraame niko akachilanga abandu abanji nibalwala.

Exercise 83 [p.118]
1. Nendeeshe inyama endie?
2. Ochiteeshe orio.
3. Omundu (Omusaatsa) oyo yakhanyala arie okhukhoonya?
4. Amanyile arie mbu ifula yiitsa okhukwa?
5. Yali nachendanga / Yachendanga ario nakhoonya abandu.
6. Yasunga / Yareeba mbu, "Enyala endie okhunyoola amapeesa akahela okhuumbakha inzu?"
7. Omanyile orie mbu nomulwale/alwala?
8. Noba nochinga ebitabo orio shiolabikwisia ta.
9. Ebikombe bianje bili heena? Mbiebino.
10. Inzu yanje njieyilia.
11. Yoombakha inzuye yenyene / omwene yenyene.
12. Shikhularuma omundu ta; khulatsia abeene.
13. Inyanga yene njieyino, nawe noshilwala.
14. Inzu yene yili habwene, ne eshilama neshibii.
15. Umboolele shinga Keya olwa yeeka okhusooma.
16. Ebitabo ebia wenya engule bili heena? Mbiebino.
17. Omundu owa inzuye yayia ngwowuno.
18. Omukhaana owa ndakhuboolelakhwo ngwoyo.
19. Omukunda kweefwe ngwokulia.
20. Tsindakhuboolelakhwo ngwoyo.
21. Mama ngwoyo.
22. Imbwa yangubo etsia yambeelesia nzietsino.
23. Omwalo ngwokwo, khandi amaaro ngako.
24. Akhanje njieyilia.
25. Obunyasi ana akhalililenje / akhabeele nikhalilanga khakhano.
26. Abaana bali heena? Mbabalia.
27. Emianda echia ndaboolelwakhwo chili heena? Njiechilia.
28. Ebiliibwa mbiebino; oliekhwo.
29. Isaa yanje yili heena? Njieyino.
30. Litaala lieenywe lili heena? Ndielo.
31. Omubano ngwokuno; ekhalache omukoye.
32. Balola mbu shianyala okhuchinga* omusiiko okwo ta. (* or, ...shinga olwa aranyala okhuchinga ...)
33. Ndamumanyia shinga olwa khubohanga tsing'ombe.
34. Ndamunyoola niyeekhaale munzu yenyene.
35. Ifwe khwalima omukunda abeene, ne oundi yaraaka amatuma.
36. Omusiani weefwe ngwowuno; olutsi / opara mbu afwanana nende / afwana shinga papa noho?
37. Abasiani abo beengana abaana banje.
38. Yeenga owenya okhukhuboolela likhuwa.
39. Tsing'ombe nzietsilia; tsienya / tsiinga etsienya okhwitsa hano.
40. Ingofia yo njieyino, khandi ebilarobio mbiebilia.

| Rel. Concord | akha- | oru- | obu- | okhu- | aha- | (o)mu- | oku- | echi- |
| Poss. Particle | kha- | rwa- | bwa- | khwa- | ha- | mwa- | kwa- | chia- |
| Absolute Pronoun | kho | rwo | bwo | khwo | ho | mwo | kwo | chio |
| Poss.: my | khanje | rwanje | bwanje | khwanje | hanje | mwanje | kwanje | chianje |
| Poss.: mine | akhanje | orwanje | obwanje | okhwanje | ahanje | omwanje | okwanje | echianje |
| Poss.: your, yours | -kho, khokho | -rwo, rurwo | -bwo, bubwo | -khwo, khukhwo | -ho, ahoho | -mwo, mumwo | -kwo, kukwo | -chio, chichio |
| Poss.: his, hers, its | -khe, khekhe | -rwe, rurwe | -bwe, bubwe | -khwe, khukhwe | -he, ahehe | -mwe, mumwe | -kwe, kukwe | -chie, chichie |
| Poss.: our, ours | kheefu/fwe | rweefu/fwe | bweefu/fwe | khweefu/fwe | heefu/fwe | mweefu/fwe | kweefu/fwe | chieefu/fwe |
| Poss.: your/s | kheenyu/we | rweenyu/we | bweenyu/we | khweenyu/we | heenyu/nywe | mweenyu/nywe | kweenyu/nywe | chieenyu/nywe |
| Poss.: their/s | khabo/bwe | rwabo/bwe | bwabo/bwe | khwabo/bwe | habo/bwe | mwabo/bwe | kwabo/bwe | chiabo/bwe |
| here it is | khakhano | ndworuno | mbwobuno | khwokhuno | mbahano | mwomuno | ngwokuno | njiechino |
| there it is | khakho | ndworwo | mbwobwo/bu | khwokhwo | mbaho | mwomu/mwo | ngwokwo | njiechio |
| there it is yonder | khakhalia | ndworulia | mbwobulia | khwokhulia | mbahalia | mwomulia | ngwokulia | njiechilia |
| self, selves | (a)kheene | (o)rwene | (o)bwene | (o)khwene | (a)heene | mwene | kwene | chiene |
| alone | khonyene | rwonyene | bwonyene | khwonyene | honyene | mwonyene | kwonyene | chionyene |
| any, all, also | khosi | rwosi | bwosi | khosi | hosi | mwosi | kwosi | chiosi |
| other, certain | (a)khandi | (o)rundi | (o)bundi | (o)khundi | (a)handi | mundi | (o)kundi | (e)chindi |
| how many? | - | rwinga? | bwinga? | khwinga? | hanga | mwinga? | - | chinga? |
| like* | kheenga | rwinga | bwinga | khwinga | heenga | mwinga | kwinga | chinga |

(* Note: but usually SHINGA for all classes)


--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PART II: COMPREHENSIVE CLASS-BY-CLASS CONCORD SYSTEM
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

CLASS 1: PERSONAL CLASS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Singular (Bleek Class 1):
  Noun Prefix: omu-
  Example Noun: omundu (person)
  Adjective Concord: omu-
  many, much: [not applicable to singular]
  Pronominal Concord: u-, o-
  Demonstrative (this): uno
  Demonstrative (that): oyo
  Demonstrative (that yonder): ulia
  Subjectival Concord:
    1st sing.: en-, enz-
    2nd sing.: o-, w-
    3rd sing.: a-, ya-
  How (do I)?: endie?
  Thus: endio
  Objectival Concord:
    1st sing.: -n-, -ya-
    2nd sing.: -khu-
    3rd sing.: -mu-
  Relative Concord: ou-
  Possessive Particle: wa-
  Absolute Pronoun:
    1st sing.: isie, -nje
    2nd sing.: iwe, -u-
    3rd sing.: ye, -e
  Possessive:
    my: wanje
    mine: owanje
    your, yours: -wo, wuwo
    his, hers, its: -we, wuwe
    our, ours: weefu, weefwe
    your/s: weenyu/nywe
    their/s: wabo, wabwe
  "Here it is": ngwowuno
  "There it is": ngwoyo
  "There it is yonder": ngwowulia
  Self: omwene
  Alone:
    1st sing.: sienyene
    2nd sing.: wenyene
    3rd sing.: yenyene
  Any, all, also:
    1st sing.: siesi
    2nd sing.: wesi
    3rd sing.: yesi
  Other, certain: (o)undi
  How many?: [not applicable]
  Like: yeenga (or usually SHINGA)

Plural (Bleek Class 2):
  Noun Prefix: aba-
  Example Noun: abandu (people)
  Adjective Concord: aba-
  many, much: abanji
  Pronominal Concord: ba-
  Demonstrative (these): bano
  Demonstrative (those): abo
  Demonstrative (those yonder): balia
  Subjectival Concord:
    1st plur.: khu-
    2nd plur.: mu-
    3rd plur.: ba-
  How (do we/they)?: khurie?
  Thus: khurio
  Objectival Concord:
    1st plur.: -khu-
    2nd plur.: -mu-
    3rd plur.: -ba-
  Relative Concord: aba-
  Possessive Particle: ba-
  Absolute Pronoun:
    1st plur.: ifwe
    2nd plur.: inywe
    3rd plur.: bo, -bwe
  Possessive:
    my: banje
    mine: abanje
    your, yours: -bo, bobo
    his, hers, its: -be, bebe
    our, ours: beefu/fwe
    your/s: beenyu/ywe
    their/s: babo/bwe
  "Here they are": mbabano
  "There they are": mbabo
  "There they are yonder": mbabalia
  Selves: abeene
  Alone:
    1st plur.: fwenyene
    2nd plur.: mwenyene
    3rd plur.: bonyene
  Any, all, also:
    1st plur.: fwesi
    2nd plur.: mwesi
    3rd plur.: bosi
  Other, certain: (a)bandi
  How many?: banga? benga?
  Like: beenga (or usually SHINGA)


CLASS 2: TREES, PLANTS, ETC.
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

  Noun Prefix: omu-
  Example Noun: omusaala (tree)

  many, much: omunji
  Pronominal Concord: ku-
  Demonstrative (this): kuno
  Demonstrative (that): okwo
  Demonstrative (that yonder): kulia
  Subjectival Concord: ku-
  How?: kurie?
  Thus: kurio
  Objectival Concord: -ku-
  Relative Concord: oku-
  Possessive Particle: kwa-
  Absolute Pronoun: kwo
  Possessive:
    my: kwanje
    mine: okwanje
    your, yours: -kwo, kukwo
    his, hers, its: -kwe, kukwe
    our, ours: kweefu/fwe
    your/s: kweenyu/we
    their/s: kwabo/bwe
  "Here it is": ngwokuno
  "There it is": ngwokwo
  "There it is yonder": ngwokulia
  Self: (o)kwene
  Alone: kwonyene
  Any, all, also: kwosi
  Other, certain: (o)kundi
  How many?: [not applicable]
  Like: kwinga (or usually SHINGA)

  Example Noun: emisaala (trees)
  Adjective Concord: emi-
  many, much: eminji
  Pronominal Concord: chi-
  Demonstrative (these): chino
  Demonstrative (those): echio
  Demonstrative (those yonder): chilia
  Subjectival Concord: chi-
  How?: chirie?
  Thus: chirio
  Objectival Concord: -chi-
  Relative Concord: echi-
  Possessive Particle: chia-
  Absolute Pronoun: chio
  Possessive:
    my: chianje
    mine: echianje
    your, yours: -chio, chichio
    his, hers, its: -chie, chichie
    our, ours: chieefu/fwe
    your/s: chi'nyu/we
    their/s: chiabo/bwe
  "Here they are": njiechino
  "There they are": njiechio
  "There they are yonder": njiechilia
  Selves: (e)chiene
  Alone: chionyene
  Any, all, also: chiosi
  Other, certain: (e)chindi
  How many?: chinga?
  Like: chinga (or usually SHINGA)

CLASS 12: MONSTROUS / AUGMENTATIVE NOUNS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
